### **Preface** 

Army Techniques Publication (ATP) 4-02.10 describes hospitalization, as one of the ten medical functions, as well as tactics, techniques, and procedures to plan and employ a Role 3 Army hospital and hospital augmentation organizations within an area of operations. This publication discusses the medical function and the essential care typically associated with Role 3 hospitalization. This publication was also developed to address hospitalization across the range of military operations, including large-scale combat operations as described in Field Manual (FM) 3-0. 

The principal audience for ATP 4-02.10 is commanders, staffs, surgeons and surgeon sections at echelon, Army Health System (AHS) planners, and Army Medical Department personnel and units. 

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable United States, international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels ensure that their Soldiers operate in accordance with the law of armed conflict and the rules of engagement (refer to Field Manual [FM] 6-27). 

This publication implements or is in consonance with the following North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) International Standardization Agreements (STANAG) and American, British, Canadian, Australian, and New Zealand (ABCANZ) Armies’ standards or publications:

**Preface** 

The use of the term Role 3 throughout this publication is used as a generalized term. When Role 3 hospitalization, Role 3 medical center, Role 3 field hospital, or Role 3 medical treatment facility is used, it is used in context for that specific type of hospital unit or to match verbiage in the paragraph. 

This publication supersedes ATP 4-02.10 dated 14 August 2020 and rescinds ATP 4-02.25 dated 7 December 2020. Throughout this publication, as appropriate, reference is made to the major policy guidance affecting each specific topic. These references should not be considered as the only policy guidance available. ### **Introduction** 

Observations from the conflict in Eastern Europe, lessons learned from Afghanistan and Iraq, analysis of the operational medicine transformational goals, and the Army's adoption of the multidomain operations operational concept were integrated into this publication. 

ATP 4-02.10 consists of four chapters: 

- **Chapter 1** presents commanders and staff with a discussion about hospitalization as a medical function and what Role 3 is within the Army Health System. It describes the linkage between the Army Health System principles and the Army tenets of operations and imperatives and describes the essential care provided by operational medical Role 3 hospitals employed in an area of operations. This includes eligibility of care, and law of armed conflict. 

- **Chapter 2** provides information on the hospitalization organization’s missions, assignment and dependencies, capabilities and functions, employment, basis of allocation, and mobility. 

- **Chapter 3** provides commanders and staff with operational considerations, such as employment of hospitalization units, eligibility of care, Geneva Conventions and medical ethics, command and support relationships, patient movement, hospital medical supply and logistics operations, nutrition care operations, hospitalization support to specific operations, and operational environments. 

- **Chapter 4** discusses specialized surgical and medical augmentation detachments and teams’ missions, assignment and dependencies, capabilities and functions, employment, basis of allocation, and mobility in their support of the Role 3 hospital and Role 2 medical company. 

- **Appendix A** outlines potential hospitalization activities that align with the Army's strategic contexts of competition below armed conflict, crisis, and armed conflict. 

- **Appendix B** outlines the key factors and data needed for hospital planning. 

- **Appendix C** is a guide for Geneva Conventions and medical ethics compliance. 

- **Appendix D** provides guidelines for environment waste management. 

- **Appendix E** provides guideline and regulatory requirements for safety management. 

- **Appendix F** outlines the training opportunities available to hospitalization units and specialized surgical and medical augmentation units. 

- **Appendix G** provides an overview of the medical information and communication systems. 

- **Appendix H** provides a brief description of telemedicine in the Role 3 hospital. 

The United States Army Medical Center of Excellence is reorganizing the placement of terms and definitions found in proponent publications within the Army Medicine’s 4-02 series doctrine manuals. It was determined that some of the terms are best suited in other publications within this series.

##### **Chapter 1** 

### **Hospitalization Overview** 

Army hospitalization is the Army’s contribution to the joint theater hospitalization medical function. The term hospitalization is used to describe that portion of health care delivery provided at Role 3 Army hospitals for all classes of patients whose conditions cannot be managed on an outpatient or patient holding status. This chapter describes the hospitalization medical function and the operational capabilities available at the Role 3 hospital. Role 4 hospitalization is a part of the military health system and is found in continental United States (CONUS)-based medical treatment facilities (MTFs) and other safe havens (to include robust overseas MTFs and normally located outside of the area of operations. As the highest role of care, the Role 4 hospital provides definitive care. 

##### **SECTION I – HOSPITALIZATION AS A MEDICAL FUNCTION** 

1-1. The _Army Health System_ is a component of the Military Health System that is responsible for operational management of the health service support and force health protection missions for training, predeployment, deployment, and postdeployment operations. The Army Health System (AHS) includes all mission support services performed, provided, or arranged by the Army Medicine to support health service support and force health protection mission requirements for the Army and as directed, for joint, intergovernmental agencies, Army Forces, and multinational forces (FM 4-02). 

#### **INTRODUCTION TO HOSPITALIZATION** 

1-2. Hospitalization is one of the ten medical functions and is part of the AHS mission for management of wounded, ill, and injured (WII) personnel in an operational environment (OE). Hospitalization occurs primarily under the health service support (HSS) mission component of the AHS which is aligned with the sustainment warfighting function. While some medical support within a hospital falls into both HSS and force health protection (FHP) (aligned with the protection warfighting function), they are typically associated with the medical treatment of patients defined by HSS. For more information on FHP, HSS, and the other medical functions, refer to Field Manual (FM) 4-02. 

1-3. A _medical treatment facility_ (Joint) is a facility established for the purpose of furnishing medical and/or dental care to eligible individuals (Joint Publication [JP] 4-02); (Army) is any facility established for the purpose of providing medical treatment. This includes battalion aid stations, Role 2 facilities, dispensaries, clinics, and hospitals (FM 4-02). **A_hospital_ is a medical treatment facility capable of providing inpatient care.** A hospital is further described as appropriately staffed and equipped to provide diagnostic and therapeutic services, as well as the necessary supporting services required to perform its assigned mission, functions, and primary tasks and purposes. In addition, a hospital may discharge the functions of a clinic. Often, the terms hospital and MTF are misused interchangeably. While a Role 3 MTF provides hospitalization and is therefore a hospital, Role 1 and 2 MTFs do not provide all the capabilities and robust medical functions included in hospitalization. Thus, Role 1 and 2 MTFs are not hospitals. 

**_Note._** Care should be taken when using the terms casualty and patient. A _casualty_ is any person who is lost to the organization by having been declared dead, duty status-whereabouts unknown, excused absence-whereabouts unknown, missing, ill, or injured (JP 4-02). **A_patient_ is a wounded, ill and injured Soldier who receives medical care or treatment from medically trained personnel.** A casualty has transitioned into a patient the moment a combat medic specialist or medical provider renders treatment, and the patient enters the continuum of care. 

1-4. The Role 3 hospital provides essential care within an area of operations (AO) to treat and return to duty (RTD) those patients who can be treated within the theater evacuation policy or to stabilize patients that require evacuation out of theater for further definitive, convalescent, or rehabilitative care. _Essential care_ is the absolutely necessary initial, en route, resuscitative, and surgical care provided to save, stabilize, and return as many Soldiers to duty as quickly as possible (FM 4-02). It includes first responder care, initial resuscitation, and stabilization as well as treatment and hospitalization. Forward care may include stabilizing surgery to ensure the patient can tolerate further evacuation as well as en route care during evacuation. The objective is to either have the patient RTD within the theater evacuation policy or to begin initial treatment required for optimization of outcome. _Return to duty_ is a patient disposition which, after medical evaluation and treatment, when necessary, returns a Soldier for duty in their unit (FM 4-02).The _theater evacuation policy_ is a command decision indicating the length in days of the maximum period of non-effectiveness that patients may be held within the command for treatment and the medical determination of patients that cannot return to duty status within the period prescribed requiring evacuation by the first available means, provided the travel involved will not aggravate their disabilities or medical condition. (ATP 4-02.2).

1-5. The hospitalization medical function has several capabilities which consists of triage, emergency medical treatment, pre-operative care, surgical and anesthesia services (general, thoracic, orthopedic, obstetrics-gynecological, urological, and oral maxillofacial), inpatient care (nursing services, intensive care unit, and intermediate care ward), outpatient services (behavioral health services, physical therapy, and operational dental care), pharmacy services, clinical laboratory services (including blood banking and microbiology), radiology services (including computed tomography), nutrition care services, medical logistics (MEDLOG), patient administration services, laundry and shower services, medical consultation services, and religious support and pastoral care. 

1-6. The Role 3 hospital center can be augmented by one or more specialty teams and detachments, further increasing the capabilities and capacities of the hospital. See Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 of this publication for further information. 

###### **The paragraphs below implement NATO STANAG 2598 and ABCANZ standards 2148 and 2152.** 

#### **DAMAGE CONTROL RESUSCITATION AND DAMAGE CONTROL SURGERY** 

1-7. **_Damage control resuscitation_ is the strategy to limit blood loss and prevent coagulopathy by combining hypotensive resuscitation, early airway control, and early and balanced use of blood products and other hemostatic agents. Also known as DCR** . **_Damage control surgery_ is surgical intervention (rapid initial control of bleeding and contamination with packing and temporary closure) to keep the patient alive, targeted at prevention of the triad of death (hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy), rather than correction of anatomy. Also known as DCS** . 

1-8. DCR and DCS happen at different stages in the military health system, depending on the severity of the injury and the resources available. DCR is used to limit blood loss and prevent dangerous conditions like coagulopathy by managing blood pressure, controlling the airway, and using blood products early. This process can start as early as Role 1, which is the immediate care given at the point of injury or at a battalion aid station. It continues at Role 2, where more advanced medical teams can provide better resuscitation. At Role 3, which is a larger hospital in the combat zone, DCR is fully available with more equipment and medical staff. 

1-9. DCS, on the other hand, is focused on quickly stopping bleeding and controlling contamination through surgery. The goal is to prevent the “triad of death,” which includes hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy. This type of surgery is usually performed at Role 2 if there are surgical teams present, but it is most done at Role 3, where larger hospitals and surgical facilities are set up to handle more severe injuries. 

1-10. In short, DCR can happen at all three early roles of care, while DCS typically occurs at Role 2 and Role 3, where surgical capabilities are available. 

##### **SECTION II – ROLE 3 MEDICAL CARE** 

1-11. At Role 3, the patient is treated in an MTF staffed and equipped to provide essential care to all categories of patients, to include damage control resuscitation (DCR), initial wound surgery, damage control surgery (DCS), and postoperative treatment for up to 30 days (the number of inpatient days may fluctuate due to proximity to the forward edge of the battle area and the OE or in accordance with the theater evacuation policy. This role of care expands the advanced trauma management and emergency medical treatment (EMT) provided at Role 2. For additional information on hospitalization in support of the Army’s strategic contexts, see Appendix A. 

1-12. Patients who are unable to tolerate and survive movement over long distances receive surgical care in a forward resuscitative and surgical detachment (FRSD) or Role 3 hospital as close to the supported unit as the tactical situation allows. This may be followed by inpatient care in accordance with the theater evacuation policy. Figure 1-1 on page 4 depicts the roles of care and where the Role 3 fits into the continuum of care. _Roles of medical_ care is the characterization of health support for the distribution of medical resources and capabilities (JP 4-02). Role 3 hospitalization includes provisions for— 

- Coordination of patient evacuation through medical regulating. 

- Providing essential care for all categories of patients in an MTF with the proper staff and equipment. 

- Providing medical treatment and hospitalization support on an area basis to units without organic medical assets.

1-13. Under the hospital center construct there are five separate units, with associated Tables of Organization and Equipment (TOE) that make up the hospital center. These units are discussed in Chapter 2. 

1-14. When patients are transferred from Role 3 by intertheater medical evacuation (MEDEVAC), they are received at a Role 4 MTF located in CONUS-based hospitals and other safe havens (to include robust overseas Role 4 MTF). If mobilization requires expansion of military hospital capacities, then the Department of Veterans Affairs and civilian hospital beds in the National Disaster Medical System (NDMS) are added to meet the increased demands created by the evacuation of patients from the operational area. These installation-based hospitals represent the most definitive medical care available within the military health system. The description that best defines a ‘Role 4 MTF’ is an MTF that provides the full range of definitive care that includes preventative, curative, acute, convalescent, restorative, rehabilitative, and surgical care. 

##### **SECTION III – HOSPITALIZATION PRIMARY TASKS AND PURPOSES** 

1-15. Using a restrictive definition for hospitalization is challenging. It is more effective to conceptualize hospitalization by focusing on its functions. At a Role 3 MTF, there are several tasks that either cannot be performed at a Role 1 or 2 MTF or are carried out with greater capability and capacity. The functions described below are not an exhaustive list, as there are additional capabilities provided by specialty augmentation teams that may supplement the hospital’s tasks. For a more detailed description of these functions and the hospital sections responsible for them, see Chapter 2 through Chapter 4. Table 1-1 depicts the hospitalization primary tasks and purposes. 

**Table 1-1. Hospitalization primary tasks and purposes**

1-16. Hospitalization includes highly robust services that encompass emergent care; enhanced medical, surgical, and ancillary capabilities; and primary inpatient and outpatient care. **_Inpatient_ refers to a person admitted to and treated within a Role 3 hospital and who cannot be returned to duty within the same calendar day** . An **_outpatient_ is a person receiving medical/dental examination and/or treatment from medical personnel and in a status other than being admitted to a hospital.** Included in this category is the person who is treated and retained (held) in a MTF other than a hospital, such as a Role 2 facility. Patient holding at a Role 2 medical company does not equal inpatient status or hospitalization. 

1-17. Planning for hospitalization is crucial. For additional hospitalization planning factors, see Appendix B. 

1-18. The Role 3 MTF has certain protections under the Geneva and Hague Conventions. Medical ethics play a role in hospitalization as well. For additional information on the medical aspects of the law of armed conflict and medical ethics, see Appendix C. 

1-19. Role 1 and 2 MTFs maintain a limited supply of medications. A Role 3 MTF operates a fully functioning pharmacy and exercises appropriate control, accountability, environmental management, and distribution of medications and controlled substances. 

1-20. Role 3 MTFs provide clinical laboratory services, which includes basic microbiology screening and blood banking. Role 3 hospital laboratories have a much more robust capability and capacity than what is provided by a Role 2 MTF. 

1-21. Radiology services provided by the Role 3 MTF are more capable than that of a Role 2 MTF. The Role 3 MTF has the added capability of providing computed tomography (CT) services. 

1-22. Medical logistics occurs at all roles of care. At Roles 1 and 2 MTFs, MEDLOG is focused on maintaining required supplies and ensuring operator level medical device services are conducted. However, a Role 3 MTF has a robust MEDLOG capability, and most notably, can conduct much of its own medical maintenance. See Chapter 3 for a discussion on MEDLOG operations within a hospital. 

1-23. A Role 3 MTF is the first place in the continuum of care where nutritional expertise and support is provided by specifically trained nutrition care personnel. See Chapter 3 for a discussion on nutrition care operations in a hospital. 

1-24. Training to fulfill all the responsibilities of a Role 3 MTF is challenging. For a list of hospitalization training opportunities, see Appendix F. 

1-25. Informal patient administration is conducted at Role 1 and 2 MTFs which is essentially patient accountability and disposition of treatment records. Role 3 MTF is the first point in the continuum of care where formal medical regulating is executed. It entails the admission and disposition of patients, maintaining patient records, security of patient valuables, statistical reporting, patient privacy policies, and coordination for patient evacuation rearward or out of theater. See Chapter 3 for a discussion of patient administration operations in a hospital. For additional information on information systems, see Appendix G. 

1-26. Respiratory care performed at a Role 3 MTF is much more than simply administering oxygen. Hospitalization includes respiratory therapy by specifically trained technicians and the ability to provide support for patients that require supplementation of oxygen, administration of aerosolized medicines, general care of the patient with ventilatory compromise, and mechanical respiratory assistance in an intensive care unit (ICU). 

1-27. The Role 3 MTF has many medical capabilities that can be shared outside the confines of the facility. Telemedicine is a way to extend the knowledge of the Role 3 MTF capabilities. For more information on of hospitalization in telemedicine, see Appendix H. 

1-28. Waste and safety are key components of a Role 3 MTF. Ensuring medical waste is properly disposed of and ensuring safety are primary focuses of hospitalization. For more information of waste, see Appendix D and for hospital safety, see Appendix E. 

1-29. DCR can and does take place by medical units prior to a patient reaching a Role 3 MTF. DCS and forward resuscitative surgery can also be accomplished at a Role 2 MTF if augmented by a FRSD. **_Forward resuscitative surgery_ is urgent initial surgery required to render a patient transportable for further evacuation to an inpatient medical treatment facility staffed and equipped to provide for the patient’s care** . Roles of care during large-scale combat operations should not be skipped as this will quickly overwhelm Role 3 MTFs within the AO. However, mission, enemy, terrain and weather, troops and support available, time available, civil considerations, and (informational considerations) (METT-TC [I]), also known as mission variables, and the theater evacuation policy may dictate when WII patients can be evacuated from near the point of injury (POI) directly to a Role 3 MTF. _Point of injury_ is the location a wounding, illness, or injury was sustained. (FM 4-02). 

1-30. Hemorrhage is the leading cause of preventable death on the battlefield. DCR was developed to work synergistically with DCS and prioritizes non-surgical interventions, which may reduce morbidity and mortality from trauma and hemorrhage. The major principle of DCR is to restore homeostasis, prevent or mitigate the development of tissue hypoxia, oxygen debt, and burden of shock as well as coagulopathy. This includes the best evidence-based practices for addressing life-threatening injuries through aggressive hemorrhage control and early blood transfusion near the POI. 

1-31. The FRSD and Role 3 MTF DCS are described as the rapid termination of an operation after control of life-threatening bleeding and contamination followed by correction of physiologic abnormalities allowing the patient to be stabilized for further evacuation. This strategy involves a staged approach to multiple injured patients designed to avoid or correct the lethal triad of hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy before definitive management of injuries. It is applicable to a wide variety of disciplines. During the first stage of DCS, hemorrhage is stopped, and contamination is controlled using the simplest and most rapid means available. Temporary wound closure methods are employed (patient dependent). The second stage is characterized by correction of physiologic abnormalities in the post-operative recovery area. Patients are warmed and resuscitated, and coagulation defects are corrected. Once patients are stabilized at the FRSD, they are evacuated rearward to the Role 3 MTF. For patients receiving DCS at the Role 3 MTF, once stabilized they can: remain at Role 3 MTF for essential care; be evacuated rearward to be closer to a strategic evacuation node; or evacuated out of theater to a Role 4 MTF where the final phase of DCS is completed, the definitive operative management of the stable patient.

##### **SECTION IV – HOSPITALIZATION SUPPORT TO ARMY OPERATIONS** 

1-32. The Army’s primary mission is to organize, train, and equip its forces to conduct prompt and sustained land combat to defeat enemy ground forces and seize, occupy, and defend land areas. It supports four strategic roles for the joint force. Army forces shape OEs, counter aggression during crisis, prevail during large-scale ground combat, and consolidate gains. The Army accomplishes its strategic roles by providing forces for joint campaigns that enable integrated deterrence of adversaries outside of conflict and the defeat of enemies during conflict or war. 

1-33. The hospitalization medical function supports the Army in achieving its primary mission by providing hospitalization at the theater army, corps, and division echelons. The synchronization and coordination of Army hospitalization, as well as its integration with joint services, is essential to conserving the fighting strength by returning as many Soldiers to duty as far forward as possible. 

1-34. _Multidomain operations_ are the combined arms employment of joint and Army capabilities to create and exploit relative advantages to achieve objectives, defeat enemy forces, and consolidate gains on behalf of joint force commanders (FM 3-0). Multidomain operations is the Army’s operational concept. It spans the competition continuum and are the Army’s contribution to joint campaigns. The Army hospitalization medical function enables multidomain operations through the provision of hospitalization, surgical, medical, and minimal care capabilities that support maneuver forces at echelon. For additional information on the strategic context, tenets, and imperatives, refer to FM 3-0. Figure 1-2 on page 9 depicts the medical functions in support of multidomain operations and where hospitalization is located within the land domain. 

**Figure 1-2. Medical functions in support of multidomain operations** 

#### **HOSPITALIZATION SUPPORT TO THE ARMY STRATEGIC CONTEXTS** 

1-35. Army forces support national objectives across a wide range of military operations from security cooperation through crisis response to large-scale combat operations. Theater armies’ campaign across the competition continuum however, Army tactical units are normally engaged in one strategic context at a time. Therefore, Army doctrine describes the strategic situation through three contexts in which Army forces conduct operations: 

- Competition below armed conflict (referred to as competition). 

- Crisis. 

- Armed conflict. 

1-36. Hospitalization capabilities are employed throughout the competition continuum with the primary difference being the scale of support and the health and enemy threats. Figure 1-3 on page 10 provides an illustration of the Army strategic contexts and operational categories. Hospitalization capabilities may be employed to support crisis in one operational area and armed conflict in another, within the same area of responsibility. For more information on hospitalization in support of the strategic contexts, see Appendix A. 

**Figure 1-3. The Army strategic contexts and operational categories** 

##### **COMPETITION BELOW ARMED CONFLICT** 

1-37. Competition exists when two or more state or non-state adversaries have incompatible interests, but neither seeks armed conflict. Nation-states compete using all instruments of national power to gain and maintain advantages that help them achieve their goals. Low levels of lethal force can be part of competition below armed conflict. Competition provides military forces time to prepare for armed conflict, opportunities to assure allies and partners of resolve and commitment, and time and space to set the necessary conditions to prevent crisis or conflict. 

1-38. The hospitalization medical function supports operations during competition include military engagement, security cooperation, set the theater tasks, and the Army support to other Services. Hospitalization can be instrumental in building partnership and capacity. Due to the scarcity of hospitalization capabilities in most nations armed forces, Army hospitalization can be an invaluable tool. 

##### **CRISIS** 

1-39. A _crisis_ is an emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve national and/or strategic objectives (JP 3-0). The context of crisis is relative to an adversary, which is different from crisis response, which can result from a natural or human disaster. During crisis, armed conflict has not yet occurred, but it is either imminent or a distinct possibility that requires rapid response by forces prepared to fight if deterrence fails. 

1-40. Operational medicine planners should be prepared for a crisis resulting from an adversary or human and natural disasters. Medical planners and commanders should consider the possibility that military action may escalate a crisis towards armed conflict and allocate hospitalization assets to best support operations and the population at risk (PAR). 

##### **ARMED CONFLICT** 

1-41. Armed conflict occurs when a state or non-state actor uses lethal force as the primary means to satisfy its interests. Armed conflict can range from irregular warfare to conventional warfare and combinations of both. Army forces may enter armed conflict with little or advanced warning. 

1-42. The hospitalization medical function enables returning Soldiers to duty as far forward as possible and clearing the battlefield of patients to enable freedom of movement and maneuver. In armed conflict, hospitalization units may operate within the theater, corps, and division echelons with attached units such as the FRSD and elements of the medical detachment (prolonged care augmentation) (PCAD) being utilized within a maneuver brigade’s AO. 

#### **HOSPITALIZATION LINK TO THE ARMY TENETS OF OPERATIONS** 

1-43. Tenets of operations are desirable attributes that should be built into all plans and operations, and they are related to how multidomain operations should be conducted. Hospital leaders use the tenets of operations to inform and assess courses of action throughout the operations process. The tenets of operations are agility, convergence, endurance, and depth. 

##### **AGILITY** 

1-44. _Agility_ is the ability to move forces and adjust their dispositions and activities more rapidly than the enemy (FM 3-0). Agility is integral to survivability during large-scale combat operations. Agility is tied to the mobility AHS principle. 

1-45. Hospitalization units lack ability to rapidly displace to avoid enemy detection and action. Hospitalization units should train and master the ability to quickly complex (set up) the hospital to become fully mission capable and decomplex (tear down) tentage, shelters, and equipment to increase greater freedom of action and endurance. Large-scale combat operations require flexibility. Hospitalization units should train and gain proficiency in the ability to quickly change task, direction, or focus, and reorganize and reintegrate as the operational situation dictates. Changes may come due to a transition between operational phases or in response to enemy actions. Elements of a hospital center in support of a division may not complex the entire hospital, may be required to split the hospital, or be required to quickly relocate to maintain proximity to the supported maneuver force. 

##### **CONVERGENCE** 

1-46. _Convergence_ is an outcome created by the concerted employment of capabilities from multiple domains and echelons against combinations of decisive points in any domain to create effects against a system, formation, decision maker, or in a specific geographic area (FM 3-0). Convergence occurs when a higher echelon and its subordinate echelons create effects from and in multiple domains in ways that defeat or disrupt enemy forces long enough for friendly forces to effectively exploit the opportunity. 

1-47. The hospitalization medical function supports convergence by setting medical conditions that provide options during crisis and enable success at the outset of armed conflict. Army hospitalization provides Role 3 support while joint forces set the theater and forces continue to move and extend lines of communication. Senior medical leaders establish medical support agreements with allies and partners that facilitate situational understanding, decision making, and synchronization of AHS capabilities during armed conflict. Medical commanders should understand the concept of operations to anticipate hospitalization requirements on a dynamic battlefield where convergence creates exploitable opportunities that enable freedom of action and mission accomplishment. 

##### **ENDURANCE** 

1-48. _Endurance_ is the ability to persevere over time throughout the depth of an operational environment (FM 3-0). Endurance is key to sustaining operations for as long as necessary to achieve the desired outcome. 

1-49. Hospitalization units anticipate changing requirements and support the force for as long as necessary. Hospitalization units should train to complex and decomplex the hospital and know other Service and unified action partner (UAP) hospital capabilities whenever possible. This synchronized integration of hospitalization creates a redundancy of joint, host nation (HN) and UAP hospitalization assets all working together. Degraded communications should be anticipated, planned for, and analog systems should be included into tactical standard operating procedures (TACSOPs) as an alternate means of communication to enable units to maintain acceptable tempo for as long as necessary.

##### **DEPTH** 

1-50. _Depth_ is the extension of operations in time, space, or purpose to achieve definitive results (ADP 3-0). Commanders achieve depth by understanding enemy strengths and weaknesses and attacking them in simultaneous and sequential fashion. 

1-51. The hospitalization medical function supports the maneuver commander by synchronizing the integration of hospitalization into a layered depth approach rearward from the forward line of troops with less hospitalization assets forward and more going rearward to support the anticipated patient loads expected during large-scale combat operations. This sustains hospitalization over time and when enemy risk prevents or limits movement in one domain with a hospitalization plan that can sustain operations as they occur in depth. Hospitalization units create depth by establishing the hospitalization chain that includes joint forces, HN, and UAPs resulting in improved interoperability with multinational forces to the degree required by operation plans (OPLANs) and contingency operations. Medical leaders should be able to describe the depth they can achieve in terms of medical operational reach. _Operational reach_ is the distance and duration across which a force can successfully employ military capabilities (JP 3-0). Medical leaders should clearly articulate hospitalization capabilities and limitations regarding friendly operations in the orders process and during rehearsals. 

#### **HOSPITALIZATION LINK TO THE ARMY IMPERATIVES** 

1-52. Imperatives are actions Army forces take to defeat enemy forces and achieve objectives at an acceptable cost. They are informed by the OE and the characteristics of the most capable threats Army forces can encounter. The Army imperatives include— 

- See yourself, see the enemy, and understand the OE. 

- Protect against constant observation and all forms of enemy contact. 

- Own the electromagnetic spectrum. 

- Create and exploit positions of physical, information, and human advantages in pursuit of decision dominance. 

- Make initial contact with the sensors, unmanned systems, or the smallest element possible. 

- Impose multiple dilemmas on the enemy. 

- Anticipate, plan, and execute transitions. 

- Designate, weight, and sustain the main effort. 

- Consolidate gains continuously. 

- Understand and manage the effects of operations on units and Soldiers. 

1-53. Hospitalization leaders and planners should be familiar with the tenets of operations and imperatives. They should consider how the tenets and imperatives impact their units and how they impact the units they support when planning and executing hospitalization. Table 1-2 on page 13 lists the Army imperatives and includes how each applies to the hospitalization medical function. 

**Table 1-2. Hospitalization considerations for imperatives**

|---|---|

#### **THREATS** 

1-54. An OE has several threats that consist of enemies, adversaries, neutrals, and hybrid threats (force that combines traditional, irregular, disruptive, or catastrophic capabilities) and includes health threats. These threats are protracted confrontation among individuals, groups of individuals, paramilitary or military forces, state actors, and nonstate actors increasingly willing to use violence to achieve their political and ideological ends. For more information on the OE, refer to FM 3-0. 

1-55. Commanders and staffs analyze an OE using the eight operational variables: political, military, economic, social, information, infrastructure, physical environment, and time. For more information on the operational variables, refer to FM 5-0 and FM 6-0. 

##### **HEALTH THREAT** 

1-56. The operational medicine views threats from two perspectives: the general threat and the health threat. Although the operational medicine’s primary concern is that of the health threat, the general threat should also be fully considered as it influences the— 

- Character, types, and severity of wounds and injuries to which our forces may be exposed. 

- Enemy’s ability and willingness to disrupt AHS operations and to respect the conditions of the Geneva Conventions regarding the protection of medical personnel while engaged in their humanitarian mission. 

1-57. The health threat is a composite of ongoing or potential enemy actions; adverse environmental, occupational, and geographic and meteorological conditions; endemic and emerging diseases; and employment of chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear (CBRN) weapons (to include weapons of mass destruction that have the potential to affect the short- or long-term health [including psychological impact] of personnel). The health threat faced by deployed United States forces is depicted in Appendix B. 

##### **ENEMY THREAT** 

1-58. Warfare has changed significantly from World War II (WWII) through recent operations in Iraq and Afghanistan to observations in Eastern Europe. While the lethality of WWII was unprecedented and every domain contested, the conflicts afterwards were lower intensity where U.S. Forces enjoyed freedom of movement due to air and maritime dominance. Casualties of recent operations in Iraq and Afghanistan had high survival rates, but this was against armed violent extremist organizations. Modern armed conflict with a peer will more closely resemble WWII with highly lethal modern weapon systems and contested in all domains. Modern artillery ammunition is five to ten times more lethal than those used in WWII. The cyberspace domain has the potential to compromise or interrupt Army information systems. Due to the lethality of these weapon systems, protection should be a constant consideration in any AHS support plan where it is regularly reassessed and improved whenever possible. 

1-59. The proliferation of drones and electromagnetic warfare in recent years creates a constant risk of observation and attack. As a result, hospitalization units should continually be vigilant and consider noise, light, sound, and electromagnetic signature discipline. While dispersion is a technique, hospitalization units are not designed to execute dispersion without degrading or losing capability and should seek other methods of cover and concealment. 

1-60. The threat to hospitalization units varies by intensity and location of the conflict. A European conflict will most likely be contiguous and involve large frontages, massed artillery, and contested airspace. In an Indo-Pacific conflict, it will likely be non-contiguous in nature and involve long lines of communication which will require flexible and creative solutions for hospitalization operations. A conflict in a maritime environment will be United States Navy led and require a greater integration of joint capabilities to support Army hospitalization. Intratheater aeromedical evacuation may not be possible until patients are moved further away from enemy areas, resulting in Role 3 hospitals conducting delayed evacuation operations. 

1-61. Deployed hospitalization units will be exposed to multiple threats including: 

- Drones (unmanned vehicles and robotics). 

- Indirect fire (artillery, rockets, and missiles). 

- Electromagnetic warfare. 

- Armored combat vehicles. 

- Aircraft (fixed and rotary-wing). 

- Weapons of mass destruction (CBRN). 

- Unconventional warfare. 

1-62. Long-range fire systems such as tube artillery and multiple rocket launchers will threaten hospitalization units in the division, corps, and theater echelons. This places hospitalization units at risk of being targeted outright or most likely by proximity. Drones provide a threat of observation and attack to hospitalization units. Even if a drone does not initially engage an ambulance, it may follow it back to the Role 3 hospital to locate and fire on other targets co-located in the vicinity, such as command posts and sustainment assets. Hospitalization units should also have a primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency (PACE) plan for communication and data management. This provides redundant means of communicating and managing information to include analog. Analog systems may be necessary when an enemy targets electromagnetic signatures because analog systems have a reduced or no electromagnetic signature.

##### **<mark>SECTION V – HOSPITALIZATION ASPECTS OF THE ARMY HEALTH SYSTEM PRINCIPLES</mark>** 

1-63. The principles of the AHS are the enduring tenets upon which the delivery of health care in a deployed environment is founded. The principles guide medical planners in developing operation plans which are effective, efficient, flexible, and executable. Army Health System plans are designed to support the tactical commander’s scheme of maneuver while still retaining a focus on Soldiers’ medical needs. 

1-64. The AHS principles apply across all medical functions and are synchronized by medical commanders through the medical command and control (C2) medical function that is nested within the C2 system. Surgeon sections at echelon provide close coordination, synchronization, and integration of all medical assets through medical technical channels (medical control). The AHS principles are conformity, proximity, flexibility, mobility, continuity, and control. These six principles reinforce and complement the Army tenets of operations and imperatives. This section describes the AHS principles through the lens of hospitalization. For a complete discussion of how the AHS principles relate to the overall FHP and HSS missions, refer to FM 4-02. 

#### **CONFORMITY** 

1-65. Conformity with the tactical plan is the most basic element for effectively providing operational medicine support. Medical planners should be involved early in the planning process to ensure continued operational medicine in support of the Army’s strategic contexts. Once the plan is established it should be rehearsed with the forces it supports. 

1-66. Hospitalization support may vary depending on the mission. Medical leaders and planners should be involved throughout the planning process to ensure that hospitalization assets are properly arrayed across the OE in support of the maneuver commander’s intent and vision. Hospitalization units at echelon are employed based on the orders process, PAR, and the basis of allocation (BOA). Hospital leaders and planners should understand that hospitalization planning varies depending on the types of supported operations. Role 3 hospitals are scalable and tailorable and can be expanded and augmented to provide the necessary support. When operations do not require movement, a Role 3 hospital may be configured to its maximum capability. When supporting operations, such as those in large-scale combat, require the ability to quickly relocate Role 3 hospitals, a supporting Role 3 hospital may be utilized with a reduced footprint to support patient care and planned movement. During large-scale combat operations against a peer threat, Role 3 hospitals within the OE may be overwhelmed due to high casualty rates and increased consumption of resources. Supported maneuver planners and hospital planners prioritize support through the orders process. 

#### **PROXIMITY** 

1-67. Proximity is to provide operational medicine support to WII Soldiers at the right time and the right place and to keep morbidity and mortality to a minimum. Operational medicine resources should be arrayed near supported forces, but not close enough to impede ongoing operations. 

1-68. To support the operational commander’s plan, it is essential that hospitalization units are positioned to rapidly receive, treat, stabilize, and coordinate for the evacuation of patients. Medical planners have not been required to plan for hospitalization at this scale and scope since Desert Shield and Desert Storm. Given the higher casualty rates and additional difficulties associated with large-scale combat operations, establishing a Role 3 hospital in the right place to best support maneuver forces is critical. For more information on largescale combat operations, refer to FM 3-0. 

#### **FLEXIBILITY** 

1-69. Flexibility is being prepared to, and empowered to, shift operational medicine resources to meet changing requirements. Changes in plans or operations make flexibility in operational medicine planning and execution essential. 

1-70. Hospital leaders should ensure that the unit can rapidly transition from supporting one level of violence to another across the range of military operations or to simultaneously support more than one type of operation, such as offensive, defensive, or stability tasks. Hospitalization design supports the ability to adapt to conditions or type of operation by providing medical commanders the capability to manage hospitalization services through its modular units. Through modularity, the hospital commander exercises C2 authority to effectively manage limited hospitalization resources so that they benefit the greatest number of Soldiers in their assigned AO. (See chapter 2 for more information on hospitalization modularity). 

#### **MOBILITY** 

1-71. Mobility ensures that operational medicine assets remain in supporting distance to maneuver forces. Operational medicine support should be continually responsive to shifting medical requirements in an OE. Operational medicine C2 headquarters continually assess and forecast unit movement and redeployment. 

1-72. This is difficult for the Role 3 hospital leaders and planners. It takes time to setup (complex) and teardown (decomplex) a Role 3 hospital, time that may not meet the supported maneuver plans or orders sequence of events. With limited organic transportation assets, the Role 3 hospital should be innovative in its use of organic and sustainment transportation platforms to meet the maneuver commander’s intent. For instance, in support of offensive operations, instead of setting up a 120-bed Role 3 hospital in the division, the hospital commander may choose to discuss assuming risk with the supporting medical brigade (support) (MEDBDE) or division surgeon section and only set up 88 beds and leave a field hospital (32-bed) packed up and ready to jump forward in support of the division. This technique maximizes the use of organic transportation platforms and greatly reduces dependency on sustainment transportation. Role 3 hospital leaders and planners should always consider the limited mobility of the Role 3 hospital in the military decision-making process and develop courses of action to mitigate this gap. See Appendix B for amounts of transportation support required to move each hospital element. 

#### **CONTINUITY** 

1-73. Continuity in care and treatment is achieved by moving the patient through progressive, phased roles of care, extending from the POI to the CONUS support base. _Continuity of care_ refers to an attempt to maintain the role of care during movement at least equal to the care provided at the preceding facility (FM 4-02). Each type of operational medicine unit contributes a measured, logical increment in care appropriate to its location and capabilities. 

1-74. The Role 3 hospital constitutes the third measured, logical increment in care appropriate to its location and capabilities. In recent operations, lower casualty rates, availability of rotary-wing air ambulances, and other factors often enabled a patient to be evacuated from the POI directly to a supporting Role 3 hospital. However, during large-scale combat operations higher casualty rates, extended distances, indirect fire, drones, and patient condition will necessitate that patients receive care at each role of care to maintain their physiologic status and enhance the chances of survival. Lack of air superiority, use of unmanned aerial vehicles, and enemy activities will make patient movement (PM) much more difficult. This may require that those patients stay at a MTF for longer periods of time than previously required. A major consideration and an emerging concern in future conflicts is providing prolonged care at any location along the continuum of care (POI through Role 3) when evacuation is delayed. The Army’s employment in support of large-scale combat operations is likely to be complex and challenging and widely differs from recent conflicts. The hospital commander and leaders working with the supporting MEDBDE, with a depth of medical knowledge, the ability to anticipate follow-on medical treatment requirements, and an assessment of the availability of specialized medical resources can adjust the patient flow to ensure each Soldier receives the care required to optimize patient outcomes. 

#### **CONTROL** 

1-75. Control is required to ensure that scarce AHS resources are efficiently employed and support the operational plan. It also ensures that the scope and quality of medical treatment meets professional standards, policies, and U.S. and international law. Within the operational area, the most qualified individuals to orchestrate this complex support are the medical commanders and surgeons assigned to surgeon sections at echelon, due to their training, professional knowledge, education, and experience. 

1-76. For hospitalization, control also ensures that the scope and quality of medical care meets professional standards, policies, and U.S. and international law. With the current task organization of the Role 3 hospital center, there are dependencies between the headquarters and headquarters detachment (HHD), hospital center, and the field hospital (32-bed) in terms of control. With limited C2 infrastructure within these two units, the most reliable course of action is for the HHD hospital center to concentrate on up and out and future operations while the field hospital (32-bed) focuses on "down and in" and current operations. 

1-77. The hospital center can conduct split-based operations when task organized with two field hospitals (32-bed). It is the hospital center commander’s decision, based on their knowledge of the units and the capabilities of their leaders, on which field hospital would forward deploy and what command and support relationship would be generated in the orders process. 

##### **SECTION VI – ELIGIBILITY OF CARE DETERMINATION** 

1-78. Across the range of military operations, one of the most pressing questions is, who is eligible for care in a U.S. Army-established MTF, and the extent of care authorized. Numerous categories of personnel seek care in U. S. facilities that are in austere areas where the host-nation civilian medical infrastructure is not sufficient to provide adequate care. A determination of eligibility and whether reimbursement for services is required is made at the highest level possible and in conjunction with the supporting staff judge advocate. Each operation is unique and the authorization for care is based on appropriate U. S. and international law, Department of Defense (DOD) directives, DOD instructions, Army regulations, doctrine, and standard operating procedures. Other factors impacting the determination of eligibility are command guidance, practical humanitarian and medical ethics considerations, availability of United States medical assets (in relationship to the threat faced by the force), and the potential training opportunities for medical forces. 

1-79. Through the orders process, hospital commanders should ensure that the eligibility for care matrix is widely disseminated throughout the command and that all medical personnel are aware of the process and how to obtain additional guidance, if required. Eligibility of care determination may impact the Role 3 hospital and effect several hospital planning considerations. Hospital leaders should look for the eligibility of care determination in Annex F (Sustainment), Appendix 3 (HSS) and plan accordingly. See Appendix B of this publication for an example of an eligibility for medical and dental care support matrix for inclusion in operations plans and orders. For more information on eligibility of care determination, refer to FM 4-02. 

##### **SECTION VII – GENEVA CONVENTIONS AND MEDICAL ETHICS** 

1-80. The U.S. is party to numerous conventions and treaties pertinent to warfare on land. Collectively, these treaties are often referred to as the Geneva and Hague Conventions. Whereas the Hague Conventions concern the methods and means of warfare, the Geneva Conventions concern the victims of war or armed conflict. The Geneva Conventions are four separate international treaties, signed in 1949. The Conventions are very detailed and contain many provisions, which are tied directly to the medical mission. These Conventions are entitled— 

- Convention (I) for the Amelioration of the Condition of the Wounded and Sick in Armed Forces in the Field. 

- Convention (II) for the Amelioration of the Condition of Wounded, Sick and Shipwrecked Members of Armed Forces at Sea. 

- Convention (III) relative to the Treatment of Prisoners of War. 

- Convention (IV) relative to the Protection of Civilian Persons in Time of War. 

1-81. The Geneva and Hague Conventions and medical ethics play a part in the hospitalization medical function. The employment of Role 3 hospitals requires knowledge not only of military field craft but also what else can impact patient care operations. This section discusses the Law of Land Warfare, Geneva Conventions, and medical ethics. 

#### **LAW OF ARMED CONFLICT** 

1-82. The conduct of armed hostilities on land is regulated by the Law of Armed Conflict. For additional information on the Law of Land Warfare, refer to DOD Directive 2310.01E and FM 6-27. This body of law is inspired by the desire to diminish the evils of war by— 

- Protecting both combatants and noncombatants from unnecessary suffering. 

- Safeguarding certain fundamental human rights of persons who fall into the hands of the enemy, particularly detainees, the wounded and sick, and civilians. 

- Facilitating the restoration of peace. 

1-83. The Law of Armed Conflict places limits on the exercise of a belligerent’s power in the interest of furthering that desire (diminishing the evils of war) and it requires that belligerents— 

- Refrain from employing any kind or degree of violence which is not actually necessary for military purposes. 

- Conduct hostilities with regard for the principles of humanity and chivalry. 

#### **GENEVA AND HAGUE CONVENTIONS** 

1-84. The Geneva and Hague Conventions details the treatment and protection of patients, regardless of their status as a combatant, civilian, enemy, or friendly. They also discuss provisions afforded medical personnel and units. 

1-85. The Geneva Conventions afford the medical unit a certain degree of protection from attack. The extent to which the combatants and irregular forces on the battlefield are adhering to the provisions of the Geneva Conventions has a bearing on site selection in that it may dictate the degree of required security for the unit. For more information on compliance with the Geneva Conventions and medical ethics, refer to FM 4-02 and Appendix C of this publication. Role 3 hospital leaders should refer to and keep on hand Appendix C of this publication, FM 6-27, and the detailed FM 4-02 information on the medical aspects of the Geneva and Hague Conventions. The main topics in FM 4-02 include: 

- Protection of the wounded and sick. 

- Protection and care. 

- Enemy wounded and sick. 

 - Search for and collection of patients. 

 - Assistance of the civilian population. 

 - Enemy civilian wounded and sick. 

- Medical repatriation. 

- Protection and identification of medical personnel. 

 - Protection. 

 - Specific cases. 

 - Identification cards and armbands. 

- Protection and identification of medical units, establishments, buildings, materiel, and medical transports. 

 - Protection from intentional attack. 

 - Protection when falling into the hands of the enemy. 

 - Identification. 

 - Loss of protection of medical establishments and units. 

 - Acts harmful to the enemy. 

 - Warning and time limit. 

 - Use of smoke and obscurants. 

- Conditions not depriving medical units and establishments of protection.  Acts. 

 - Defense of medical units and self-defense by medical personnel. 

 - Guarding medical units. 

 - Arms and ammunition taken from the wounded. 

 - Personnel and materiel of the veterinary corps. 

 - Care of civilian wounded and sick. 

- The 1977 protocols to the Geneva Conventions. 

- Compliance With the Geneva Conventions. 

- Medical care for retained and detained personnel. 

- Violations and consequences. 

#### **MEDICAL ETHICS** 

1-86. Health care personnel are well-trained in and guided by the ethics of their professional calling. This training and ethical principles, coupled with the requirements of international law as it pertains to the treatment of detainees, and civilians during conflict will ensure the ethical treatment of all sick and wounded personnel. 

1-87. Health care personnel engaged in a professional provider-patient treatment relationship with detainees shall not participate in detainee-related activities for purposes other than health care. Role 3 hospital leaders should see and keep on hand Appendix C of this publication. For detailed information on medical ethics, refer to FM 4-02. 

##### **Chapter 2** 

### **Hospitalization Organizations** 

The hospital center provides Role 3 essential care within the theater evacuation policy to either return the patient to duty or stabilize the patient for evacuation to a Role 4 MTF outside the AO. The hospital center’s assigned medical personnel, facilities, equipment, and materials provide the requisite capabilities to render significant preventive and curative health care. These highly robust services encompass primary inpatient and outpatient care; emergency care; and enhanced medical, surgical, and ancillary capabilities. The modular design of the hospital center provides the capability to tailor and deploy capabilities as elements or multiple individual capabilities that provide incrementally increased hospitalization services. This chapter provides information on the mission, assignment and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment of these units. 

##### **SECTION I – INTRODUCTION TO THE HOSPITAL CENTER** 

2-1. The hospital center is a Role 3 MTF comprised of the HHD, hospital center and four modular units with inherent capabilities that deliver mission-specific medical and surgical support. This modular design can provide a task-organized Role 3 hospital with a minimum of 32-beds up to a maximum of 240 beds within an assigned AO. The characteristics of each hospital element and their collective competencies provide the ability to accommodate split-based operations with both surgical and medical support. Commanders can taskorganize these hospitalization elements to support the range of military operations. This matches the anticipated mix of required capabilities and medical specialties to the population supported and the clinical challenges they present. The hospital center, in combination with varying augmentation detachments, coupled with attached specialty detachments and teams provides the medical and staffing capabilities to provide the appropriate Role 3 medical care suitable to supporting the range of military operations across all strategic contexts within the competition continuum. 

2-2. The Role 3 hospital center is designed to support the Army’s requirement to conduct a mix of offensive, defensive, stability tasks, and defense support of civil authorities simultaneously in a variety of scenarios. Under the C2 of the HHD, hospital center, there are four distinct modular elements, with associated TOEs, that comprise the Role 3 hospital. These elements are: 

- The field hospital (32-bed) (up to two). 

- The hospital augmentation detachment (surgical 24-bed), referred to as surgical augmentation detachment (SAD). 

- The hospital augmentation detachment (medical 32-bed), referred to as medical augmentation detachment (MAD). 

- The hospital augmentation detachment (intermediate care ward [ICW]) 60-bed) (up to two), referred to as intermediate care ward augmentation detachment (ICWAD). 

**_Note._** The organizational structures and positions presented in this publication are established in tables of organization and equipment. These tables were current at the time this publication was released. The organization of these units is subject to change to comply with manpower requirements criteria and other force design updates. These organizations are also subject to change at the unit level to meet wartime requirements and changes are reflected in the units’ modified table of organization and equipment. 

2-3. When referring to the entire organization (or at its minimum configuration, the HHD, hospital center and one field hospital [32-bed]), it is called the hospital center. The field hospital is simply the 32-bed subordinate unit that is one piece of the hospital center. 

2-4. The modular design of the Role 3 hospital center provides the ability to tailor and deploy capabilities as modules or multiple individual capabilities that provide incrementally increased hospitalization services. The hospital center may be augmented by one or more hospital augmentation detachments, medical detachments, or hospital augmentation teams. The Role 3 hospital center may also be task organized to enhance its capabilities to provide FHP and HSS within its assigned AO. The current hospital center organizational construct provides a more agile, deployable, versatile, and medically capable hospital. 

2-5. The HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) are the core and lowest denominator of the hospital organization. The field hospital (32-bed) represents the smallest unit that can provide the complete clinical capabilities of a Role 3 MTF. This company-sized unit is designed to be self-supporting and expandable. The HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) are designed as the first increment to be deployed in support of an expeditionary force. The HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) can be expanded incrementally to a maximum 240-bed capability (see Appendix B for figures that depict various Role 3 configurations). 

2-6. The HHD, hospital center can provide C2 for one or two field hospitals (32-bed) in separate locations without augmentation. The field hospital (32-bed) is not designed to be split into two parts but rather two field hospitals (32-bed) can establish operations in two separate locations while task-organized under one HHD, hospital center. Combinations of the modular units within the hospitalization capability would be suitable to support across the range of military operations and to fully integrate operations with joint, HN, and UAPs. 

**_Note._** Planning and providing hospitalization present unique challenges to the hospital commander, medical planner, and staff. Based on a myriad of mission variables, it may be necessary, but not by design, to employ the field hospital (32-bed) independent from the HHD, hospital center for a short period of time. Simultaneous or near-simultaneous employment of the two organizations is ideal for C2, consultative services, and utilization of equipment resources. If mission variables or resource constraints prohibit the simultaneous employment of these organizations, the field hospital (32-bed) can employ and provide Role 3 hospitalization without the HHD, hospital center. The field hospital (32-bed) would be task-organized to the senior medical commander, such as the MEDBDE, until the HHD, hospital center is deployed to the AO. 

- 2-7. Specific capabilities of the Role 3 hospital center include: 

 - Computed tomography equipment. 

 - Microbiology laboratory services. 

 - Surgical support. 

 - Critical care provider (intensivist) to manage patients in the ICU. 

 - Internal medicine provider (hospitalist) to manage patients in the ICW. 

 - Emergency providers in the triage, preoperative care, and EMT section. 

 - Minimal inpatient behavioral health capabilities and behavioral health consultation and education. 

 - Increased capacity of ICU beds. 

- 2-8. Improvements in versatility and agility: 

 - Command and control and enhanced communications capability to conduct split based operations indefinitely without augmentation. 

 - Augmentation detachments with specific clinical specialties can be adapted to better support the mission. 

- 2-9. Deployability and adaptability: 

 - Initial hospitalization capabilities are provided by a HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32bed). 

 - The HHD, hospital center, field hospital (32-bed), and all hospital augmentation detachments have separate modular TOE structures. 

 - Each hospital augmentation detachment is designed to expand the field hospital’s (32-bed) medical or surgical capacities. 

- Commanders can tailor the medical forces to support the OE, matching the anticipated mix of capabilities and medical specialties to the population supported and the clinical challenges they present. 

- The modular composition of the hospital center makes planning for required medical capabilities much easier; planners can design and take only the field hospital (32-bed) and medical augmentation detachments that contain the medical treatment or surgical capabilities needed as determined in mission analysis. However, once the hospital is established and operating in the AO, it becomes very difficult to unplug an augmentation detachment and plug it back into to another hospital elsewhere. The augmentation detachments become an integral part of the hospital operations (personnel, equipment, and functions), and disconnecting one of them will be a challenge, but not impossible. 

- The hospitalization capability can be built up or scaled down based on the tactical situation. 

**_Note._** The remainder of this chapter provides a brief description of the duties and responsibilities of each type of hospitalization unit’s sections and teams. Any additional section or team duties and responsibilities should be annotated in the unit TACSOP. The unit TACSOP should also provide detailed duties and responsibilities for each assigned officer, warrant officer, enlisted, or civilian operating within the Role 3 hospital. 

##### **<mark>SECTION II – HEADQUARTERS AND HEADQUARTERS DETACHMENT, HOSPITAL CENTER</mark>** 

2-10. This section provides an overall discussion of the HHD, hospital center’s mission, assignments and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment. Any additions should be annotated in the unit TACSOP. 

#### **MISSION** 

2-11. The HHD, hospital center provides C2 to assigned and attached units engaged in the operational support of Role 3 hospitalization and outpatient services for all classes of patients within an AO. This is normally up to two field hospitals (32-bed) and requisite augmentation detachments all in one or more locations. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

2-12. The unit assignment and dependencies depict who the HHD, hospital center is assigned to and the units it is dependent upon to execute its mission. If there are additional dependencies, then the HHD hospital center should add them to their TACSOP along with how they work with each unit they are dependent upon. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

2-13. The HHD, hospital center is assigned to the headquarters and headquarters company, MEDBDE or senior medical headquarters within the AO. Assignment and task organization through the orders process can change rapidly and often. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

2-14. This unit is dependent upon appropriate elements within the AO for religious; legal; AHS support; finance; personnel and administrative services; logistical services, mortuary affairs; security of enemy prisoners of war (EPW), retained and detained patients, and United States (U.S.) prisoner patients. This unit is also dependent upon the following: 

- The composite supply company for Class I, II, III, and VII supplies and to provide potable water and unclassified map support. 

- Headquarters and headquarters company (HHC) and special troops battalion (STB), sustainment brigade or HHC and STB, division sustainment brigade for transportation services when a single lift of the unit is required, vehicle recovery operations, transportation and equipping for RTD personnel, to include individual clothing and equipment, seasonal outer garments, chemical protection garments, and shower and laundry services not related to patient care. 

- The engineer construction company to construct medical shelter platforms. 

- The prime power battalion to generate long-term hard-stand electrical power and provide advice and technical assistance for electrical power and distribution and for relief of tactical generators as required for the hospital. 

- The signal company, expeditionary signal battalion to provide automatic switching services for both analog and digital voice and data traffic, tactical multichannel high-capacity transmission systems, and multichannel satellite ground terminals. 

- The casualty liaison team for accurate and timely casualty information, facilitates real-time casualty information for commanders, and assists in the management of casualty operations as needed. 

- The field hospital (32-bed), nutrition care section for feeding of hospital staff. 

- The MEDBDE S-3 PM branch or U.S. Air Force aeromedical evacuation liaison team for coordinating intratheater and intertheater PM to higher roles of care, generally out of the AO. 

- The medical company, (ground ambulance) to provide ground MEDEVAC support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and emergency movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The medical company (air ambulance) to provide intratheater aeromedical evacuation support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The MEDLOG company for Class VIII support and augmentation of personnel for medical device maintenance, as required. 

- The following companies, detachments, and teams are needed to increase AHS hospitalization capabilities and capacity as required: 

 - Field hospital (32-bed). 

 - SAD (24-bed). 

 - MAD (32-bed). 

 - ICWAD (60-bed). 

 - Hospital augmentation team (head and neck). 

 - Medical detachment (forward resuscitative and surgical) (to include the airborne FRSD). 

 - Medical detachment (minimal care). 

 - Medical detachment (prolonged care augmentation). 

 - The medical detachment (veterinary services) for food protection and veterinary public health support. 

**_Note._** The food protection mission includes food safety; food defense; quality assurance inspection and surveillance activities associated with rations, food, bottled water, and ice manufacturing sources; and food and water risk assessments. The veterinary public health reduces transmission of zoonotic diseases; monitors animals as sentinels of threats to humans by investigating unexplained animal deaths; and investigates potential rabies exposures to humans. The potential of foodborne disease, the threat of CBRN contamination of subsistence, and the need to assess and mitigate the zoonotic disease threat requires a veterinary presence. 

#### **CAPABILITIES AND FUNCTIONS** 

2-15. The unit capabilities and functions describe what the HHD, hospital center is capable of accomplishing. Functions are descriptions of what each subcomponent of the HHD, hospital center is responsible for in support of the overall mission. Due to assigned missions, if there are additional capabilities or functions then the HHD hospital center should add them to their TACSOP along with prescriptive verbiage for each capability and function. 

##### **CAPABILITIES** 

2-16. This unit provides the following: 

- Command and control of organic and attached elements, to include AHS support planning, policies, and support operations within the hospital’s AO. 

- Information to commanders and staff on the health of the command and on health aspects affecting the unit’s mission or AHS support. 

- Command and control for split-based operations as required. 

- Augmentation to the field hospital, (32-bed) motor maintenance section. 

- Additional maintenance capabilities of the field hospital (32-bed). 

- Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

##### **FUNCTIONS** 

2-17. The HHD, hospital center has seven sections that comprise the headquarters element. They are as follows: 

- Command Section. 

- Personnel Section (S-1). 

- Intelligence/Operations Section (S-2/S-3). 

- Logistics Section (S-4). 

- Signal Section (S-6). 

- Clinical Operations Section. 

- Transportation Section. 

2-18. The following information describes the functions of each element and requirements specified in the section I of the TOE. Figure 2-1 depicts the task organization of the HHD, hospital center. 

<!-- Start of picture text -->
Legend:<br>OPS operations S-2 / S-3 intelligence / operations<br>PLS palletized load system S-4 logistics<br>PS personnel services S-6 signal<br>S-1 personnel<br><!-- End of picture text -->

**Figure 2-1. Task organization of the headquarters and headquarters detachment, hospital center** 

###### **Command Section** 

2-19. The command section provides C2 for elements of the hospital and coordination for hospitalization support within the hospital’s AO. The command section provides advice to supported tactical commanders on the health of the command and provides medical surveillance activities within the AO. Command and staff personnel provide supervision and coordination of administrative, logistics, operations, medical, surgical, nursing, and hospital ministry services. 

###### **Clinical Operations Section** 

2-20. The clinical operations section— 

- Consists of the principal consultants and technical advisors for the command in medical, surgical, and nursing care. 

- Manages medical provider credentialing and administration as well as clinical care quality assurance, treatment protocol management, and AHS support and training. 

- Establishes patient management policies, and ensures facilities and resources are adequate to treat all types of disease and injury, to include CBRN patients. 

- Provides staff supervision of clinical activities throughout the hospital to include proper staffing of the subordinate hospital elements. 

- Plans and coordinates health services clinical resources within the hospital. 

- Plans and coordinates clinical medical resources to provide effective and consistent treatment of WII personnel to RTD or evacuate from the AO. 

- Monitors clinical policies, protocols, and procedures pertaining to the medical and surgical treatment of WII personnel IAW Army Regulation (AR) 40-68. 

- Plans and monitors the provision of direct patient care within assigned or attached hospital elements. 

- Monitors the management of clinical specialties including professional medical provider rotation policies, if applicable. 

- Evaluates and interprets medical statistical data that includes hospitalization and bed reports. 

###### **S-1 Section** 

2-21. The S-1 section— 

- Provides human resources services for the hospital center and subordinate elements attached and assigned, to include personnel administration, mail distribution, and awards and decorations. 

- Maintains absence and rest and recuperation schedules. 

- Coordinates for morale, welfare, and recreation support. 

- Prepares and manages correspondence for the command. 

- Maintains unit personnel readiness status. 

- Ensures personnel and deployment manifests are maintained and coordinates for replacements as needed. 

- Coordinates with the senior medical unit’s protocol office and public affairs officer for protocol and public affairs support. 

###### **S-2/S-3 Section** 

###### 2-22. The S-2/S-3 section— 

- Plans and coordinates medical resources to provide effective and consistent treatment of WII personnel to return to full duty or evacuate from the AO. 

- Plans and coordinates health services resources within the hospital. 

- Assists in maintaining situational understanding by coordinating for current medical intelligence and the common operating picture with the MEDBDE and theater medical command (TMC) S-2 (G-2)/S-3 (G-3). 

- Maintains tactical situational understanding through coordination with supported units. 

- Assists in coordinating AHS training requirements and execution within the hospital. 

- Develops mass casualty (MASCAL) plans and determines the medical workload requirements based upon the casualty estimate within the hospital’s AO. 

- Develops, synchronizes, and coordinates hospitalization support within the AO to support the commander’s decisions, planning guidance, and intent. 

- Forwards medical statistical data, including hospitalization and bed reports, in accordance with established procedures. 

- Provides support to the commander for plans, intelligence, operations, security, deployment, redeployment, and relocation of the hospital center and subordinate elements. 

###### **S-4 Section** 

###### 2-23. The S-4 section— 

- Monitors MEDLOG services within the hospital and supported units. 

- Monitors health services and FHP resources within the hospital. 

- Determines MEDLOG requirements and priorities. 

- Adapts medical materiel sets (MMS) for a specific scenario to include adding items based on forecasted types of injuries. 

- Monitors the requisition, procurement, storage, maintenance, distribution, management, and documentation of Class VIII materiel and special hospital items of subsistence required for patient care. 

- Provides planning, programming, coordinating, and supervision of all activities concerning the internal logistical operations of the hospital center and subordinate elements. 

- Maintains and manages the hospital’s property book.
