- Coordinates for external logistical support requirements, such as equipment transportation support, inbound resupply tracking, and contracts. 

- Coordinates for resupply of all classes of supply. 

- Monitors medical device and nonmedical equipment status, reporting, and maintenance programs. 

###### **S-6 Section** 

2-24. The S-6 section— 

- Provides for all aspects of automation and communications-electronics for the command. 

- Determines signal requirements, capabilities, and operations. 

- Provides advice and consultation on medical information systems used within the hospital center and subordinate elements. 

- Ensures internal and external communication connectivity within the hospital and subordinate elements. 

- Establishes and maintains internal information systems, servers, devices, and telemedicine systems. 

###### **Transportation Section** 

2-25. The transportation section— 

- Provides organic transportation required for mission accomplishment of the field hospital (32-bed) (this is only vehicles and maintenance personnel, the drivers come from the HHD, hospital center subordinate units). 

- Provides transportation for the HHD, hospital center. 

- Coordinates for external transportation support when the mission demands exceed organic assets. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

2-26. The HHD, hospital center deploys and provides C2 to assigned and attached units engaged in the operational support of hospitalization and outpatient services for all classes of patients within its assigned AO. The hospital center also provides medical C2 of FHP and HSS to ensure they are included in the command’s running estimates, operation orders (OPORD), and OPLAN. The HHD, hospital center deploys with the first field hospital (32-bed) and concentrates on up and out C2 of the hospital center while the field hospital concentrates on ("down and in") C2 of the field hospital and any attached augmentation elements.

2-27. Based on the orders process, the HHD, hospital center may be collocated with two field hospitals at one location. In a split-based configuration, the HHD, hospital center will remain with one field hospital and may provide C2 to the second forward deployed field hospital if required. See Chapter 3 and Appendix B. of this publication for more information on the employment and planning of the HHD, hospital center. 

**_Note._** It is important to note that the HHD, hospital center is designed to be able to provide C2 of two field hospitals (32-bed) in two separate locations, not one field hospital (32-bed) split in two locations. The field hospital (32-bed) is not designed to be split. 

##### **BASIS OF ALLOCATION** 

2-28. The BOA for an HHD, hospital center is one per a maximum of two field hospitals (32-bed), and hospital augmentation detachments, up to a total of 240 beds. If medical planners determine that one HHD, hospital center cannot adequately provide C2 of two separated field hospitals (32-bed) due to distance and insufficient communications, a second HHD, hospital center should be requested, METT-TC (I) dependent. 

##### **MOBILITY** 

2-29. The unit requires 35 percent of its TOE equipment to be transported in a single lift with its authorized organic vehicles. The unit’s organic transportation section has the vehicles required to transport 35 percent of the field hospital (32-bed) and is responsible for transportation support of hospital augmentation detachments (with personnel augmentation from the supported units to drive the vehicles). 

##### **SECTION III – FIELD HOSPITAL (32-BED)** 

2-30. This section provides a discussion of the field hospital (32-bed), mission, assignments and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment. Any additions should be annotated in the unit TACSOP. 

#### **MISSION** 

2-31. The field hospital (32-bed) provides Role 3 hospitalization and outpatient services for all classes of patients within an assigned AO. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

2-32. Assignment means units or personnel assigned relatively permanently to a command or mission, where that organization controls and administers the units or personnel for the primary function, or greater portion of the functions, of the unit or personnel. 

2-33. A dependency is a limitation or capability gap a unit may have that results in it being dependent on another organization to mitigate that limitation or gap. The Section I may list dependencies, but the unit TACSOP should add additional dependencies as required and to describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how these units will support the field hospital (32-bed). 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

2-34. The field hospital (32-bed) is assigned to the HHD, hospital center. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

2-35. This unit is dependent upon the following: 

- Appropriate elements within the AO for religious; legal; AHS support; finance; personnel and administrative and logistical services; mortuary affairs; security of EPW, retained and detained patients, and U.S. prisoner patients. 

- The composite supply company for Class I, II, III, and VII supplies and to provide potable water and unclassified map support. 

- HHC and STB, sustainment brigade or HHC and STB, division sustainment brigade for transportation services when a single lift of the unit is required, vehicle recovery operations, transportation and equipping for RTD personnel, to include individual clothing and equipment, seasonal outer garments, chemical protection garments, and shower and laundry services not related to patient care. 

- The engineer construction company to construct medical shelter platforms. 

- The prime power battalion to generate electrical power and to provide advice and technical assistance for electrical power and distribution. 

- The expeditionary signal company to provide automatic switching services for both analog and digital voice and data traffic, tactical multichannel high-capacity transmission systems, and multichannel satellite ground terminals. 

- The casualty liaison team for accurate and timely casualty information, facilitates real-time casualty information for commanders, and assists in the management of casualty operations as needed. 

- The MEDBDE S-3 PM branch or U.S. Air Force aeromedical evacuation liaison team for coordinating intratheater and intertheater PM to higher roles of care, generally out of the AO. 

- The medical company, (ground ambulance) to provide ground PM support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The medical company (air ambulance) to provide intratheater aeromedical evacuation support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The MEDLOG company for Class VIII support. Augmentation of personnel for medical device maintenance, as required. 

- The following companies, detachments, and teams are needed to increase AHS hospitalization capabilities and capacity as required: 

 - Field hospital (32-bed). 

 - SAD (24-bed). 

 - MAD (32-bed). 

 - ICWAD (60-bed). 

 - Hospital augmentation team (head and neck). 

 - Medical detachment, forward resuscitative and surgical (to include the airborne FRSD). 

 - Medical detachment, minimal care. 

 - Medical detachment (PCAD). 

- The medical detachment (veterinary services) for food protection and veterinary public health support. 

#### **CAPABILITIES AND FUNCTIONS** 

2-36. The unit capabilities and functions depict what the field hospital (32-bed) is capable of accomplishing. Functions are descriptions of what each subcomponent of the field hospital (32-bed) is responsible for in support of the overall mission. Due to assigned missions, if there are additional capabilities or functions then the field hospital (32-bed) should add them to their TACSOP along with prescriptive verbiage for each capability and function. 

##### **CAPABILITIES** 

2-37. This unit provides— 

- Company-level C2 of organic elements to include AHS support, planning, policies, and support operations within the hospital’s AO and can operate up to 72 hours with its initial basic load of supply. 

- Information to commanders and their staff on the health of their command and on health aspects affecting the unit’s missions. 

- Hospitalization for up to 32 patients consisting of one ward providing intensive nursing care for up to 12 patients and one ward providing intermediate nursing care for up to 20 patients. 

- Surgical capability, including general, orthopedic, and obstetrics-gynecological based on operating room (OR) tables capable of providing 36 OR hours per day. 

- Emergency treatment to receive, triage, and resuscitate patients to include not only military personnel but eligible DOD Civilian employees and contractors, local nationals, detainees, and EPWs/retained/unprivileged enemy belligerents personnel as required. 

- Army Health System support for organic personnel (unit-level support). 

- Pharmacy, clinical laboratory, blood banking, radiology/CT, and nutrition care service for patients and organic staff. 

- Personnel administration, patient administration, MEDLOG, preventive medicine (PVNTMED), and laundry services for direct patient-related linen and shower facilities for ambulatory patients, and direct patient care providers. 

- Coordinates with the U.S. Air Force Theater Patient Movement Requirements Center for medical regulating and movement of patients from the AO. 

- Technical advice and consultation on medical information systems and programs such as the operational medicine information system-Army (OMIS-A) comprised of the Theater Medical Information Program-Joint (TMIP-J) software suite. 

- All work areas and assemblages with three days of supply within identified MMS. 

- Field maintenance support to all elements of the hospital and all attached and assigned units. 

- Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

##### **FUNCTIONS** 

2-38. The field hospital (32-bed) has 19 elements that comprise the company. They are: 

- Command Section. 

- Company Headquarters. 

- Operations Section. 

- Patient Administration Section. 

- Nutrition Care Section. 

- Supply and Services Section. 

- Motor Maintenance Section. 

- Laundry and Shower Section. 

- Triage, Preoperative, Emergency Medical Treatment Section. 

- Operating Room and Central Materiel Services Section. 

- Anesthesia Service Section. 

- Nursing Service Section. 

- Intensive Care Unit. 

- Intermediate Care Ward. 

- Pharmacy Section. 

- Laboratory Services and Blood Bank Section. 

- Radiology Section. 

- Computed Tomography Section. 

- Hospital Ministry Team. 

2-39. The following information describes the functions of each element and requirements specified in the section I of the TOE. This also describes the task and purpose of each section. Figure 2-2 on page 31 depicts the task organization of the field hospital (32-bed). 

<!-- Start of picture text -->
Legend:<br>CMS centralized material services ICU intensive care unit<br>COMP computerized ICW intensive care ward<br>EMT emergency medical treatment OPS operations<br>FWD forward OR operating room<br>LAB laboratory PRE-OP pre-operative<br>IC intensive care REL religion<br><!-- End of picture text -->

**Figure 2-2. Task organization of the field hospital (32-bed)** 

###### **Command Section** 

###### 2-40. The command section— 

- Provides internal C2 and management of the field hospital (32-bed) and attached hospital augmentation detachments and teams. 

- Provides administrative support, prepares unit plans for movement, routine and medical support operations, and mission-related task organization. 

- Focuses the field hospital (32-bed) staff on the internal operations of the hospital center and augmentation detachments as well as current and short-range operational planning ("down and in"), when collocated with the HHD, hospital center. 

**_Note._** The field hospital (32-bed) is a company level organization with limited C2 assets. As a course of action and mission dependent, after complexing the hospital, at the field hospital commander’s discretion, it is common practice to dual hat the senior practical nurse noncommissioned officer (NCO) as the field hospital (32-bed) senior enlisted leader. 

###### **Company Headquarters** 

###### 2-41. The company headquarters— 

- Provides C2 of the field hospital (32-bed) and attached hospital augmentation detachment and teams. 

- Supervises and coordinates the operations and administrative services. 

- Provides logistical functions for the hospital elements and attached units, to include general and medical supplies; environmental control of patient treatment areas; power and vehicle maintenance; fuel distribution; and equipment records and repair parts management. 

- Provides laundry services for direct patient care providers and patients and coordinates through the higher headquarters for all other laundry support. 

- Provides shower services for patients and medical providers. 

###### **Operations Section** 

2-42. The operations section— 

- Focuses on current and short-range operational planning when collocated with the HHD, hospital center. 

- Plans and coordinates medical resources to provide effective and consistent treatment of WII personnel involving return to full duty or evacuation from the hospital’s AO. 

- Plans and coordinates AHS resources within the hospital’s AO. 

- Compiles medical statistical data that includes hospitalization and bed reports and forwards them in accordance with established procedures. 

- Assists in maintaining situational understanding by coordinating for current AHS support information with medical operations of the next higher, adjacent, and subordinate headquarters. 

- Assists in coordinating AHS training requirements and execution within the hospital. 

- Develops MASCAL plans and determines the medical workload requirements based upon the casualty estimate within the hospital’s AO. 

- Develops, synchronizes, and coordinates AHS within the hospital’s AO to support the commander’s decisions, planning guidance, and intent. 

- Coordinates AHS training requirements and execution. 

- Provides support to the commander for plans, intelligence, operations, security, deployment, redeployment, and relocation of the field hospital (32-bed) and subordinate elements. 

- Provides for the installation, operation, management, and maintenance of the information management systems. 

- Establishes and maintains secure and nonsecure local area network. 

- Manages the joint operational medicine information systems applications and OMIS-A. 

- Ensures automated logistics interfaces in all sections of the hospital and attached units. 

###### **Patient Administration Section** 

2-43. Historically, formal medical regulating starts at Role 3 where more than one patient administrative personnel are first assigned withing the continuum of care. Informal medical regulating is normally conducted at Role 1 and Role 2. For additional information on medical regulating refer to ATP 4-02.2. The Role 3 patient administration section— 

- Secures and accounts for patients’ valuables. 

- Prepares patient-statistical reports for the hospital and coordinates reporting to the appropriate C2 structure. 

- Coordinates requests for patient evacuation and provides reports to higher headquarters. 

- For patients that have died of wounds received in action at the Role 3 hospital, coordinates with patient’s unit and mortuary affairs for prompt removal of remains and personal effects. 

- Provides patient administration services statistical data to the operations section. 

**_Note._** A casualty liaison team from a division sustainment brigade human resources company or combat sustainment support battalion human resources company may be collocated with the Role 3 hospital to assist in the management of casualty operations, provide casualty information (reporting and tracking), and facilitate real-time casualty information for commanders through the ability to provide updated information from supported sites such as the Role 3 hospital. 

###### **Nutrition Care Section** 

2-44. See Chapter 3 for more detailed discussion about nutrition care operations in a Role 3 hospital. The nutrition care section— 

- Supervises, manages, and controls all nutrition care operations within the hospital. 

- Provides food service management, including meal preparation, modified diet food preparation, and distribution of food and beverages to patients and staff. 

- Provides the full complement of medical nutrition therapy to inpatients and outpatients. 

- Serves as the command advisor on health and nutrition for patients and MTF staff. 

- May serve as dietician in general support of units in the hospital’s AO. 

- Provides nutrition care services for patients, including EPWs or retained/detained personnel while ensuring culturally appropriate foods are provided according to command guidance. 

- Provides nutrition care services logistics and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Supply and Service Section** 

2-45. See Chapter 3 for more detailed discussion about logistics management in a Role 3 hospital. Supply and services section provides logistical functions for the hospital and attached units to include: 

- Medical materiel. 

- Medical maintenance management. 

- Blood management (in coordination with clinical laboratory). 

- Water distribution. 

- Waste disposal. 

- Environmental control of patient treatment areas. 

- Temporary hospital morgue (only for those that expired while under care at the MTF). 

- Provides supply and services logistics requirements and statistical data to the operations section. 

###### **Motor Maintenance Section** 

- 2-46. Motor maintenance section performs field maintenance functions on— 

 - Organic wheeled vehicles. 

 - Power generation equipment. 

 - CBRN equipment. 

 - Environmental control units. 

###### **Laundry and Shower Section** 

- 2-47. Laundry and shower section— 

 - Provides laundry services for direct patient care providers and patients. 

 - Coordinates with supporting element for all other laundry support not directly related to patient care. 

 - Provides shower services for patients and medical providers. 

 - Provides laundry and shower logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Triage, Preoperative, Emergency Medical Treatment Section** 

###### 2-48. Triage, preoperative, EMT section— 

- Provides for the receiving, triaging, and stabilizing of incoming patients as follows: 

- • Receives patients. 

- Assesses their medical condition. 

- Provides EMT services. 

- Transfers the patient to the appropriate functional area within the hospital. 

- Monitors patient conditions and prepares those requiring immediate surgery for the OR. 

- Communicates directly with incoming evacuation platforms (ground and air) to provide en route consultation and to ensure readiness to receive incoming patients. 

- Provides on-site and remote consultation services via digital means when available. 

- Provides medical and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Operating Room and Central Materiel Services Section** 

###### 2-49. The OR and central materiel services (CMS) section— 

- Provides supervision of the OR and CMS. 

- Schedules nursing staff, prepares and maintains the OR and CMS, and maintains surgical and nursing standards within these areas. 

- Works with the anesthesia section to perform initial surgery for battle and nonbattle injuries and follow-on surgery for patients received from other MTFs. 

- Provides general, orthopedic, and obstetrics-gynecological surgical services with two OR tables for a total of 36 hours of table time per day. An additional surgical table is available in the radiology section and can be utilized to increase the total OR capacity, if necessary. 

- Projects OR schedules and determines OR surgical backlog in terms of projected hours to complete each surgery and numbers of patients. 

- Provides records and reports to the commander. The staff reads from and inputs to the automated clinical record using available information systems. 

- Functions with the SAD (24-bed), OR, and CMS as one hospital center surgical service when consolidated. 

- Provides OR/CMS services surgical and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Anesthesia Service Section** 

###### 2-50. The anesthesia service section— 

- Provides anesthesia and respiratory services for the hospital. 

- Provides supervision and administration of anesthetics to patients undergoing surgery. 

- Provides clinical validation of medical equipment and supply sets for each mission. 

- Reviews the readiness of clinical portion of the tactical standard operating procedures (TACSOP). 

- Reviews the proficiency of area of concentration 66F and military occupational specialty 68V to execute the mission of the section. 

- Coordinates with and assists the EMT section in trauma care services. When consolidated, it functions with the hospital augmentation detachment (surgical 24-bed), anesthesia, and respiratory services as one service. 

- Provides anesthesia services medical and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Nursing Service Section** 

###### 2-51. Nursing service section— 

- Manages daily operations of nursing services throughout the hospital. 

- Compiles daily surgical and medical statistical data, prepares hospitalization and bed reports, and forwards to operations section (Chapter 1Appendix A). 

- Compiles daily surgical, medical, ancillary services, and clinical logistics requirements and forward to the operations section. 

- Provides scheduling and supervision of nursing staff to include preparation and coordination of duty rosters, emergency MASCAL plans, and contingency staffing. 

- Plans, organizes, executes, and directs nursing care practices and activities of the hospital. 

- Ensures training and readiness for deployment of nurses and other medical personnel as required. 

- • Provides clinical validation of medical devices and medical material sets of the hospital for each mission, the readiness of clinical portion of the TACSOP, and the proficiency of nurse officers and enlisted nursing personnel to execute the mission of the hospital. 

- Plans, coordinates, and supervises the layout and design of the clinical aspects of the physical facilities. 

###### **Intensive Care Unit** 

- 2-52. Intensive care unit— 

 - Provides one 12-bed ICU for critically WII patients requiring the most intensive monitoring and care. 

 - Manages surgical and medical patients, adult and pediatric, whose physiological status is so disrupted that they require immediate and continuous medical and nursing care. 

 - Provides preoperative stabilization area and postanesthesia recovery area for patients either awaiting surgery or recovering from surgery. 

 - Provides daily ICU logistics requirements and bed status to the nursing services section. 

**_Note._** Of the 12 beds, only 10 are resourced with all the requisite capabilities of a fully functioning ICU bed (nursing staff, intravenous pumps, and ventilators). The remaining two beds can be and are typically used as transition beds for patients awaiting evacuation, for patients that are too complex for the ICW, or simply as overflow beds. 

###### **Intermediate Care Ward** 

- 2-53. Intermediate care ward— 

 - Provides one 20-bed ICW. 

 - Manages surgical or medical patients whose conditions require observation for real or potential life-threatening disease or injury. The acuity of care may range from those requiring constant observation to those patients able to ambulate and begin to assume responsibility for their care. 

 - Provides ICW daily logistics requirements and bed status to the nursing services section. 

**_Note._** The level of care and acuity of the ICW patients may fluctuate and, although not routine, ICW patients may require monitoring devices and ventilator support. 

###### **Pharmacy Section** 

- 2-54. Pharmacy section— 

 - Develops, coordinates, and executes programs and policies ensuring safe and appropriate medication use within the hospital. 

 - Develops, maintains, and publishes the approved hospital formulary; screens all medication orders for drug-drug, drug-nutrient interactions, or medication allergies. 

 - Monitors individual medication therapies for safe and appropriate disease state management and recommends alternative drug regimens to meet situational requirements. 

 - Monitors and reports on all medication-related patient safety problems. 

 - Provides consultation services to medical and MEDLOG staff. 

 - Monitors and enforces hospital-wide quality control of pharmaceuticals. 

 - Provides outpatient pharmacy services. 

 - Provides inpatient pharmacy services, including sterile products preparation services. 

 - Provides drug and medication information services and provides bulk drug and controlled substance distribution support for patient care areas. 

- Provides direct patient care services and provides pharmacy supply and support services. 

- Provides and exercises appropriate control and accountability for all controlled substances and rosters with signature documentation for all individuals approved by the hospital commander to prescribe, receive, order, or distribute controlled drugs. 

- Provides outpatient medications for the required number of days to complete therapy or the supply of medications required for MEDEVAC out of the AO. 

- Provides pharmacy daily logistics requirements and pharmaceutical statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Laboratory Services and Blood Bank Section** 

2-55. Laboratory services and blood bank section— 

- Performs analytical procedures in hematology, urinalysis, chemistry, blood banking, and limited basic microbiology screening. Analytical microbiology capability is available with attachment of the hospital augmentation detachment (surgical 24-bed) and the hospital augmentation detachment (medical 32-bed). 

- Coordinates with environmental laboratories for data regarding potential toxic exposures from the operating environment. 

- Provides blood banking services, including all routine blood grouping and typing, abbreviated cross-matching procedures, emergency blood collection, and blood inventory management. 

- Provides storage and issues liquid and frozen blood products. 

- Coordinates with the supply and services section and directly with the medical detachment (blood support) and with the blood program office for blood supply and resupply requirements. 

- Provides automated records and reports of current and projected blood status to the commander and higher headquarters. 

- Provides daily laboratory and blood bank daily logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Radiology Section** 

2-56. Radiology section provides radiological services to all areas of the hospital and operates on a 24-hour basis. This includes triage and pre-operative. The radiology section will provide daily radiology logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Computerized Tomography Section** 

2-57. Computerized tomography (CT) section— 

- Provides radiological services to all areas of the hospital. 

- Provides the ability to support CT services for all areas of the hospital and operates on a 24-hour basis. 

- Provides daily CT logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Hospital Ministry Team** 

2-58. Hospital ministry team, provides religious support and pastoral care ministry for patients, assigned staff, and subordinate organizations. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

2-59. The field hospital (32-bed) is designed to be employed with the HHD, hospital center. It provides ("down and in") C2 for the field hospital (32-bed) and attached augmentation elements concentrated on Role 3 hospitalization and outpatient services in an assigned AO. 

2-60. Based on the orders process, two field hospitals may be collocated in one location under the C2 of an HHD, hospital center. When employed in a split-based configuration one field hospital will remain with the HHD, hospital center while the other field hospital may be forward deployed to another location, as required. See Chapter 3 and Appendix B for more information on the employment and planning of the field hospital (32-bed). 

##### **BASIS OF ALLOCATION** 

2-61. Hospital commanders and staffs use the BOA below with the PAR and orders process when planning for the field hospital (32-bed): 

- Phase I-III major combat operations (MCO) for brigade and echelons above brigade (EAB) hospital direct admissions per 100 occupied beds: 

 - 1.11 field hospitals/100 wounded in action (WIA). 

 - 0.66 field hospitals/100 disease and non-battle injury (DNBI). 

 - 0.39 field hospitals/100 nerve. 

 - 0.82 field hospitals/100 blister. 

- For theater direct admissions per 100 occupied beds: 

 - 0.82 field hospitals/100 WIA. 

 - 0.45 field hospitals/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.48 field hospitals/100 nerve. 

 - 0.47 field hospitals/100 blister. 

- For theater hospital transfer admissions per 100 occupied beds: 

 - 0.77 field hospitals/100 WIA. 

 - 0.31 field hospitals/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.93 field hospitals/100 nerve. 

 - 0.13 field hospitals/100 blister. 

- For theater hospital transfer admissions with skip: 

 - 0.69 field hospitals/100 WIA. 

 - 0.21 field hospitals/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.92 field hospitals/100 nerve. 

 - 0.92 field hospitals/100 blister. 

- Phase IV (Stabilize) Apply MCO rule for U.S. supported forces and add for directed support for HN PAR at one field hospital (32-bed) per 50,000. 

- Phase V (Enable Civil Authority) BOA adjustments will be based on roles of care directed and PAR supported. 

**_Note_** . See Appendix B of this publication for additional information referencing the unit’s BOA. 

##### **MOBILITY** 

2-62. This unit requires 35 percent of its TOE equipment to be transported in a single lift using authorized organic vehicles and HHD, hospital center transportation section vehicles driven by field hospital (32-bed) personnel. 

##### **<mark>SECTION IV – HOSPITAL AUGMENTATION DETACHMENT (SURGICAL 24BED)</mark>** 

2-63. This section provides a discussion of the hospital augmentation detachment (24-bed) (referred to as SAD [24-bed] or SAD) mission, assignments and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment. 

#### **MISSION** 

2-64. The SAD provides augmentation to the capabilities of the field hospital (32-bed) with thoracic, urology, oral maxillofacial surgical capabilities, 24 additional ICU beds, outpatient services, and microbiology. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

2-65. See the field hospital (32-bed) section for descriptions of what an assignment and dependency mean. The SAD has many dependencies due to its task organization and mission. 

2-66. The SAD in coordination with hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) leaders should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I and add additional dependencies in the unit TACSOP as required and describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how the units will support the SAD. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

2-67. Assigned to the HHD, hospital center and further attached to the field hospital (32-bed). Once attached to the field hospital (32-bed), this unit loses much of its own autonomy and identity. It is intended to augment a Role 3 hospital that already possesses much of its capabilities and therefore, gets absorbed into the field hospital’s (32-bed) medical and surgical structure. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

2-68. This unit is dependent upon— 

- Appropriate elements within AO for religious; legal; AHS support; finance; personnel; administrative and logistical services; mortuary affairs; security of EPWs, retained, and detained patients and U.S. prisoner patients, transportation services when a single lift of the unit is required; vehicle recovery operations; and transportation and equipping for RTD personnel (to include individual clothing and equipment, seasonal outer garments, chemical protection garments, and shower and laundry services not related to patient care). 

- The composite supply company for Class I, II, III and VII supplies and to provide potable water and unclassified map support. 

- Engineer assets to construct medical shelter platforms. 

- The prime power battalion to generate electrical power and to provide advice and technical assistance for electrical power and distribution. 

- The expeditionary signal company to provide automatic switching services for both analog and digital voice and data traffic, tactical multichannel high-capacity transmission systems, and multichannel satellite ground terminals. 

- The casualty liaison team for accurate and timely casualty information, facilitates real-time casualty information for commanders, and assists in the management of casualty operations as needed. 

- Field Hospital (32-Bed), for feeding of hospital staff and patients. 

- The MEDBDE S-3 PM branch or U.S. Air Force aeromedical evacuation liaison team for coordinating intratheater and intertheater PM to higher roles of care, generally out of the AO. 

- The medical company, (ground ambulance) to provide ground PM support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The medical company, (air ambulance) to provide intratheater aeromedical evacuation support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The MEDLOG company for Class VIII support. Augmentation of personnel for medical device maintenance, as required. 

#### **CAPABILITIES AND FUNCTIONS** 

2-69. The unit capabilities and functions depict what the SAD (24-bed) is capable of accomplishing. Functions are descriptions of what each subcomponent of the SAD (24-bed) is responsible for in support of the overall mission. Due to assigned missions, if there are additional capabilities or functions then the SAD (24-bed) should add them to their TACSOP along with prescriptive verbiage for each capability and function. 

##### **CAPABILITIES** 

2-70. This SAD provides— 

- Augmentation of surgical capability for thoracic, urology, and oral maxillofacial surgery based on two OR tables for a total of 36 operating table hours per day. 

- Augmentation of hospitalization with up to 24 patients consisting of two wards providing intensive care nursing. 

- Consultation and outpatient clinic services for patients referred from other MTFs. 

- Behavioral health, public health nursing, and physical therapy services. 

- Three-day supply of basic load within identified MMS in all work areas and deployed assemblages. 

- Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

##### **FUNCTIONS** 

2-71. The SAD has seven elements that comprise the detachment. They are: 

- Detachment Headquarters. 

- Supply and Services Section. 

- Operating Room and Central Materiel Services Section. 

- Anesthesia Service Section. 

- Intensive Care Units A and B. 

- Specialty Clinics Section. 

- Microbiology Section. 

2-72. The SAD is designed to augment the field hospital (32-bed). The following information describes the functions of each element specified in the section I of the TOE. This also describes the task and purpose of each section. Figure 2-3 on page 40 depicts the task organization of the SAD. 

<!-- Start of picture text -->
Legend:<br>CMS centralized material service OR operating room<br>IC intensive care<br><!-- End of picture text -->

**Figure 2-3. Task organization of the surgical augmentation detachment (24-bed)** 

###### **Detachment Headquarters** 

2-73. The detachment headquarters may augment the HHD, hospital center or field hospital (32-bed) with additional personnel to assist with administrative oversight of personnel and equipment. However, the personnel in the detachment’s headquarters primarily have patient care responsibilities once the detachment is integrated in the hospital operations and may not have the ability to focus on administrative or C2 responsibilities. 

###### **Supply and Service Section** 

2-74. The supply and services section augments the supply and services functions within the field hospital (32-bed) to increase the hospital logistics capabilities. 

###### **Operating Room and Central Materiel Services Section** 

2-75. The OR and CMS section— 

- Provide resources to expand the operative capacities of the field hospital (32-bed) with two OR tables and staff for 36 operating table hours per day. 

- Provide sterilization and operator maintenance of equipment. 

- Provide augmentation to the field hospital (32-bed) OR and CMS and, when combined, function as one surgical service. 

###### **Anesthesia Service Section** 

- 2-76. Anesthesia service section— 

 - Incrementally expands the anesthesia and respiratory service capacities of the hospital. 

 - Provides supervision and administration of anesthetics to patients undergoing surgery. 

 - Provides augmentation to the field hospital (32-bed) OR and CMS and, when combined, function as one service. 

###### **Intensive Care Unit** 

- 2-77. Intensive care unit alpha— 

 - Incrementally expands the capacity of the hospital to care for critically WII patients. 

 - Provides nursing care for those patients who require close observation and vital sign monitoring, complex nursing care, and mechanical respiratory assistance. 

 - Serves as preoperative stabilization and postanesthesia recovery area. 

- 2-78. Intensive care unit bravo— 

 - Incrementally expands the capacity of the hospital to care for critically WII patients. 

 - Provides nursing care for those patients who require close observation and vital sign monitoring, complex nursing care, and mechanical respiratory assistance. 

 - Serves as preoperative stabilization and postanesthesia recovery area. 

###### **Specialty Clinics Section** 

- 2-79. Specialty clinics section provides— 

 - Ambulatory care expansion capabilities of the field hospital (32-bed) to primary care, family practice, and behavioral health. 

 - Patient care and family medicine consultation services, evaluation and treatment of dermatological and gynecological diseases, injuries, disorders, orthopedic and physical therapy services. 

 - Outpatient behavioral health and inpatient behavioral health consultation and education services. 

 - • Obstetrics-gynecology clinic with the basic medical supplies and equipment necessary to evaluate, diagnose, and clinically manage routine patient complaints related to the female reproductive system. 

 - Orthopedic clinic with the basic medical supplies and equipment necessary to evaluate, diagnose and clinically manage musculoskeletal conditions, to include mobile cast capability. 

 - Supplies and equipment to conduct primary care (sick call) operations and comprehensive routine medical care to include electrocardiographs in the medical services clinic. 

 - Physical-occupational clinic to evaluate and treat neuromusculoskeletal injuries, minor soft tissue wounds to include burn wound treatment, behavioral health, injury prevention, and human performance optimization. 

 - Provides daily specialty clinics logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

###### **Microbiology Section** 

2-80. Microbiology section provides additional capability to the field hospital (32-bed) to accomplish aerobic and anaerobic cultures, limited parasitology, and antibiotic susceptibility testing. This includes enhanced microbiology testing such as culture and sensitivity, gram staining, rapid infectious disease diagnostics and special staining. The microbiology section provides daily logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

2-81. The SAD is not designed as a standalone unit and will be employed with and further attached and deployed within the field hospital (32-bed) assigned AO. The SAD provides additional surgical capabilities, 24 ICU beds, an outpatient clinic, and additional microbiology services to the field hospital (32-bed). 

2-82. Based on the orders process, a SAD may be attached to a field hospital (32-bed). See Chapter 3 and Appendix B of this publication for more information on the employment and planning of the SAD (24-bed). 

##### **BASIS OF ALLOCATION** 

2-83. Hospital commanders, staffs, and medical planners use the BOA below with the PAR and orders process when planning for the SAD: 

- Phase I-III MCO for brigade and EAB hospital direct admissions per 100 occupied beds:  1.11 SADs/100 WIA. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.20 SADs/100 nerve. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 blister. 

- For theater direct admissions per 100 occupied beds:  0.75 SADs/100 WIA.  0.00 SADs/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 nerve. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 blister. 

- For theater hospital transfer admissions per 100 occupied beds:  0.57 SADs/100 WIA.  0.00 SADs/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 nerve. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 blister. 

- For theater hospital transfer admissions with skip:  0.38 SADs/100 WIA. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 DNBI. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 nerve. 

 - 0.00 SADs/100 blister. 

- Phase IV (Stabilize) Apply MCO rule for U.S. supported forces. Model did not earn a SAD for HN PAR. All requirements for surgical were earned by the field hospital. 

- Phase V (Enable Civil Authority) BOA adjustments will be based on roles of care directed and PAR supported. 

**_Note_** . See Appendix B of this publication for additional information referencing the unit’s BOA. 

##### **MOBILITY** 

2-84. This unit has no organic vehicles and relies on external assets for mobility. 

##### **SECTION V – HOSPITAL AUGMENTATION DETACHMENT (MEDICAL 32-BED)** 

2-85. This section provides a discussion of the hospital augmentation detachment (32-bed) (referred to as MAD [32-bed] or MAD) mission, assignments and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment. 

#### **MISSION** 

2-86. The MAD provides augmentation the field hospital (32-bed) with one additional ICU ward (12 beds), one ICW (20 beds), outpatient services, operational dental care, and additional microbiology capabilities within a field hospital (32-bed) assigned AO. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

2-87. See the field hospital (32-bed) section for descriptions of what an assignment and dependency mean. Like the SAD, the MAD has many dependencies due to its task organization and mission. 

2-88. The MAD in coordination with hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) leaders should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I and add additional dependencies in the unit TACSOP as required and describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how these units will support the MAD. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

2-89. The detachment is assigned to the HHD, hospital center and further attached to the field hospital (32bed). Once attached to the field hospital (32-bed), this unit loses much of its own autonomy and identity. It is intended to augment a hospital that already possesses much of its capabilities and therefore, gets absorbed into the field hospital (32-bed) operations. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

2-90. This unit is dependent upon the following: 

- Appropriate elements within theater army for religious; legal; AHS support; finance; personnel; administrative and logistical services; mortuary affairs; security of EPW patients and U.S. prisoner patients; transportation services when a single lift of the unit is required; vehicle recovery operations; and transportation and equipping for RTD personnel (to include individual clothing and equipment, seasonal outer garments, chemical protection garments, and shower and laundry services not related to patient care). 

- The composite supply company for Class I, II, III and VII supplies and to provide potable water and unclassified map support. 

- The horizontal construction company to construct medical shelter platforms. 

- The prime power battalion to generate electrical power and to provide advice and technical assistance for electrical power and distribution. 

- The expeditionary signal company to provide automatic switching services for both analog and digital voice and data traffic, tactical multichannel high-capacity transmission systems, and multichannel satellite ground terminals. 

- The casualty liaison team for accurate and timely casualty information, facilitates real-time casualty information for commanders, and assists in the management of casualty operations as needed. 

- Field Hospital (32-Bed), for feeding of hospital staff and patients. 

- The MEDBDE S-3 PM branch or U.S. Air Force aeromedical evacuation liaison team for coordinating intratheater and intertheater PM to higher roles of care, generally out of the AO. 

- The medical company, (ground ambulance) to provide ground PM support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The medical company, (air ambulance) to provide intratheater aeromedical evacuation support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- The MEDLOG company for Class VIII support. Augmentation of personnel for medical device maintenance, as required. 

#### **CAPABILITIES AND FUNCTIONS** 

2-91. The unit capabilities and functions depict what the MAD is capable of accomplishing. Functions are descriptions of what each subcomponent of the MAD is responsible for in support of the overall mission. Due to assigned missions, if there are additional capabilities or functions then the MAD should add them to their TACSOP along with prescriptive verbiage for each capability and function. 

##### **CAPABILITIES** 

###### 2-92. The MAD provides— 

- Augmentation to the field hospital (32-bed) for up to 32 patients consisting of one ward providing intensive care nursing for up to 12 patients, requiring the most intensive monitoring and care, and one ward providing intermediate care nursing for up to 20 patients. 

- Augmentation to the specialty clinic of the hospital with consultation and outpatient clinic services for patients referred from other MTFs. 

- Augmentation to the specialty clinic with additional behavioral health, public health nursing, and physical therapy capabilities. 

- Operational dental care consisting of emergency dental care and essential dental care designed to circumvent potential dental emergencies. 

- Augmentation to the field hospital (32-bed) with additional personnel for patient administration, logistical, and nutritional care services. 

- Three days of supply within all sections and MMS. 

- Augmentation to the maintenance capabilities of the field hospital (32-bed). 

- Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

##### **FUNCTIONS** 

2-93. The MAD has nine elements that comprise the detachment. They are: 

- Detachment Headquarters. 

- Nutrition Care Section. 

- Supply and Services Section. 

- Anesthesia Service Section. 

- Intensive Care Unit. 

- Intermediate Care Ward. 

- Specialty Clinics Section. 

- Dental Section. 

- Microbiology Section. 

2-94. The MAD is designed to augment the field hospital (32-bed). The following information describes the functions of each element specified in the section I of the TOE. This also describes the task and purpose of each section. Figure 2-4 on page 45 depicts the task organization of the MAD.

###### **Detachment Headquarters**

The officer in charge and noncommissioned officer (NCO) in charge functions as the clinical head nurse and ward master when attached to field hospital (32-bed) and therefore, are primarily focused on patient care once integrated into the hospital.

2-96. The nutrition care section augments the field hospital’s (32-bed) nutrition care section providing nutrition services. See the field hospital (32-bed) section of this publication for more information on the nutrition care section and Appendix B for nutrition care operations.

2-97. The supply and services section augments the supply and services section of the field hospital (32-bed) with additional personnel to expand the logistical support capability and capacity.

2-98. Anesthesia service section—

- Provides supervision and administration of anesthetics to patients undergoing surgery. 

###### **Intensive Care Unit** 

2-99. The ICU— 

- Provides augmentation to the field hospital (32-bed) with one 12 bed ICU providing for critically WII patients. 

- Manages surgical and medical patients, adult and pediatric patients whose physiological state is so disrupted that they require immediate and continuous medical and nursing care. 

- Provides preoperative stabilization area and postanesthesia recovery area for patients either awaiting surgery or recovering from surgery.

2-100. The ICW augments the field hospital (32-bed) with one 20 bed ICW managing surgical or medical patients whose conditions require observation for real or potential life-threatening disease or injury. The acuity of care may range from those requiring constant observation to those patients able to ambulate and begin to assume responsibility for their care. The level of care and acuity of these patients may fluctuate depending on the intensity of their wounds, illness, or injury. Although not routine, ICW patients may require monitoring devices and ventilator support. 

###### **Specialty Clinics Section** 

2-101. The specialty clinics section provides— 

- Ambulatory care expansion capabilities of the field hospital (32-bed) for primary care, family practice and behavioral health. 

- Patient care and family medicine consultation services, evaluation and treatment of dermatological and gynecological diseases, injuries, disorders, orthopedic, and physical therapy services.

- • Obstetrics-gynecology clinic with the basic medical supplies and equipment necessary to evaluate, diagnose, and clinically manage routine patient complaints related to the female reproductive system.

###### **Dental Section**

###### **Microbiology Section**

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

2-104. The MAD is not designed as a standalone unit and will be employed with and further attached and deployed with the field hospital (32-bed) assigned AO. ##### **<mark>SECTION VI – HOSPITAL AUGMENTATION DETACHMENT (INTERMEDIATE CARE WARD 60-BED)</mark>** 

2-108. This section provides a discussion of the hospital augmentation detachment intermediate care ward (60-bed) (referred to as ICWAD [60-bed] or ICWAD) mission, assignments and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment. 

#### **MISSION** 

2-109. The ICWAD provides augmentation the field hospital (32-bed) bed capacities with three 20 bed ICWs and provide intermediate nursing care and additional personnel to support nutrition and patient administration capabilities. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

2-110. See the field hospital (32-bed) section for descriptions of what an assignment and dependency mean. Like other hospital augmentation detachments, the ICWAD has many dependencies due to its task organization and mission. 

2-111. The ICWAD in coordination with hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) leaders should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I and add additional dependencies in the unit TACSOP as required and describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how these units will support the ICWAD. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

2-112. The ICWAD is assigned to the HHD, hospital center and further attached to the field hospital (32bed). Once attached to the field hospital (32-bed), this unit loses much of its own autonomy and identity. It is intended to augment a hospital that already possesses much of its capabilities and therefore, gets absorbed into the field hospital (32-bed) operations. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

- 2-113. The ICWAD is dependent upon the following: 

 - Appropriate elements within theater army for religious; legal; AHS support; finance; personnel; administrative and logistical services; mortuary affairs; security of EPW patients and U.S. prisoner patients; transportation services when single lift requirements exceed unit capability; vehicle recovery operations; and transportation and equipping for RTD personnel (to include individual clothing and equipment, seasonal outer garments, chemical protection garments, and shower and laundry services not related to patient care). 

 - The composite supply company for Class I, II, III and VII supplies and to provide potable water and unclassified map support. 

 - The horizontal construction company to construct medical shelter platforms. 

 - The prime power battalion to generate electrical power and to provide advice and technical assistance for electrical power and distribution. 

 - The expeditionary signal company to provide automatic switching services for both analog and digital voice and data traffic, tactical multichannel high-capacity transmission systems, and multichannel satellite ground terminals. 

 - The casualty liaison team for accurate and timely casualty information, facilitates real-time casualty information for commanders, and assists in the management of casualty operations as needed. 

 - Field Hospital (32-bed), for feeding of hospital staff and patients. 

 - The MEDBDE S-3 PM branch or U.S. Air Force aeromedical evacuation liaison team for coordinating intratheater and intertheater PM to higher roles of care, generally out of the AO. 

 - The medical company, (ground ambulance) to provide ground PM support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

 - The medical company, (air ambulance) to provide intratheater aeromedical evacuation support to and between roles of care within the AO, transportation of emergency Class VIII resupply, and movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

 - The MEDLOG company for Class VIII support. Augmentation of personnel for medical device maintenance, as required. 

#### **CAPABILITIES AND FUNCTIONS** 

2-114. The unit capabilities and functions depict what the ICWAD is capable of accomplishing. Functions are descriptions of what each subcomponent of the ICWAD is responsible for in support of the overall mission. Due to assigned missions, if there are additional capabilities or functions then the ICWAD should add them to their TACSOP along with prescriptive verbiage for each capability and function. 

##### **CAPABILITIES** 

###### 2-115. The ICWAD provides— 

- Hospitalization for up to 60 patients consisting of three wards providing intermediate nursing care. 

- Augmentation to the patient administration and nutrition care sections. 

- Limited maintenance on organic equipment. 

- Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

##### **FUNCTIONS** 

2-116. The ICWAD has four elements that comprise the detachment. They are: 

- Detachment Headquarters. 

- Nutrition Care Section. 

- Patient Administration Section. 

- Intermediate Care Ward. 

2-117. The ICWAD is designed to augment the field hospital (32-bed). The following information describes the functions of each element specified in the section I of the TOE. This also describes the task and purpose of each section. Figure 2-5 depicts the task organization of the ICWAD. 

**Figure 2-5. Task organization of the intermediate care ward augmentation detachment (60bed)** 

###### **Detachment Headquarters** 

2-118. Detachment headquarters may augment the HHD, hospital center or field hospital (32-bed) with additional personnel to increase C2 capabilities. However, the officer in charge and NCO in charge are medical providers in the ICW, absorbed in patient care, and may not be able to primarily focus on administrative or C2 responsibilities. 

###### **Nutrition Care Section** 

2-119. The nutrition care section augments the field hospital (32-bed) with additional personnel to increase nutrition services. Refer to the field hospital (32-bed) section of this publication for more information on the nutrition care section and Appendix B for nutrition care operations. 

###### **Patient Administration Section** 

2-120. The patient administration section augments the hospital with additional personnel to increase patient administration services. See the field hospital (32-bed) section and Appendix B of this publication for more information on the patient administration section and PM. 

###### **Intermediate Care Ward** 

2-121. The ICW augments the hospital with three ICWs to increase the intermediate nursing care by managing surgical or medical patients whose conditions require observation for real or potential lifethreatening disease or injury. The acuity of care may range from those requiring constant observation to those patients able to ambulate and begin to assume responsibility for their own care. The acuity of care of these patients may fluctuate depending on the intensity of their wounds, illness, or injury. Although not routine, ICW patients may require monitoring devices and ventilator support. The ICWs provides daily logistics requirements and statistical data to the nursing services section. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

2-122. The ICWAD is not designed as a standalone unit and will be employed with and further attached and deployed with the field hospital (32-bed) assigned AO. The ICWAD provides increased ICW bed augmentation to the field hospital (32-bed) capacities and further augments patient administration and nutrition care sections. 

2-123. Based on the orders process, PAR, and BOA up to two (2) ICWADs may be attached to one or a combination of two field hospital (32-bed). Normally the hospital center in support of the division will not receive an ICWAD as the main mission of a Role 3 hospital in the division AO is to perform surgeries to stabilize patients for further evacuation out of the division AO to ensure freedom of movement and maneuver. See Chapter 3 and Appendix B of this publication for more information on the employment and planning of the ICWAD (60-bed). 

##### **BASIS OF ALLOCATION** 

2-124. Hospital commanders, staffs, and medical planners use the BOA below with the PAR and orders process when planning for the ICWAD: 

- Phase I-III MCO for brigade and EAB hospital direct admissions per 100 occupied beds:  0.61 ICWs/100 WIA. 

 - 0.66 ICWs /100 DNBI. 

 - 0.39 ICWs /100 nerve. 

 - 0.82 ICWs /100 blister. 

- For theater direct admissions per 100 occupied beds:  0.82 ICWs /100 WIA. 

 - 0.45 ICWs /100 DNBI. 

 - 0.48 ICWs /100 nerve. 

 - 0.47 ICWs /100 blister. 

- For theater hospital transfer admissions per 100 occupied beds: 

 - 0.77 ICWs /100 WIA. 

 - 0.31 ICWs /100 DNBI. 

 - 0.93 ICWs /100 nerve. 

 - 0.13 ICWs /100 blister. 

- For theater hospital transfer admissions with skip: 

 - 0.69 ICWs /100 WIA. 

 - 0.21 ICWs /100 DNBI. 

 - 0.92 ICWs /100 nerve. 

 - 0.92 ICWs /100 blister. 

- Phase IV (Stabilize) Apply MCO rule for U.S. supported forces and add for directed support for HN PAR at one (1) ICWAD per 50,000. 

- Phase V (Enable Civil Authority) BOA adjustments will be based on roles of care directed and PAR supported. 

**_Note._** See Appendix B of this publication for additional information referencing the unit’s BOA. 

##### **MOBILITY** 

This unit has no organic vehicles and relies on external assets for mobility. 

This page intentionally left blank. 

##### **Chapter 3** 

### **Employment of Hospitalization Organizations** 

This chapter discusses topics that are applicable to all hospitalization units, regardless of organizational structure. It includes information on the tactical employment of the Role 3 hospital and specialized augmentation detachments and teams. Leaders should understand how the operational variables and the mission variables, may impact the employment the hospital. 

##### **SECTION I – CONCEPT OF EMPLOYMENT** 

3-1. This section provides an overall discussion of the employment of the hospital center and examples of how it can be configured, depending on the required bed capacity and medical capabilities. It describes early entry planning, the use of specialty organizations, and hospital configurations. 

#### **HOSPITAL CENTER COMPONENTS** 

3-2. The HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) should be deployed and employed together as the smallest element to provide Role 3 hospitalization. When referring to the organization (or at least the HHD and one or more of the subordinate units), it is called the hospital center and not the field hospital. The field hospital is the 32-bed subordinate unit that is one piece of the hospital center. The two do not necessarily need to be collocated upon initial employment, but the HHD, hospital center possesses C2, logistics, maintenance, and equipment capabilities that make the employment of both units together much more effective and efficient. Further, the field hospital (32-bed) is not designed to be split between two locations. A requirement to provide all the Role 3 capabilities of a field hospital (32-bed) in two locations cannot be accomplished by one. Therefore, a second field hospital (32-bed) would be required and one HHD, hospital center can provide C2 for both. 

##### **HEADQUARTERS AND HEADQUARTERS DETACHMENT, HOSPITAL CENTER** 

3-3. The HHD, hospital center provides C2 for up to two functioning field hospitals (32-bed) as well as additional medical augmentation detachments with a combined maximum of 240 beds. The hospital center’s modular design has the capability to provide differing bed quantities and types of beds as well as surgical and medical augmentation to project a hospital structure that can support variable medical mission requirements. 

3-4. Split-based operations covering two locations present unique hospitalization planning requirements. The distance between separated hospital elements as well as requisite surgical and patient bed requirements for each location have a significant impact on the planning and employment process. Medical planners may want to consider employing two HHD, hospital centers if split-based operations significantly impede C2 capabilities. 

3-5. The HHD, hospital center and its individual subordinate elements are not designed to be broken apart. Hospital commanders and medical planners should take this into consideration when deciding to split a Role 3 hospital to support two locations. The following examples describe how a HHD can provide C2 for two distinct subordinate units operating in two separate locations. The hospital center will remain at one location with one field hospital while the other field hospital forward deploys but remains under the C2 of the HHD, hospital center. If the Role 3 hospital center is supporting a division or corps in the offense, it can assume risk by only establishing 208 beds, leaving one field hospital (32-bed) packed up and ready to move in support of the offense. Once the order comes to move, the packed-up field hospital moves to the new location and complexes. Once operational the 208 bed Role 3 decomplexes and prepares to link up with the forward deployed field hospital. 

##### **FIELD HOSPITAL (32-BED)** 

3-6. The field hospital (32-bed) is the cornerstone of the deployed hospital. It represents the smallest hospital element with complete requisite clinical capabilities of a Role 3 MTF. This hospital is designed to be selfsupporting while remaining transportable and expandable. Combinations of the modular hospital units are suitable in providing hospitalization across the range of military operations. It is the basis for any combinations of capabilities (bed types/numbers and functions). The other augmentation detachments discussed are not designed to be autonomous in a deployed scenario; they are intended to augment and complement the field hospital (32-bed). 

3-7. The field hospital (32-bed) can provide complete Role 3 hospitalization under the C2 of the HHD, hospital center with a 72-hour basic load of medical and nonmedical supplies. Hospitalization is provided for up to 32 patients consisting of one ward with intensive care nursing for up to 12 patients (10 beds are fully equipped for patients requiring the most intensive monitoring or care) and one ward providing intermediate care nursing for up to 20 patients, emergency treatment to receive, triage, and prepare incoming patients for surgery, surgical capability consisting of general, orthopedic, and obstetrical-gynecological surgery based on two operating tables staffed for 36 operating table hours per day. An additional 36 hours of surgical capacity is available by utilizing the remaining 12-hour capacity from the original two OR tables and an additional 24-hour capacity from an OR table established in the x-ray section. This additional surgical capacity was designed to facilitate augmentation by surgical teams and detachments as well as to accommodate temporary peaks in surgical requirements. The hospital also includes pharmacy and clinical laboratory services with limited basic microbiology screening, blood banking, CT and radiology services, personnel administration, patient administration, logistical, nutritional care services, and a hospital ministry team for hospital staff and patients. 

3-8. The field hospital (32-bed), as an individual unit and is not designed to perform split operations. If the capabilities of the field hospital (32-bed) are required in two separate geographical locations, then two field hospitals (32-bed) and requisite hospital augmentation detachments are required to support the mission. 

##### **HOSPITAL AUGMENTATION DETACHMENTS** 

3-9. Hospital augmentation detachments and teams may be attached to the field hospital (32-bed) to increase its bed, surgical, staffing, and medical specialty capabilities. These capabilities are included through the orders process using BOA and PAR factors to determine the number of units required. 

3-10. Planning for hospitalization is crucial during competition below armed conflict and refined during crisis. Medical planners should refer to the detailed information in Chapter 2 and Appendix B of this publication. 

#### **EARLY ENTRY PLANNING AND OPERATIONS** 

3-11. Commanders may be expected to task-organize portions of the HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) to provide a limited early entry Role 3 capability until the remainder of the field hospital (32-bed), HHD, and augmentation detachments arrive later in the deployment progression. Use of specialty detachments such as the FRSD may be used in early entry operations. 

3-12. There are many ways to task-organize units, or components of units, to establish a light, flexible, and mobile early entry capability. For example, using an FRSD as the base unit for an early entry package provides the DCR and DCS capability. Providing a portion of the hospitalization capabilities from the hospital center, such as ICU beds with requisite equipment, staffing, and vehicles, provides more for the FRSD than what it would normally depend upon when employed with a Role 2 medical company. Finally, adding limited pharmacy, lab, and x-ray to the early entry package meets the minimum Role 3 capabilities while retaining a more expeditionary response than a complete field hospital (32-bed). 

3-13. Commanders and staffs should be prepared to provide a Role 3 capable early entry element, which may not look the same for each mission. Its composition, size, personnel, equipment, and capability may be different based on the uniqueness of the units, mission, and PAR it is required to support. 

3-14. Commanders and planners should anticipate the minimum requirements for an early entry medical package to include DCR, DCS, and inpatient care. For additional planning factors for early entry, see Appendix B. 

#### **HOSPITAL CENTER CONFIGURATIONS** 

3-15. The hospital center has the capability of being deployed in multiple configurations to provide requisite care to any military or foreign humanitarian assistance operation. The hospital center has the capability to perform split-based operations within an AO. The HHD, hospital center has the capability to provide C2 for up to a 240-bed hospital when configured to operate in one or two locations. 

3-16. A Role 3 hospital in support of a corps will look different from one in support of a division. See Appendix B of this publication for figures and tables for examples of hospital center configurations that would support a range of military operations. 

##### **SECTION II – EMPLOYMENT OF ROLE 3 HOSPITALS** 

3-17. This section provides guidance and references for employing medical units and hospitals in an AO. Detailed planning can be found in the references provided. 

#### **EMPLACEMENT** 

3-18. The displacement and reestablishment of a Role 3 hospital is dependent upon the mission and other mission variables. Unit displacements are normally conducted because of orders issued by the higher headquarters. Frequently, the time to respond to orders is short; therefore, the hospital commander should disseminate guidance to the staff in the most expedient method. Upon receiving the commander’s guidance, the staff conducts the mission analysis, incorporating changes based on new information or situation. The hospital saves time by rehearsing moves, using knowledge from experience, and maintaining a detailed TACSOP. 

3-19. The hospital operations section develops the OPORD according to the higher headquarters’ plan and the TACSOP. To save planning time, hospitals should have examples of OPORDs for various types of offense, defense, stability, and defense support of civil authorities’ hospital support missions from across the range of military operations. The hospital commander reviews and approves the OPORD. The hospital commander ensures that the move is coordinated with higher headquarters and all supported elements. All supported elements should be aware of when hospital operations at the current location will be curtailed and the date and time of opening hospital operations at the new site. This can be referred to as an h-hour sequence. Role 3 hospital unit displacements necessitate the transfer of patients and hospital operations to other Role 3 hospitals. To minimize disruption of hospital operations, the hospital should move in echelons. Displacement by echelons is contingent upon the higher commander’s intent, the tactical situation, and the availability of support requirements. See Appendix B of this publication for displacement planning considerations. 

##### **WARNING ORDER** 

3-20. The warning order (WARNORD) is a preliminary notice of an order or action that is to follow (JP 5- 0). It is also a planning directive that initiates the development and evaluation of military courses of action by a commander. Also called WARNORD (JP 5-0). A move is usually initiated by a WARNORD issued by the higher headquarters. The WARNORD serves notice of a contemplated action or order that is to follow. The amount of detail included in a WARNORD depends on the time available, the means of communications, and the information requested by the commander. 

3-21. Upon receiving the WARNORD, the commander analyzes the mission and provides planning guidance to the staff. Using the higher headquarters’ service support annex, status reports, and other appropriate documents, the staff formulates their running estimates for the commander’s approval. With the acceptance and approval of the running estimates, the commander provides a decision and concept of operations. Concurrently with the running estimate sequence, other unit personnel conduct preliminary equipment checks and equipment loading procedures. Based on the commander’s decision, the staff coordinates with the higher headquarters to affect the transfer of patients to other MTFs. 

3-22. In preparation for displacement, the commander should organize the unit into manageable echelons, preserving unit integrity as much as possible. Preparation for displacement requires— 

- Identifying external support requirements (for example, a hospital may require additional materiel handling equipment and security escorts). 

- Phasing down and transferring MTF operations. 

- Evacuating and transferring patients to other MTFs. 

- Performing map, ground, and air reconnaissance of the routes and selecting the new site when possible. 

- Selecting routes. 

- Designating start points and release points. 

- Reconnoitering the route to the starting point. 

- Providing for fuel, security, maintenance, supply, and equipment evacuation. 

- Determining the order (echelons), rate of march, maximum speed of vehicles, catch-up speed, and distance between vehicles. 

- Establishing checkpoints and halts. 

- Establishing communications security procedures. 

- Establishing mission-oriented protective posture-level. 

- Dispatching reconnaissance and advanced parties. 

- Controlling traffic. 

- Planning for environmental considerations, policies, and requirements. 

- Issuing orders. 

##### **OPERATION ORDER** 

3-23. The _operation order_ is a directive issued by a commander to subordinate commanders for the purpose of effecting the coordinated execution of an operation. Also called OPORD. (JP 5-0). The operations officer has staff responsibility for formulating, publishing, and obtaining the commander’s approval of and distributing the OPORD. The OPORD provides unit staff and personnel the information needed to carry out an operation. Preparation of this order normally follows the completion of area reconnaissance and an estimate of the situation. When time is available and the existing tactical situation conditions prevent detailed planning or area reconnaissance, the higher headquarters prepares an initial march plan and issues a fragmentary order to modify these plans as needed. A _fragmentary order_ is an abbreviated operation order issued as needed to change or modify an order or to execute a branch or sequel. Also called FRAGORD (JP 5-0). If conditions and time permit, information in the OPORD includes— 

- External transportation support. 

- Security support. 

- Destination and routes. 

- Rate of march, maximum speeds, and order of march. 

- Start points and times. 

- Scheduled halts, vehicle distances, and release points. 

- Required communications. 

3-24. Each unit section reports its supply, vehicle, equipment, workload, and maintenance status to the operations officer. This information is used in coordination with higher headquarters to finalize the convoy organization, compute additional transportation, security, and additional external support requirements, and perform march computations. Geospatial analysis can provide terrain analysis and environmental analyses in support of operations. Refer to TM 3-34.56/MCRP 3-40B.7 for more information on waste management for deployed forces. 

##### **AREA RECONNAISSANCE** 

3-25. The higher headquarters normally prescribes the reconnaissance route. The operations section uses a map reconnaissance in such cases to confirm checkpoints, identify problem areas, and begin planning positions of the unit in the new area. This effort includes the gathering of data already generated, if any, on the route and the new area that includes specific environmental health risks, environmental considerations (in accordance with ATP 3-34.5/MCRP 3-40B2), and related information. If the route is not prescribed and the unit is not included as part of a reconnaissance party with other units, the operations section briefs the reconnaissance team on the displacement plan and provides the team with a strip map and the designated mission oriented protective posture (MOPP)-level and notifies higher headquarters of the route selected. The composition of the reconnaissance team is directed by the unit commander.

3-26. The reconnaissance party wears the appropriate protective gear based on the threat analysis and monitors all radiological and chemical detection devices. It performs duties to— 

- Verify map information. 

- Note capabilities of road networks. 

- List significant terrain features and potential problem areas. 

- Identify and mark contaminated areas and minefields. 

- Compute travel times and distances. 

- Perform route and ground reconnaissance to include site selection. 

##### **ADVANCE PARTY** 

3-27. The advance party moves before the main body and is dispatched as directed by the commander. Its composition is recommended by the operations officer and approved by the commander. It prepares the new site for arrival of the main body and may provide minimal medical support or surgical if an FRSD is available and not forward deployed with a Role 2 medical company. The advance party performs duties to— 

- Ensure the area is free of enemy activity and monitor radiation exposure measurements within the area of the new site. A security sweep of the new site is normally done by security support forces. 

- • Position chemical alarms. 

- Establish communications with higher headquarters and old location. 

- Designate boundaries of hospital elements, based on unit defense plans and consistent with types of weapons and personnel availability. 

- Increase security by manning key points along the perimeter. 

- Establish a command post. 

- Ensure personnel follow dispersion and other measures. 

- Position personnel to guide main body from the release point to designated locations 

- Provide initial medical or surgical capabilities. 

##### **MAIN BODY** 

3-28. The main body moves as directed in the OPORD. The last echelon normally closes out any remaining operations ensuring the old site is clear of anything that could be of intelligence value to the enemy and then moves to the new site. This echelon includes maintenance elements to deal with disabled vehicles from the rest of the convoy. It also picks up guides and markers along the route. As the main body arrives at the new site, it is met by the advanced party and guided to designated positions. Personnel begin to complex the Role 3 hospital at the new site and the establish operations following the priorities set by the commander. This timeline and priorities are detailed in the hospital’s h-hour sequence for complexing the hospital at the new site and being able to accept patients according to the supported unit’s OPLAN (see Appendix B). 

##### **SITE SELECTION** 

3-29. Site selection is an important factor impacting on the accomplishment of the hospital’s mission. Improper site selection can result in inefficiency and possible danger to unit personnel and patients.
