For example, if there is insufficient space available for setting up the system or for ambulances to turnaround, congestion, and traffic jams around the hospital can result; or, if the area selected does not have proper drainage, heavy rains may cause flooding in hospital areas. The hospital should not be placed near hazardous materials (such as petroleum, oils, lubricants, and ammunition) or storage areas and motor pools. The selected site is cleared of mines, booby traps, and CBRN hazards. The selected site is not located near potential areas of contamination, such as a garbage dump, landfill, or other waste disposal site. 

##### **COMMANDER’S PLAN AND MISSION** 

3-30. The specifics of the operation plan, the way it will be executed, and the hospital’s assigned mission can affect the selection of a site. The requirements for an area that is only to be used for a short period of time can differ significantly from an area expected to be used on an extended basis. For example, if the hospital’s mission requires that it relocates several times a month, the entire hospital and its capabilities will not be established; only essential hospitalization services, shelters, and equipment will be used. On the other hand, if it is anticipated that the hospital will be located at one site for an extended period, the entire hospital may be complexed or buildings and pre-established shelters, if available, may be used.

##### **ROUTES OF EVACUATION AND ACCESSIBILITY** 

3-31. The preferred means of evacuation is by air; the primary means of evacuation is by ground. Whenever the tactical situation permits, URGENT and URGENT-SURGICAL precedence patients should be evacuated by air and ROUTINE and CONVENIENCE precedence patients evacuated by ground. During large-scale combat operations, any combination of air and ground will be used based on mission variables to quickly move patients through the continuum of care. CBRN patients (contaminated) should be evacuated on ground ambulances due to the difficulty and time required to decontaminate an air ambulance. The hospital should be situated so that it is accessible from several different directions and areas. It should be located near and be accessible to main road networks and air corridors, but not placed near lucrative targets of opportunity. The site should not be so secluded that incoming ambulances and casualty evacuation platforms have difficulty locating the hospital. For additional information on MEDEVAC operations refer to ATP 4-02.2. For additional information on casualty evacuation operations refer to ATP 4-02.13. 

##### **EXPECTED AREA OF PATIENT DENSITY** 

3-32. To ensure the timely delivery of AHS support, the Role 3 hospital should be in a location proximate enough to the supported units to provide timely hospitalization support. Without proximity to the areas of patient density, the evacuation routes will be unnecessarily long, resulting in delays in both treatment and evacuation that may result in increased morbidity. The longer the distance that must be traveled, the longer it takes for the patient to reach the next role of care. Further, this time delay reduces the number of ambulances available for medical evacuation support. 

##### **HARDSTAND, DRAINAGE, OBSTACLES, AND SPACE** 

3-33. The site should provide good drainage during inclement weather. Care should be taken to ensure that the site selected is not in or near a dry river or stream bed, has drainage that slopes away from the hospital location, not through the hospital site, and that there are no areas where water can pool. 

3-34. The ground, in the selected area, should be of a hard composition that is not likely to become marshy or excessively muddy during inclement weather or temperature changes. This is particularly true in extreme cold-weather operations where the ground is frozen at night and begins to thaw and become marshy during daylight hours. The area should be able to withstand a heavy traffic flow of incoming and departing ambulances in various types of weather. 

3-35. The area selected should be free of major obstacles that will adversely impact on the unit layout (such as disrupting the traffic pattern); cause difficulties in erecting shelters (overly rocky soil); or require extensive preparation of the area before the hospital can be established. Engineer site preparation is required for the establishment of hospitals. 

3-36. The space to establish the treatment and administrative areas of the hospital is dependent upon the mission, expected duration of the operation, and whether CBRN operations are anticipated. The site should provide adequate space for establishment of all hospital elements including possible augmentation. It should be adequate in size to accommodate dispersion of unit assets according to the TACSOP. The land space required for a hospital varies a great deal, depending on how much of the MTF will be established. For example, one field hospital (32-bed) requires nearly seven acres. These seven acres includes space for the field hospital (32-bed) deployable medical system and adequate space for vehicles, containers, ancillary services, such as field feeding, laundry and bath, water distribution, and sleeping areas. 

##### **COMMUNICATIONS** 

3-37. While considering all factors of site selection, remember that terrain and enemy activity can impede the hospital’s communication systems. 

##### **LIKELY ENEMY TARGETS** 

3-38. The site should not be too closely located to likely enemy targets including— 

- Ammunition storage facilities. 

- Petroleum, oils, and lubricants points. 

- Motor pools. 

- Main supply routes (the facility should be in the vicinity of the main supply route for accessibility but not directly on it). 

- Bridges. 

- River crossing points. 

- Strategic towns and cities. 

- Industrial complexes and factories. 

##### **COVER AND CONCEALMENT** 

3-39. The area should provide maximum cover and concealment without hampering mission accomplishment or communications capability. Overhead cover is desirable for protection from biological and chemical warfare agent contamination in the event of an attack. 

##### **AIRCRAFT LANDING SITES** 

3-40. The site selected should have sufficient space available to serve as a landing site for incoming and outgoing air ambulances. Sufficient space should be allocated for establishing a landing site for contaminated aircraft downwind and at a safe distance from the patient decontamination site (PDS), the unit and treatment areas. Another important feature that should be considered is a space sufficient to support a helicopter landing zone for evacuation aircraft, ensuring the site is free of hazards, such as power lines, poles, or debris. The helicopter landing zone should be able to accommodate a CH-47 (referred to as size 4 and should be 265 feet [80 meters] in diameter). 

##### **PERIMETER SECURITY** 

3-41. The site selected should maximize the use of available terrain features and defilade for cover and concealment. Personnel assigned to the Role 3 hospital are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. The extent of perimeter security requirements is dependent upon whether the unit is included in a base cluster or is solely responsible for its own security. For additional information on defending the hospital, refer to FM 4-02 and see Appendix C of this publication. 

##### **FLOW OF TRAFFIC (PATIENTS AND VEHICLES)** 

3-42. In establishing the traffic patterns within the unit area, consider the following: 

- The selected site should permit the establishment of the treatment and administrative areas in such a manner as to maximize the smooth flow of patients through the triage, diagnostic, treatment, and holding areas. Using overlapping internal traffic patterns should be minimized. 

- The external traffic pattern should afford a smooth flow of vehicle traffic through the unit area. There should be sufficient space allocated for ambulance turnaround once the patient has been delivered to the triage area. Intersections accommodating cross traffic should be avoided as they present the potential for traffic jams and accidents. 

- A route from the landing site to the triage area should be established which minimizes the distance the patient should be carried, and which affords easy access to the treatment area. 

- Traffic patterns of the other units in the base cluster should be considered when determining internal routes. 

##### **EQUIPMENT** 

3-43. Certain pieces of equipment require strategic placement within the unit area. In selecting the site, the placement of this type of equipment should be considered. For example, trailer-mounted 100-kilowatt generators must be placed in such a manner as to enhance their safe operation and to reduce their heat signature and noise level yet be close enough to unit and treatment areas that the limited amount of cable can reach. It is preferable to maximize the use of natural terrain features within the site to provide a portion of the needed shielding rather than having to rely solely on the use of sandbags.

##### **DECONTAMINATION AREA** 

3-44. The site should be large enough to provide an area for patient decontamination. The specific site selected to establish the decontamination site must be downwind of the unit and treatment areas and a safe enough distance to prevent contamination if rotary wing aircraft are used to transport contaminated patients. For additional information for establishing a PDS and how to conduct patient decontamination in a CBRN environment, refer to ATP 4-02.7 and ATP 4-02.4. 

#### **SHELTERING THE HOSPITAL** 

3-45. When providing Role 3 hospitalization in a deployed environment the hospital should be established so that the patients and staff are sheltered from the elements. It is also desirable to have some degree of environmental control. 

##### **EXPEDIENT SHELTERS** 

3-46. Expedient shelters are generally more convenient and easier to establish and use when the hospital is conducting a movement and should provide emergency care. Expedient shelters may be as simple as a tarp being erected to shield the patient and care providers from the sun or rain. In situations where weather and terrain permit, a shaded area adjacent to the route of march will suffice. It may be as simple as setting up on the tailgate of a vehicle which may be adequate for the immediate situation. 

##### **TENTAGE AND SHELTERS** 

3-47. Tentage and shelters for a Role 3 hospital are often a mixture of tentage and shelter types. The two most prevalent are the tent, expandable, modular, personnel shelter (temper) and the one and two-sided International Organization for Standardization shelter. The positioning of the shelter requires materiel handling equipment. Additionally, engineer support may be required to prepare the site prior to establishing a Role 3 hospital. This engineer support should include trenches and patient bunkers for the protection of patients and hospital staff. The use of camouflage for Role 3 hospitals should be reviewed by legal at the TMC unless policy is established and delegates to the MEDBDE. 

3-48. The Role 3 hospital is equipped with medical tents and shelters on their TOE. Tentage and shelters on the TOE are for hospitalization patient treatment areas based on the common table of allowances and the unit’s TOE. Shelters in the Role 3 hospital are self-contained transportable multi-application utility shelter that expands on one or two sides and are container sized. Hospital staff are required to perform -10 level maintenance on organic tents and shelters. Anything above -10 level is coordinated thru sustainment. 

3-49. Tents provide hospital personnel with a shelter system that is quick to setup and strike. Their portability and convenience are especially useful for forward deployed hospitalization and specialized augmentation formations. Tents are easy to camouflage and conceal and allow flexibility in site selection. 

**_Note 1._** When a unit replaces existing nonmedical tentage or shelters, selection criteria for new tents should include compatibility with the unit’s current tentage or shelters size requirements and existing heating, cooling, and electrical requirements and capabilities. 

**_Note 2_** . Shelters or tentage for C2 purposes (HHD, hospital center or field hospital operations section) and logistics support area (sleeping area) are nonmedical and fall under the common tables of allowance or modified TOE which are purchased with unit funds. 

##### **SEMIPERMANENT BUILDINGS** 

3-50. Semipermanent buildings are generally constructed and used in base clusters or forward operating bases particularly in long-term operations predominated by stability tasks. Semipermanent buildings offer several features that make them very desirable. The structures can be built to specific dimensions which are required to establish and operate a Role 3 hospital. 

##### **BUILDINGS OF OPPORTUNITY** 

3-51. Buildings of opportunity present several distinct advantages and should be used whenever possible. These may include electrical lighting, air conditioning and central heat, telephones, running water, toilets, and laundry and bath facilities. Prior to establishing a hospital in an existing structure, the building must first be inspected and approved for occupancy by the supporting engineers. The building’s existing layout may pose a significant challenge to hospital personnel when trying to establish an efficient layout. 

##### **HEALTH FACILITY MANAGEMENT** 

3-52. The facility management capability at a Role 3 hospital consists of the utilities operation and maintenance warrant officer who generally serves as the overall maintenance officer (nonmedical). This individual provides technical expertise to operate, maintain, and repair the Army’s utility systems. Units should plan for long-term sustainment of power generation equipment and may consider shifting this requirement (tactical or commercial generators) to the Logistics Civil Augmentation Program or other contract support. Refer to ATP 4-10.1 for more information to the Logistics Civil Augmentation Program. Additional capabilities available at a hospital include utilities equipment repairers, motor pool, and laundry and bath. Other personnel may be further assigned or tasked to perform repairs or facility management functions. 

**_Note._** The health facility planner on the TMC and MEDBDE staff is responsible for monitoring facility engineer support to subordinate medical units, assisting medical units with facility project requirements, establishing facility management policies in accordance with theater policies, and coordinating for external facility engineering support for extended sustainability of MTFs and infrastructure. For a comprehensive discussion of facility planning considerations, refer to ATP 4-02.1, Chapter 8. 

###### **The two paragraphs below implement NATO STANAG 2060 and 2931.** 

#### **CAMOUFLAGE OF MEDICAL UNITS** 

3-53. If the failure to camouflage endangers or compromises tactical operations, the camouflage of the MTF may be ordered by a NATO commander of at least brigade-level or equivalent. Dispersion of tents and equipment is accomplished to the maximum extent possible. A controlled entry into the medical unit’s area is established. NATO STANAG 2060 and 2931 provides for camouflage of the Geneva emblem and Red Crescent on medical facilities where the lack of camouflage might compromise tactical operations. The STANAGS defines medical facilities as medical units, medical vehicles, and medical aircraft on the ground. Camouflage of the Red Cross means covering it up or taking it down. 

**_Note._** The black cross on an olive background is not a recognized emblem of the Geneva Conventions. 

3-54. It is not envisioned that the Role 3 hospital will be camouflaged. For more information on the law of armed conflict and Geneva Conventions, refer to FM 4-02, FM 6-27, and see Appendix C of this publication. 

#### **SPECIALIZED ORGANIZATION AUGMENTATION** 

3-55. Through the orders process, BOA, and PAR specialized detachments and teams can be attached and fall under the C2 of the HHD, hospital center. These units may include elements of— 

- The medical detachment (prolonged care augmentation). 

- The medical detachment (minimal care). 

- The medical detachment (forward resuscitative and surgical). 

- The hospital augmentation team (head and neck). 

3-56. These specialized units may be attached to the hospital center. In the case of the PCAD, medical detachment (minimal care), and FRSD, they may be further attached as follows: 

- PCAD. Forward deployed as far forward as the Role 2 medical company (brigade support) (BSMC). 

- Medical detachment (minimal care). In support of a medical detachment (combat and operational stress control) or dental company (area support) (DCAS). 

- FRSD. Forward deployed as far forward as the Role 2 BSMC. 

3-57. When not forward deployed, the Role 3 hospital uses specialized organization personnel as augmentation of assigned hospital staff but never fully relies on these assets as they may be pulled away at a moment’s notice, mission dependent. When the operational requirement is complete, specialized organizations may return to the Role 3 hospital. For a discussion on specialized detachments and teams that may be attached to the Role 3 hospital, see Chapter 4 of this publication. 

3-58. Hospital leaders should consider specialized units in the planning process. Specific use of specialized organizations operating in the Role 3 hospital footprint should be included in the hospital’s TACSOP. For additional planning factors, see Appendix B of this publication. 

###### **The two paragraphs below implement ABCANZ standard 2082.** 

#### **TRAINING CONSIDERATIONS** 

3-59. Commanders and planners employ hospitals in a variety of ways to meet the medical support requirements presented at a given time or for a given theater. During operations in Iraq and Afghanistan, Role 3 hospitals did not normally deploy with their full complement of assigned personnel due to policy, PAR, and the orders process. Commanders and planners employed hospitals in a variety of ways to meet the medical support requirements presented at a given time or for a given theater. In many cases, hospital personnel lost many skills required to deploy, establish, and relocate Role 3 hospitals utilizing the personnel and equipment on their TOEs. It is important, as the Army continues its transition to large-scale combat operations, to ensure Role 3 hospitals are prepared to deploy without the luxury of established buildings, long-standing SOP, and left-behind equipment that Role 3 hospitals focus training on Headquarters Department of the Army standard mission essential task list. 

3-60. Hospitals are receiving new equipment as the Army moves into a period of modernization. The new air beam tents, for example, create challenges for Soldiers and leaders, due to their difference (integral floors and an integrated inflation system) that may cause reconfiguration of load plans and additional training on setup and maintenance. The new air beam tents have slightly different dimensions than previous generations of tents and therefore, change how ward masters develop staking plans and complex the hospital. However, simply requiring the unit to practice erecting and taking down the tents will pay dividends when establishing the hospital in an OE in support of maneuver forces. 

3-61. Another skill that has likely diminished over the last several years is the ability to develop layout options and staking plans for the hospital complex. It is a difficult task and takes a lot of practice to become proficient at developing ways to organize hospital capabilities that efficiently support patient care and allow for the timely establishment of hospitalization. Planning the layout is more complicated when considering whether hospital augmentation detachments will be added or taken away from the MTF throughout the course of operations. Complexing a base hospital that, in the best ability possible, allows detachments to integrate or be removed comes with a great deal of practice, trial and error, and planning. 

3-62. Rarely, in the last several years of supporting operations in Iraq and Afghanistan, have Role 3 hospitals been required to use TOE equipment to establish water and power distribution throughout the MTF. In conjunction with a well thought out staking plan, ensuring the power and water distribution (to include wastewater removal) supports the requirements throughout the hospital is essential. Furthermore, the distribution of power and water is obviously best if established very early in the complexing of the facility. Once tents are erected and equipment established inside, it would be detrimental to realize adequate water and power supply has not been established. For detailed information on Role 3 hospitalization and specialized medical and surgical augmentation training considerations, see Appendix F of this publication. 

##### **<mark>SECTION III – HOSPITAL CENTER COMMAND AND SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS</mark>** 

3-63. Army command relationships define superior and subordinate relationships between unit commanders. Army support relationships are not a command authority, they are established by Army commanders when subordination of one unit to another is inappropriate. Hospital leaders should be well versed in the aspects of Army command and support relationships to better provide hospital and on order specialized hospital medical augmentation within their assigned AO. For more information on Army command and support relationships, refer to FM 3-0, FM 4-02, and FM 5-0. 

#### **ARMY COMMAND RELATIONSHIPS** 

3-64. There are five types of command relationships. They are: 

- Organic. 

- Assigned. 

- Attached. 

- Operational control (referred to as OPCON). 

- Tactical control (referred to as TACON). 

**_Note._** Unless modified, administrative control (referred to as ADCON) responsibility of hospitalization organizations remain with the parent unit when they are under the operational or tactical control of a gaining unit. 

3-65. The HHD, hospital center is assigned to and maintains a command relationship with the MEDBDE. Through mission requirements and the orders process the Role 3 hospital may be further attached or under the operational or tactical control of a supported unit. Care should be taken when doing this as hospitalization assets are scarce and may need to be reallocated elsewhere with little planning time and may impact timelines. 

3-66. The HHD, hospital center provides C2 for the Role 3 hospital comprised of one or two field hospitals (32-bed) and other assigned or attached hospital augmentation detachments and elements of specialty organizations. 

3-67. Hospital augmentation detachments will be assigned to an HHD, hospital center, and further attached or assigned to a field hospital (32-bed). Medical augmentation detachments lose their identity as a separate and distinct unit when they are employed. While there will likely still be a supervisory chain of command like what the personnel were familiar with prior to deploying, their medical functions are absorbed into those similar capabilities, wards, and sections of the hospital once they become attached to the field hospital (32bed). 

3-68. The HHD, hospital center is dependent on the field hospital (32-bed) for administrative support, feeding, unit-level maintenance, security, power, and classes of supply. The HHD, hospital center does not have a very robust or large staff. When it is assigned two field hospitals (32-bed) and associated augmentation detachments, operating in two separate locations, the HHD, hospital center may have to rely on the field hospital (32-bed) staff that it is collocated with. Combining the two staffs allows for a large enough staff to operate 24-hours a day with key leaders present all the time. A technique, to keep the functions of the two separate staffs distinct, is to have the HHD, hospital center staff focus on mid and long-range planning, coordination with the MEDBDE, and synchronization with other external units ("up and out"). The field hospital (32-bed) staff can remain mostly focused on the internal operations of the hospital and augmentation detachments as well as current and short-range operational planning ("down and in"). When necessary, the staffs can be utilized cross-functionally due the nature of them working in the same command post. 

#### **ARMY SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS** 

3-69. There are four types of Army support relationships. They are: 

- Direct support. 

- General support. 

- General support-reinforcing. 

- Reinforcing. 

**_Note_** . Area support is not a support relationship. Area support is a task assigned to a sustainment unit directing it to support units in or passing through a specified location (ATP 4-90). Medical units also perform the area support task. 

3-70. The hospital center normally operates in a general or direct support role to the supported unit, retaining their command relationship with the MEDBDE. This is the preferred method and allows quicker reaction to reallocating scarce hospitalization assets and is in synchronization with the six AHS principles. Only under certain situations would the Role 3 hospital not be under a MEDBDE. An example would be if a MEDBDE was not task organized in support of division, but the Role 3 hospital is. In this situation, every effort should be made to ensure the Role 3 hospital is under the C2 of the senior medical commander. 

##### **<mark>SECTION IV – HOSPITALIZATION ROLE IN PATIENT MOVEMENT OPERATIONS</mark>** 

3-71. _Patient movement_ is the act or process of moving a sick, injured, wounded, or other person to obtain medical and/or dental care or treatment, which include medical regulating, patient evacuation, and en route medical care (JP 4-02). The Army refers to patient evacuation as MEDEVAC. For more information on MEDEVAC and medical regulating, refer to ATP 4-02.2. 

3-72. The Role 3 hospital has a role in MEDEVAC and medical regulating. Listed below are key areas focused on the Role 3 hospital’s roles and responsibilities. 

#### **MEDICAL EVACUATION** 

3-73. Medical evacuation is the key factor in ensuring the continuity of care is provided to our Soldiers by providing en route medical care during evacuation, facilitating the transfer of patients to the Role 3 hospital. Medical evacuation support is normally provided on a direct support and area support basis. 

3-74. The Role 3 hospital has the responsibility of coordinating with the supporting MEDBDE S-3 PM branch to request evacuations assets to clear beds. If no MEDBDE is available then through the orders process evacuation coordination will be detailed and conducted thru the senior medical commander, appropriate surgeon section at echelon, or other service PM asset. 

#### **MEDICAL REGULATING** 

3-75. Medical regulating is an integral part of medical evacuation. _Medical regulating_ is the coordination for the movement of patients to match patients with an appropriate medical treatment facility. See also health service support, medical treatment facility. (JP 4-02). Medical regulating is designed to ensure the efficient and safe movement of patients. It is a system that entails identifying the patients awaiting evacuation, locating the available beds, and coordinating the transportation means for movement. Historically, medical regulating starts at Role 3, where robust patient administration section exists with the capacity to provide the necessary medical regulating support. Informal medical regulating is normally conducted at Role 1 and Role 2. The Role 3 hospital patient administration section will ensure the continuity of patient records starting at Role 2 through Role 3 MTFs. The requirement to transfer patients from the Role 3 hospital to another MTF within the operational area may occur. This results from— 

- Surgical backlogs. 

- Current bed status or number and location of patients by diagnostic category. 

- Specialty care or resource requirements. 

- Tactical situation, such as MASCAL, planned movement of the Role 3 hospital, or enemy activity. 

3-76. In the Role 3 hospital, attending physicians notify the hospital patient administration section of patients requiring evacuation. The patient administration section works with the medical regulating officer to request and coordinate movement authority from the MEDBDE S-3 PM branch or other approved medical regulating asset. 

3-77. The medical regulating officer tracks the availability of medical specialties at each Role 3 hospital and the specific number of beds that can be assigned to that specialty. A bed report is given for the medical capabilities available at each hospital which helps designate where a patient will be evacuated to. Bed reporting is broken down into contingency bed reporting and noncontingency bed reporting. The requesting medical provider will call to verify that the receiving medical provider has accepted the patient and is aware the patient is inbound. 

3-78. Medical regulating functions related to the Role 3 hospital include consolidating all evacuation requests within the hospital and forwarding an evacuation request to the next higher headquarters for action. The Role 3 hospital patient administration section is responsible for keeping the next higher echelon informed of all statuses. Additional Role 3 hospital patient administration section duties include— 

- Matching existing medical capabilities with reported patient needs. 

- Scheduling and arranging movement of U.S., NATO, or DOD patients and authorized beneficiaries. 

- Establishing procedures regulating the evacuation of incoming and outgoing patients. 

- Tracking near real time patient care data within the Role 3 hospital and regulating patients with a single DOD automated information system that supports medical regulating, PM, and patient in transit visibility. 

- Know and execute the originating MTF responsibilities described in ATP 4-02.2. 

##### **<mark>SECTION V – CLASS VIII SUPPLY AND LOGISTICS OPERATIONS IN A ROLE 3 HOSPITAL</mark>** 

3-79. For the Role 3 hospital, MEDLOG is comprised of Class VIIIA (medical materiel), Class VIIIB (blood support), and medical maintenance. The MEDLOG assets of the Role 3 hospital are small but have an impactful mission to ensure medical supplies, blood, and medical maintenance operations are maintained for the hospital to perform its mission. 

#### **CLASS VIIIA (MEDICAL SUPPLIES)** 

3-80. Class VIIIA support for Role 3 hospitals is a vital part of the AHS mission and includes management of a commodity that should be adapted to specific theater health care requirements, distribution plans, and capabilities provided by theater sustainment organizations. 

3-81. During port operations and reception, staging, onward movement, and integration the Role 3 hospitals should be capable of operations immediately upon initial entry of forces. It is imperative that Role 3 hospitals deploy with their complete unit basic load or have it pushed into theater for integration prior to the required date for patient reception. Therefore, MEDLOG support is included in planning for port opening and early entry operations. Port operations may include the issue of medical unit sets from Army pre-positioned stock, receipt of unit deployment packages, and integration of refrigerated and controlled substances. Early entry operations may also include reach back support to a designated installation medical supply activity. 

3-82. Class VIIIA sustainment of Role 3 hospitals present the most complex medical materiel requirements and may consume materiel at a tremendous rate when providing trauma care in support of combat operations. Specialty care for burn injuries, orthopedic injuries and surgeries, and neurosurgery often require materiel and equipment that is not standard and may not have been anticipated or stocked in sufficient quantities prior to deployment. Role 3 hospitals are normally a direct customer of a MEDLOG company that can meet the unit’s mission requirements. 

3-83. The HHD, hospital center, field hospital (32-bed), its associated augmentation detachments, and attached specialty organizations operating within the Role 3 hospital footprint will rely on organic MEDLOG assets or on the supporting MEDLOG company for Class VIIIA support and augmentation of MEDLOG personnel, as required. For additional information on MEDLOG, refer to ATP 4-02.1. 

##### **MAJOR MEDICAL ASSEMBLAGES** 

3-84. Major medical assemblages or MMS are deployable medical system-equivalent Army-unique sets that consist of a grouping of medical and nonmedical items under a single stock number managed by the Army Medical Logistics Command and are used primarily by the Army. Hospital leaders can view their assemblages on the Army Medical Logistics Command’s Medical Materiel Information Portal.

3-85. Each MMS is developed specifically for each hospital unit and is designed to meet the minimum mission essential wartime requirements to sustain a range of military operations for 72 hours. Potency and dated medical materiel are not included in the MMS but is provided separately upon deployment as part of the unit deployment package. Hospital leaders should consider seven days of supply mission variable dependent. 

##### **SPECIALTY SETS** 

3-86. Stability and defense support of civil authorities’ tasks require more definitive or tailored assemblages such as humanitarian assistance sets. There are three types of humanitarian assistance sets as follows: 

- Humanitarian assistance surgical augmentation set. 

- Humanitarian assistance pediatric augmentation set. 

- Humanitarian assistance adult augmentation set. 

3-87. These sets were established to augment the Role 3 hospital and are not intended for use as standalone sets. They contain special medical and surgical supplies and equipment that are essential for providing AHS support to a civilian population during operations where stability (as part of a global health engagement) or defense support of civil authorities are the primary focus. 

3-88. The sets do not have an assigned line-item number and are not authorized by TOE or modified TOE. There is no basis of issue for these sets. Units should determine if there is a need for the sets during planning or as dictated by the Office of The Surgeon General and medical mission requirements. 

3-89. Humanitarian assistance sets are managed by the Army Medical Logistics Command. The deputy chief of staff, logistics is the release authority for these sets. For additional information and questions concerning the humanitarian assistance sets refer to the Army Medical Logistics Command website. 

##### **POTENCY AND DATED MATERIEL PROGRAM** 

3-90. The Centrally Managed Medical Potency and Dated Materiel Program provides unit deployment packages for early deploying Role 3 hospitals departing CONUS home stations. Unit deployment package is a term coined within the Centrally Managed Medical Potency and Dated Materiel Program that represents a hospital’s basic load of medical potency and dated materiel. 

3-91. In the event of a deployment, this program gives the Army Medical Logistics Command the ability to push deployment packages (minus support kit items) to early deploying hospitals at home station or another location. The deployment package quantities are based on the unit assemblages it is authorized. 

3-92. The Army Medical Logistics Command’s Army war reserve sustainment stocks, in conjunction with theater single integrated MEDLOG manager operations, support and maintain the medical requirements of deployed units after initial issue of a deployment package. The Office of The Surgeon General is the release authority for this materiel. 

3-93. Unit deployment packages are released at no cost for validated Role 3 hospitals that deploy on or before deployment plus 31 days of a declared contingency operation or conflict. There are limited amounts of unit deployment packages, so early entry units may receive them while units deploying later may not. For more information on centrally managed programs, refer to ATP 4-02.1 and the Army Medical Logistics Command website. 

**The following paragraphs implement STANAG 2939 and ABCANZ standard 2156.** 

#### **CLASS VIIIB (BLOOD SUPPORT)** 

3-94. Blood support has evolved significantly with the fielding of the deployable medical system blood laboratory and shelters, tactical expandable (also known as the International Organization for Standardization shelters). These shelters are hard-walled shelters used in the deployable medical system-equipped hospitals. 

Hospital personnel involved in the handling, storage, or administration of blood and blood products should receive appropriate training and education on the safe and effective use of these products. 

##### **HOSPITAL BLOOD INVENTORY MANAGEMENT AND RESUPPLY** 

3-95. Hospital blood inventory management and resupply operations are coordinated directly with the supporting medical detachment (blood support) or other supporting blood supply unit. The Role 3 hospital has the storage capacity to maintain— 

- 480 units of Groups A, B, and O liquid red blood cells (RBC) of both rhesus (Rh) positive and negative blood types. 

- 420 units of whole blood (WB) of both Rh positive and negative blood types. 

- 50-100 units of Groups A, B, O, and AB fresh frozen plasma depending on how individual plasma units are packaged. 

- 50-100 units of Groups A, B, O, and AB cryoprecipitate depending on how individual cryoprecipitate units are packaged. 

- Five units of Groups A, B, O, and AB platelets of both Rh positive and negative blood types. 

3-96. The above storage capacity is for one field hospital (32-bed). If a second field hospital (32-bed) is taskorganized under the HHD, hospital center and is fully established and collocated with the first field hospital (32-bed), blood inventory management and resupply are doubled and should be a combined element. 

3-97. The Role 3 hospital has the capability to conduct limited emergency fresh WB and apheresis platelet collections but does not have the capability to perform Food and Drug Administration (FDA) cleared infectious disease testing of the donor units (rapid screening methods for hepatitis, human immunodeficiency virus, and syphilis testing may be available). The decision to transfuse blood collected in a theater is governed by theater blood policy. 

3-98. The relatively large quantity of blood maintained requires the use of large-capacity, blood bank-type refrigerators equipped with audible and visual temperature alarm systems. Freezers for frozen plasma storage are similarly monitored. For additional information on blood banking and transfusions, refer to TM 4- 02.70/NAVMED P-5120/AFMAN 41-111_IP. 

##### **EMERGENCY BLOOD COLLECTION** 

3-99. With proper planning, the Armed Services Blood Program will normally be able to provide adequate inventories of blood products to meet mission requirements. However, emergency situations may arise where the amount of blood products needed to complete a transfusion exceeds the available supply. In such cases, in-theater collection and processing is required to provide apheresis platelets and fresh WB In-theater collections are not conducted using the same rigorous screening and viral marker testing as CONUS-based donor centers. When conducting emergency collections, there are two general types of non-FDA compliant blood products that may be used including— 

- Blood products (usually WB or apheresis platelets) collected in theater and released for transfusion prior to the completion of required FDA testing. 

- Blood products, acquired from the HN or other countries, which may not be subject to the same set of stringent standards as is required by the FDA. 

3-100. Whenever emergency blood collections through walking blood banks are required, every attempt will be made to ensure that donors are pre-screened with the required FDA-licensed blood donor testing. On the day of donation, donors should be tested using Armed Services Blood Program Office or individually appointed as the Joint Blood Program Officer-approved rapid infectious disease screening test kits. A set of specimen sample tubes must also be collected for retrospective FDA-required blood donor testing using licensed methodologies. Patients receiving blood products from in-theater collection must be followed for up to one year after transfusion for infectious diseases, such as human immunodeficiency virus and Hepatitis B and Hepatitis C. For additional information on emergency blood collection including donor selection and apheresis collection, refer to TM 8-227-12/NAVMED P6530/AFH 44-152_IP. 

3-101. The employment of emergency transfusion protocols involving the use of non-FDA compliant blood products should be limited to instances where FDA compliant blood products are not available or when these products cannot be delivered at an acceptable rate to sustain resuscitation of a bleeding patient. The decision to use fresh WB that has not been screened using rapid field tests or complete FDA-approved donor testing for infectious agents is a medical decision that must be made after thorough consideration of the risks and benefits.

3-102. Most available RBC and WB units will be Rh-positive. Type O Rh-negative donors only make up approximately 15 percent of the donor population. Rh-negative RBC and WB units are preferred for females of unknown Rh type or known Rh-negative patients. Transfusing Rh-positive RBCs to any Rh-negative female can result in future complications if she develops an antibody to Rh and her future fetus is Rh-positive. The impact of antibody production in males is not significant, thus priority for Rh-negative blood is given to females if there is not enough Rh-negative blood to meet all patient needs. Rh-positive RBCs/WB should be transfused for emergency care and thoroughly documented in the patient’s medical record. 

3-103. Unless otherwise specified, approximately 15% of liquid RBC requested may be Rh-negative. The blood distribution system plans for 15 percent of all blood distributed in theater to be Rh-negative. This is true for all MTFs in theater. Blood donor availability may limit Rh-negative blood product production and distribution. 

#### **MEDICAL DEVICE MAINTENANCE** 

3-104. The Role 3 hospital health services maintenance technician and biomedical equipment specialists are responsible for field maintenance of medical devices assigned or attached to the Role 3 hospital. This includes the added workload from attached specialty organizations, such as the FRSD or PCAD, collocated with the Role 3 hospital when not forward deployed to a Role 2 medical company. 

3-105. The Role 3 hospital provides limited field maintenance for augmentation and specialty organization’s medical devices on an area basis. This includes medical elements assigned or attached to the hospital. The hospital should also maintain automated maintenance records on assigned medical devices and supported units in the approved medical maintenance management system according to the medical maintenance strategy established in the AO or theater. 

3-106. The Role 3 hospital relies on the supporting MEDLOG company for medical device field maintenance and augmentation of biomedical equipment personnel, as required. The MEDLOG company is responsible for maintaining medical maintenance regeneration enablers and patient movement items and providing field maintenance to the Role 3 hospital in accordance with the maintenance allocation chart and medical maintenance strategy. The MEDLOG company maintains automated maintenance records on all equipment of supported medical units or elements. For additional information on medical device maintenance, refer to ATP 4-02.1 and TB Med 750-2. 

##### **SECTION VI – NUTRITION CARE OPERATIONS** 

3-107. The nutrition care section provides hospital nutrition care services that includes meal preparation and service to patients and staff, medical nutrition therapy, dietetic planning, patient education, and theater health promotion. The nutrition care section also advises the hospital commander on nutrition and health matters and may also advise and guide other commanders in theater on nutrition matters. 

3-108. Hospital food service operations are paramount to ensure inpatients receive sufficient nutrition to prevent complications associated with malnutrition, while complying with the appropriate diet orders given their medical diagnosis. Hospital food service operations also provide staff feeding given the unique requirements of hospital staff to remain onsite or near treatment areas to maintain at or near continuous operations during conflict. 

3-109. Hospitalized patients will receive three prepared hot meals and other nourishments as medically indicated. The individual operation ration is authorized for patients only in emergency situations when other rations are not available. Milk and enhancements will be added to the menu as they become available. Staff assigned to medical units will be fed according to the Service AO ration policy. However, to simplify procurement, meal preparation, and service, staff may be served the patient regular hot meal if available. For additional information refer to ATP 4-41. 

#### **STAFF RESPONSIBILITIES** 

3-110. The primary hospital staff members responsible for nutrition care operations are the registered dietitian and the nutrition care specialist. The hospital commander is responsible for providing military personnel for support duties in the nutrition care section. 

3-111. For detailed information on the qualifications and responsibilities of the dietitian and nutrition care specialist refer to Training Circular (TC) 8-502 and DA Pamphlet (DA Pam) 611-21. 

##### **REGISTERED DIETITIAN** 

3-112. The registered dietitian formulates policies, develops procedures, and directs and supervises the operation of nutrition care services and the provision of comprehensive nutrition care programs in the deployable hospital. In addition, the dietician— 

- Manages medical food preparation and service systems and staff food preparation services. 

- Coordinates and ensures the procurement and receipt of safe, wholesome food items and rations for patients and staff and medical diet supplements for patients who require a modified diet. 

- Provides nutrition health promotion programs for the military community and develops and directs nutrition education or dietary intervention programs for the military. 

- Assists the physician by providing patient nutritional assessment, nutrition therapy recommendations, and medical nutrition therapy. 

- Serves as a consultant at all levels of nutrition-related health, human performance and stability operations issues and medical food service operations. 

- Develops, implements, and directs nutrition and medical food service education programs for nutrition care specialists and other medical personnel. 

- Communicates nutrition care’s workload with hospital leaders to ensure other assigned duties do not interfere with the patient care and feeding missions. 

##### **NUTRITION CARE SPECIALIST** 

3-113. The nutrition care specialist performs clinical dietetic functions in the dietary management and treatment of patients and staff as well as— 

- Assists in the nutritional screening and assessment of individual patients. 

- Assists in the health promotion program activities. 

- Prepares, serves, and delivers modified and regular food items in the management of the nutritional needs of patients (across the life span and a diversity of people, cultures, and religions in support of the mission) under the supervision of a dietitian or senior nutrition care NCO. 

- Monitors, requisitions, plans, and maintains inventory control of subsistence, supply, and equipment. 

- Prepares and serves meals to staff. 

- Serves as the vehicle operator for the nutrition care section. 

- Coordinates with the field sanitation team for potable water, waste disposal, and pest control. 

##### **OTHER NUTRITIONAL CARE SUPPORT PERSONNEL** 

3-114. The nutrition care section has specific support needs in theater, and the dietitian and/or nutrition care specialist should be involved in the initial planning deployments to ensure nutrition care support requirements are known and are factored into planning. For example, medical rations will be required to support patients’ unique feeding requirements and support personnel are needed to assist with food service to staff and sanitation duties. The number of personnel will be based on mission requirements. This support may come from Soldiers or contracted dining facility attendants. 

3-115. Soldiers assigned for support duties may be unfamiliar with food sanitation principles and will require training and supervision from nutrition support personnel. 

3-116. In operations where civilian-contracted dining facility attendants are available, the chief and NCO incharge will coordinate with the contracting officer representative for the number of attendants required and shift schedules in accordance with the contract. Additionally, a dietitian and the appropriate number of nutrition care specialists should be deployed to ensure that patients’ nutritional needs are met in support of their respective diagnoses. Dietitians should coordinate with the contracting officer or the contracting officer representative to understand the contract and how the dietitian and nutrition care specialist team should coordinate for patient feeding in accordance with the performance work statement. The dietitian is not the contracting officer nor the contracting officer representative and should ensure that all matters pertaining to the contracted personnel are handled in accordance with the guidance provided to avoid violating the contract or initiating an unauthorized commitment of government funds. Medical diet supplements may not be included in the contract and may require procurement through Class I or Class VIIIA supply channels.

#### **FEEDING OPERATIONS AND PROCUREMENT** 

3-117. Dietitians and senior nutrition care specialists should coordinate with hospital S-4 to ensure procurement of the requisite food, beverage, hospital dietary supplements, and cleaning and sanitation supplies to meet patient and staff feeding, food safety, and sanitation requirements across the full range of military operations and training exercises. This will often require coordination with the Army Food Program representative (assistant chief of staff, logistics) and the senior theater or installation food advisor to ensure procurement of military rations, commercial food and beverages, medical dietary supplements, and cleaning and sanitation supplies through approved sources. 

##### **FEEDING OPERATIONS CONSIDERATIONS AND CHALLENGES** 

3-118. The common nutrition-related issues presented in field feeding include dehydration, inadequate energy and carbohydrate intake and gastrointestinal complaints. Even an individual that is mildly dehydrated (body water losses amounting to as little as two percent of body weight) will have impaired performance, reduced appetite, and sluggishness. To prevent diarrhea and constipation, advise Soldiers to hydrate, choose high-fiber foods, eat wholesome foods maintained in sanitary conditions, and avoid eating or drinking locally produced foods unless approved by veterinary personnel. Stress the value of consuming military rations that are designed to provide for nutritional requirements. 

3-119. In a contract food service dining facility, the dietitian and nutrition care specialist retain responsibility for ensuring that patients receive meals specific to their diet order. The dietitian and/or nutrition care specialist coordinates between the nursing staff of each ward, nutrition care section personnel, and appropriate contractor for following patient diet orders and delivering patient meals. In a case where the contract does not require the contracted personnel to deliver meals to patients, the nutrition care section should— 

- Set up an area close to the wards to assemble patient meals and nourishments with the following equipment: table, serving supplies, refrigerator, blender, microwave (if power supply can support), rodent-proof containers, shelves, and sanitation supplies (gloves and cleaning supplies). 

- Use the contractor’s daily menu and make modifications for patient diets. 

- Turn in meal tickets to the contract staff to fill the diet orders. 

- Establish a system to order and pick up food from the contract dining facility. Carry the covered food from the contract facility to the hospital in boxes or insulated containers. Assemble the patient trays and deliver to the patient. 

- Establish a method for disposing of patient trays and other waste. 

###### **The following paragraph implements STANAG 2937.** 

##### **MILITARY RATIONS** 

> 3-120. Unit group rations and field packaged meals are designed to simplify and streamline the process of providing the highest quality meals in the field. They integrate modules of heat and serve (formerly known as T-Rations and A-Rations) with quick-prepared, user-friendly brand name commercial products. The unit group ration is used by unit food service facilities to sustain groups of personnel during worldwide operations. Refrigeration is required with some unit group rations. Menus and recipes are included with each module. The heat and serve module feed up to 50 Soldiers and requires separate supplements and enhancements of milk, bread, fresh fruits and vegetables, and cold cereal to provide a complete meal averaging 1,450 calories per serving. The A-ration module includes perishable items that consume refrigeration, transportation, fuel, equipment, and potable water requirements. The A-ration module serves up to 50 Soldiers a high-quality meal and provides 1,450 calories and are complete when served with bread, salad, fruit, and cereal. For more information regarding military rations, refer to ATP 4-41.

3-121. Medical diet supplements are used in combination with the unit group ration to prepare modified patient diets to meet the requirements for high-calorie-high-protein, pureed liquid, full liquid, and clear liquid diets. The purchase and resupply of these items should be coordinated during the hospital’s predeployment phase. 

3-122. Individual operation rations are individually packaged meals designed for issue, either in single meals or in multiples of three for a complete ration and are only authorized for patients in emergency situations when other rations are not available. The components are packaged in flexible envelopes with flameless ration heaters. Each meal provides an average of 1,300 calories, 169 grams of carbohydrate, 41 grams of protein, and 50 grams of fat. There are 24 menus of which four are vegetarian menus. Each box of 12 ready to eat meals has two vegetarian menu meals. Under emergency conditions the meals and the medical diet supplements can be combined to meet the patient feeding requirements for high-calorie-high-protein, blenderized liquid, full liquid, and clear liquid diets. 

##### **CLINICAL NUTRITION OPERATIONS** 

3-123. The clinical dietetics involves a systematic process of providing nutrition care to patients. The process begins upon admission and ends at discharge. The admitted patient’s physician writes a diet order. Nursing staff updates the ward diet roster to reflect the changes and new patients on DA Form 1829 ( _Hospital Food Service - Ward Diet Roster)_ . 

- 3-124. The nutrition care personnel then— 

 - Complete the patient nutrition screening within 24-hours of admission or encounter and document it in the medical record. Results of the nutrition screening determine the medical nutrition therapy protocol. 

 - Interview patient for food preferences, food tolerances, food allergies, and cultural or religious practices that impact food consumption. 

 - Create a menu for each patient. 

 - Identify nourishment and forced fluid requirements to adequately stock. 

 - Deliver meals and nourishments to patients. 

 - Monitor changes to patient diet orders. 

 - Conduct daily follow-ups on high and medium risk patients and on all patients every five to seven days, patient load permitting. 

##### **CLINICAL DIETETICS REFERENCES** 

3-125. Normal nutrition and assessment are addressed in the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics Nutrition Care Manual. It is not the intent of this section to repeat any of the information from the nutrition care manual (NCM). This section provides other sources of information that enhances the application of the NCM procedures for use in a field setting with limited types of food items available to prepare patient meals. 

3-126. For more information on military dietary reference intakes, refer to AR 40-25/OPNAVINST 10110.1/MCO 10110.49/AFI 44-141. The military dietary reference intakes are intended for healthy and fit Soldiers performing their mission. The military dietary reference intakes are provided in the currently fielded operational rations. Consuming the daily ration provides Soldiers with essential calories, vitamins, and minerals. 

##### **NUTRITION AND DISEASE** 

3-127. Dietitians use the nutrition care process to provide comprehensive medical nutrition therapy to patients according to their medical diagnoses and medical histories. This process involves four interrelated steps that dietitians use to evaluate each patient requiring a dietitian’s care consisting of nutrition assessment, nutrition diagnosis, nutrition intervention, and monitoring and evaluation. The medical nutrition therapy for specific conditions and diagnoses are defined in the NCM. This publication provides evidence-based, disease specific guidance regarding the diagnosis, related physiology and nutrition implications, examples of food selection, and diet modifications. During foreign humanitarian assistance situations dieticians and nutrition care specialists may be asked to complete nutrition assessments for the local population.

##### **PATIENT AND STAFF FEEDING POLICY** 

3-128. The army ensures that patients and staff receive proper nutrition through its patient and staff feeding policy. Commanders of medical units provide appropriate nutrition care to patients, with dietitians and nutrition care specialists playing a key role in medical field feeding. Hospitalized patients receive three hot meals a day, along with other nourishments as medically indicated, and staff members are fed using the same standard when possible. The nutrition care staff works to account for all rations received and used and is prepared to adapt to emergency situations and diverse population needs, such as those encountered in foreign humanitarian assistance deployments. 

###### **Patient Feeding Policy** 

3-129. AR 30-22, Army Food Program states that commanders of medical units organized under a TOE will provide appropriate nutrition care to patients, and that dietitians or nutrition care specialists are responsible for providing for medical field feeding. ATP 4-41 further states that hospitalized patients will receive three hot meals a day and other nourishments as medically indicated. In emergency situations where other rations are not available, individual operational rations may be given to patients; however, milk and enhancements will be added to the menu as soon as they are available. 

3-130. Nutrition care staff should account for rations received and rations used. For additional guidance on field feeding and Class I operations, refer to ATP 4-41. For example, the unit group ration and medical supplements ration items should be accounted for on the medical field production schedule or on the DA Form 3034 ( _Production Schedule)_ . The inpatient census report prepared by patient administration division is the "head count sheet" for inpatients. 

###### **Staff Feeding Policy** 

3-131. In accordance with AR 30-22 and DA Pam 30-22, when personnel and supply resources permit, the hospital staff will be fed using the patient standard of three hot meals per day. When personnel and supplies do not permit, the hospital staff members follow operations ration policy which is individual field ready meals for up to 21 days; authorized enhancements or alternate rations should be provided after 21 days. 

3-132. The nutrition care staff should ensure appropriate accountability of rations using the production schedule and the DA Form 2970 ( _Headcount Report)_ . All Class I should be appropriately accounted for per ATP 4-41. 

##### **DIET ORDERS** 

3-133. The most common diet orders on a deployment are regular, high-calorie-high-protein, clear liquid, and full/pureed liquid. Use available rations and medical diet supplements to prepare other therapeutic diets listed in the NCM. 

3-134. Foreign humanitarian assistance deployments will be in support of civilians (from infants to the very old) for whom a wide variety of nutrition and dietetic needs will be required. Nutrition care personnel should be prepared to respond to the nutrition needs of this diverse population with potentially complex needs. 

##### **THERAPEUTIC DIET PREPARATION AND RATIONS** 

3-135. Therapeutic diets are prepared to accommodate patients’ diet orders. Patient meals are prepared to meet the medical condition(s) and cultural, religious, allergy and intolerance factors to the extent possible. The NCM is the primary reference for therapeutic diets. A copy of the manual should be printed prior to deployment to ensure the reference is available in the event internet access is unreliable or unavailable in theater. Therapeutic diet menu items come from the unit group rations, medical diet supplements, meal enhancements, enteral feeding formulas (if available) and individual field ready to eat rations. 

3-136. Medical diet supplements, used in combination with the individual operation rations and unit group rations provides commercial semi perishable food components required to prepare therapeutic diets. Each medical diet supplement supports 50 patients for a 15-day period based on the following diet mix: 28 regular, five high-calorie, high-protein, 11 pureed liquids, and six clear liquids. The medical diet supplement is ordered from Defense Logistics Agency Troop Support Subsistence with the national stock number: 897001-470-5077. If necessary, individual components may be ordered separately. Enteral feeding products can be ordered through the medical supply section of the hospital. Coordination for ordering components of the medical diet supplement and enteral feeding products should be done prior to any exercise or deployment.

#### **NUTRITION DURING STABILITY OPERATIONS** 

3-137. It is important to understand the diseases and health risks inherent to each country in which deployed and countries that share a border. It is also important to understand the dietary habits and the culture, both of which will benefit the advice and guidance provided to the commander during foreign humanitarian support missions. When contracting agents hire local civilians to work in the food service facility, there may be additional health risks to the supported population. Preventive medicine and veterinary personnel will have detailed information about endemic and epidemic diseases and should be consulted. _Preventive medicine_ is the anticipation, communication, prediction, identification, prevention, education, risk assessment, and control of communicable diseases; illnesses; and exposure to endemic, occupational, and environmental threats. Also called PVNTMED. (JP 4-02). 

3-138. Invaluable information on diseases, injuries, and nutritional requirements in areas of deployment can be obtained from the National Center for Medical Intelligence, U.S. Army Research Institute of Environmental Medicine, U.S. Army Medical Research Institute of Infectious Diseases, U.S. Army Public Health Center, World Health Organization, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, the Center for Army Lessons Learned, and possibly the country’s Ministry of Health. 

##### **SECTION VII – HOSPITALIZATION SUPPORT TO OPERATIONS** 

3-139. It is important for hospital center and field hospital commanders, leaders, and planners to remain situationally aware and connected to the maneuver plans to be prepared for any situation. This includes knowing when units are preparing for and engaged in all operations. The Role 3 hospital then overlays the 6 AHS principles with the OPORD to ensure the hospital is nested with the plan and is in the right place at the right time and appropriately staffed and equipped for the unique injuries that may accompany these types of operations. For more information on operations, refer to ADP 3-0, ADP 3-90, FM 3-0, FM 3-94, ATP 3-91, ATP 3-92, ATP 3-93, and other applicable OE or operational 3-series publications. 

3-140. This section covers hospitalization support to offense, defensive, stability, Defense Support of Civil Authorities (DSCA) operations. It provides brief discussions and references for hospitalization units operating in a multitude of operations that include reconstitution, CBRN environments, urban, subterranean, MASCAL, prolonged care, detainee, and deceased personnel. 

#### **HUMAN PERFORMANCE OPTIMIZATION AND COMMUNITY NUTRITION** 

3-141. The military dietary reference intakes are intended for energy needs for personnel working in a temperate climate and may need to be adjusted based on the operating environment. They are met in the currently fielded operational rations. For more detailed information about the military dietary reference intakes, refer to AR 40-25/OPNAVINST 10110.1/MCO 10110.49/AFI 44-141. 

3-142. Environmental conditions can impact Soldier food and beverage consumption needs. When energy needs are higher, such as in cold weather or high-altitude environments, performance can diminish if energy intake does not meet Soldier needs. Hydration needs are also higher in cold and high-altitude environments. Select information is highlighted in this appendix for nutrition demands in cold, hot, and high-altitude operations. For a more complete account of nutritional demands in these environments use the following resources: 

- The U.S. Army Research Institute of Environmental Medicine review of their published technical notes on nutrition during military operations. 

- The Human Performance Resource by Consortium for Health and Military Performance. 

- Nutrition Care Manual website. 

- U.S. Army Combat Capabilities Development Command Soldier Center, Soldier Sustainment Directorate, Combat Feeding Division. 

3-143. Rations available for military operations in a cold environment include the meal, cold weather ready to eat meal, the most common individual ration that Soldiers receive during cold-weather operations. It is a restricted ration, nutritionally incomplete and intended for use over short periods, up to ten days. Each meal provides an average of 1,600 calories. The meal, cold weather will not freeze. There is also a compact, eaton-the-move assault ration. It substantially reduces the weight, cube, load, and preparation time, enabling Soldiers to carry enough subsistence for several days for periods of high mobility. The assault ration contains an average of 2,900 calories per day and is also a restricted ration, nutritionally incomplete, and intended for short periods. 

3-144. The nutritional concerns in hot environments include dehydration and inadequate food intake. Individual appetites may be suppressed during the first eight days of exposure to a hot environment. To maintain adequate sodium or salt intake, individuals should eat at least two meals a day. To prevent dehydration, individuals should follow the work/rest and water consumption guide, refer to ATP 4-25.12. Carbohydrate-electrolyte beverages are justified for use in hot environments in specific circumstances. Carbohydrate-electrolyte beverage criteria is provided in AR 40-25. Further details on carbohydrateelectrolyte-beverages are outlined in Technical Bulletin Medical (TB Med) 507. If mission demands support the need, the modular operational ration enhancement, hot weather pack, augments daily operational rations with additional components, providing approximately 1,100 calories per pack. 

3-145. Nutritional concerns in high-altitude operations include weight loss due to acute mountain sickness and higher energy needs, dehydration, and gastrointestinal complaints. If mission demands support the need, the modular operational ration enhancement, high altitude/cold weather pack augments daily operational rations, providing additional components for high altitude and cold weather environments, providing approximately 1,100 kilocalories per pack. 

3-146. Dietary supplements are not regulated and may contain ingredients that can cause adverse effects. Products marketed for performance enhancement and weight loss are two of the most consumed supplement types for Soldiers. Performance enhancement and weight loss dietary supplements may contain stimulants that can cause dehydration, dizziness, palpitations, high blood pressure, stroke, and heart attack. Furthermore, exercise, dehydration, caffeine, and some medications (to include over-the-counter medications) compound the effects from these products, potentially increasing the risk and severity of adverse side effects. For guidance on nutrition supplements, refer to the Operation Supplement Safety (Consortium for Health and Military Performance), the Human Performance Resource Center, and the Sports NCM. 

3-147. Soldiers’ nutritional health can be altered in a deployed environment. Dietitians and nutrition care specialists can promote nutritional health through surveillance of mission demands and ration provision to ensure Soldiers’ nutrient needs are met per AR 40-25, especially if restricted rations are consumed. Nutrition education can be provided based on surveillance of Soldiers’ dietary supplement consumption, adherence to Army body composition standards, dehydration trends, and other topics as relevant to the hospital and Soldiers in the AO. Dietitians may collaborate with other medical providers and chaplains to identify issues or concerns and develop appropriate education campaigns. 

#### **DEFENSIVE OPERATIONS** 

3-148. Defensive operations defeat enemy attacks, gain time, economize forces, hold key terrain, or develop conditions favorable for offensive or stability operations. Although offensive operations are usually required to achieve decisive results, it is often necessary, even advisable, to defend. Defensive operations alone do not normally achieve a decision unless they are sufficient to achieve the overall political goal, such as protecting an international border. 

##### **CHARACTERISTICS OF THE DEFENSE** 

3-149. Defending commanders strive to regain the initiative from attacking enemy forces. This is a primary feature of the defense. Disruption, flexibility, maneuver, mass and concentration, operations in depth, preparation, and security are all defensive characteristics used by commanders to regain the initiative. Their ability to synchronize their operations greatly improves the probability of a successful defense. Successful defenses share some important characteristics. They include:

- Disruption – deceiving or destroying enemy reconnaissance forces, breaking up combat formations, separating echelons, and impeding an enemy force’s ability to synchronize its combined arms. 

- Flexibility – developing plans that anticipate a range of enemy actions and allocate resources accordingly. 

- Maneuver – achieving and exploiting a position of physical advantage over an enemy force. 

- Mass and concentration—creating overwhelming combat power at specific locations to support the main effort. 

- Depth – engaging multiple enemy echelons, enemy long-range fires, sustainment, and C2. 

- Preparation – preparing the defense before attacking enemy forces arrive. 

- Security – conducting security, protection, information activities, operations security, and cyberspace and electromagnetic warfare tasks. 

##### **TYPES OF DEFENSIVE OPERATIONS** 

3-150. There are three types of defensive operations. These operations have significantly different concepts and present different problems. Units consider each independently when planning and executing the defense. Although the names of these types convey the overall aim of a selected operation, most defenses at division and higher echelons combine elements of all three types. The types of defensive operations are: 

- Area defense. 

- Mobile defense (defense of a linear obstacle, reverse slope defense, and perimeter defense). 

- Retrograde (delay, withdraw, and retirement). 

3-151. Hospital planners are actively considering key factors to ensure effective hospitalization support during defensive operations. As part of their TACSOP, hospital centers are developing strategies to provide medical support in various defensive scenarios. Some key planning factors to consider include: 

- The hospital's ability to support different types of defensive operations, such as: 

 - Area defense versus mobile defense 

 - Delay, withdrawal, or retirement 

- The impact of different division types on hospitalization support, including the role of a Role 3 hospital. 

- The feasibility of setting up multiple field hospitals in separate locations, and the considerations for consolidating hospitals when transitioning to offense or stability operations. 

- The priority of hospitalization within sustainment and transportation plans 

- The potential for indirect fire injuries and delayed evacuations, including: 

 - Wound types, such as significant injuries caused by large indirect fire pieces of shrapnel and concussive injuries 

 - The need for engineer support for patient bunkers 

 - Delaying evacuation until it is feasible 

 - Ensuring hospital site selection includes the criteria of elevated terrain features 

- The need for Role 3 hospitals to prepare for a surge of patients when evacuation windows open, including concentrating on clearing beds to accommodate the influx of patients. 

#### **OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS** 

3-152. The offense is the decisive form of war. The offense is the ultimate means commanders have of imposing their will on enemy forces. Army forces conduct the offense to defeat and destroy enemy forces as well as gain control of terrain, resources, and population centers. Commanders may also conduct the offense to deceive or divert an enemy force, develop intelligence, or hold an enemy force in position. Commanders seize, retain, and exploit the initiative when conducting the offense. Specific operations may orient on an enemy force or terrain objective to achieve a position of advantage. Taking the initiative from an enemy force requires the conduct of the offense, even in the defense. For additional information on offensive operations refer to ADP 3-90. 

3-153. The main purposes of the offense are to defeat or destroy enemy forces and to gain control of terrain, resources, or population centers. Additionally, commanders conduct the offense to— 

- Secure decisive terrain. 

- Deprive the enemy of resources. 

- Gain information. 

- Fix an enemy force in position. 

- Disrupt an enemy force’s attack. 

- Set the conditions for successful future operations. 

3-154. The offense supports friendly operations in the OE. Offensive operations destroy, dislocate, disintegrate, or isolate an enemy force. 

##### **CHARACTERISTICS OF THE OFFENSE** 

3-155. Audacity, concentration, surprise, and tempo characterize the offense. Commanders maneuver forces to advantageous positions before an operation. To shape their decisive operation, they initiate selective contact with enemy forces. This may determine the outcome of the major operation, battle, or engagement. Decisive operations capitalize on the successful application of the characteristics of the offense. Successful offenses share these characteristics: 

- Audacity – the ability to assume risk to create opportunity with bold action. 

- Concentration – orchestrating forces or effects to create and exploit opportunity (concentrating effects is called "mass."). 

- Surprise – taking action that catches enemy forces off guard. 

- Tempo – maintaining a pace of operations that is faster than the enemy’s, but not so fast that it cannot be sustained for as long as necessary to achieve all assigned objectives. 

##### **TYPES OF OFFENSIVE OPERATIONS** 

3-156. The four types of offensive operations are movement to contact, attack, exploitation, and pursuit. The types of offensive operations describe friendly force arrangements by purpose. The types of offensive operations describe the general sequence of the offense and recommended formations. The purpose discriminates one from another. The four types of offensive operations are— 

- Movement to contact which may result in a meeting engagement. 

- Attack (ambush, counterattack, raid, and spoiling attack). 

- Exploitation. 

- Pursuit (frontal and combination). 

3-157. Within the context of the maneuver commander’s operational framework and the general phasing scheme, hospitalization operational planning considerations should focus on applying the six AHS principles within the construct of the offensive scheme of maneuver. Some planning factors for providing hospitalization in support of offensive operations to consider include: 

- As part of the hospital’s TACSOP, does the hospital center have templated OPLANS/OPORDS for each type of offensive operation that can be quickly revised during the planning process. 

 - How would the hospital center support a movement to contact over long distances? 

 - What unique hospitalization considerations exist in supporting an attack versus an exploitation or pursuit? 

 - What changes, if any, exist when a Role 3 hospital is in support of different types of divisions (joint force entry, penetration, or standard)? 

- If the hospital center has two field hospital does the OE and sustainment plan support only setting up one field hospital to allow the second to remain packed and ready to move in support of offensive gains and to maintain proximity to the supported force? 

- What hospitalization considerations are required to consolidate gains or transition to defensive operations, stability operations, or back to post-conflict competition? 

- Know the theater evacuation policy and make every attempt to clear beds whenever possible. 

- What priority does the hospital center fall within the sustainment and transportation plans in the offense? 

 - The hospital may have to rely on sustainment for transportation assets to maintain proximity. 

 - Planning for water (potable and removal of gray) and fuel is crucial. 

 - What is the mortuary affairs plan for the hospital? 

- Direct fire injuries may be more prevalent in the offense, how does this affect hospitalization.  Wound types (more direct fire penetrating injuries). 

 - Delaying evacuation until such a time it is feasible to evacuate.
