- When Role1 and Role 2 are conducting prolonged care operations and evacuation rearward is delayed, the Role 3 hospital should concentrate on clearing beds (if possible) to prepare for the large surge of patients when evacuation windows of opportunity open and an influx of patients are evacuated to the Role 3 hospitals. Planning and disseminating triage priorities should be considered if patient demand outstrips resources and supplies, especially when the Role 3 hospital is forced to delay evacuation due to the current OE. 

#### **STABILITY OPERATIONS** 

3-158. At some point during an operation, units transition from one phase of the major operation or campaign plan to another and begin executing a sequel to their previous order. The successful conduct of stability operations may be the decisive operation to a major operation or campaign. The transition to a focus on the conduct of stability operations from the conduct of the offense cannot be an afterthought. To assist in this transition to a stability focus, units continuously consolidate gains. Commanders actively consider activities necessary to consolidate gains while seeking to achieve their end state. It is the final exploitation of tactical success. 

3-159. Stability operations are part of every operation. However, the proportion of stability operations, in relation to offensive and defensive tasks, may change. Whether an operation is a peace operation preventing conflict or a large-scale combat operation, forces will always integrate offensive, defensive, and stability operations. For example, in a peace operation, forces may still perform offensive tasks such as raids to capture war criminals. Conversely, during large-scale combat operations, forces perform stability tasks to control captured areas or provide emergency essential services. For more information on stability, refer to DODD 3000.05 and FM 3-07. 

3-160. Military forces provide support to facilitate the completion of tasks for which the HN is normally responsible. Typically, these tasks have a security component ideally performed by military forces. However, military forces sometimes provide logistics, medical, or administrative support to enable the success of civilian agencies and organizations. These tasks generally fall into one of three categories, representing the collective effort associated with an operation focused on stability. Task for which— 

 - Military forces retain primary responsibility. 

 - Civilian agencies or organizations likely retain responsibility, but military forces are prepared to execute until transition can safely occur with those organizations. 

 - Civilian agencies or organizations retain primary responsibility. 

- 3-161. The primary stability tasks are: 

 - Establish civil security. 

 - Establish civil control. 

 - Conduct security cooperation. 

 - Restore essential services. 

 - Support to governance. 

 - Support to economic and infrastructure development. 

3-162. Medical support and medical capacity building are major contributors to the successful execution of the primary stability tasks, and in some cases may be the primary tools used in shaping the environment, as military medical assistance is often more readily accepted by the affected nation. When conducting stability tasks, hospitalization support should be conducted in consonance with the combatant commander’s theater engagement strategy. Medical planners should be involved early in the planning process to ensure hospitalization is coordinated with the supporting assistant chief of staff, civil affairs operations (G-9), the MEDBDE civil affairs S-9 section (if deployed), and UAP to ensure that all stability tasks conducted are part of the regional strategy for the AO. For more information on the medical aspects of stability operations, refer to DODI 2000.30 and ATP 4-02.42. Hospitalization in support of each stability scenario will differ. In many stability scenarios, the medical element is constrained in size, necessitating that some services (such as hospitalization) be provided outside of the AO. As the medical element is normally task organized for the specific mission, AHS support resources or modules that are normally only deployed with elements of a hospital center may be included in the task organization (even though the full hospital unit may not be deployed). For stability operations, hospitalization planners should consider the following planning factors:

- The optimal location for the Role 3 hospital, taking into account the PAR and METT-TC (I) factors, to ensure effective and efficient medical support. 

- The eligibility of care determination, as outlined in the HSS annex (annex Q if operating jointly), which may change significantly during stability operations. 

- The Role 3 hospital's augmentation of affected-nation capabilities, prioritizing the hospitalization of U.S. armed forces patients while supporting the local population. 

- The status and capabilities of affected nation and UAP hospitals in the AO, including:  Operational status. 

 - Communications systems and compatibility with the Role 3 hospital. 

 - Staffing levels and types of medical providers. 

 - Hospitalization functions and capabilities, such as surgical, inpatient, and outpatient services. 

 - Medical device status and maintenance strategy. 

 - Class VIII inventory and management. 

- The security plan for the hospital, ensuring the safety and security of patients, staff, and equipment. 

- The provision of basic health care and hospitalization support for various groups, including former combatants, belligerents, dislocated civilians, and detainees, as part of the initial response to establish civil security. 

- The maintenance of situational awareness regarding the efforts of other UAP organizations operating in the area, to avoid duplication, encourage synchronization of support, and ensure proper use of all available resources. 

- The dissemination of medical intelligence considerations, including general threat, health threat, and medical intelligence, to hospitalization leaders and staff. 

- The training and knowledge of hospital personnel regarding health threats and diseases prevalent in the region, as well as language, social, religious, cultural, and political factors that may impact the provision of hospitalization. 

- The availability of interpreters and other resources to support effective communication and care. 

- The unit's TACSOP, OPLAN, or OPORD should address: 

 - Expense reimbursement for support to stability tasks. 

 - Reimbursement for medical training or hospitalization services provided. 

 - Approval processes for transferring or issuing medical supplies and equipment to the affected nation. 

 - Coordination with the chaplain or unit ministry team regarding religious-based dietary restrictions for hospitalized patients. 

#### **DEFENSE SUPPORT TO CIVIL AUTHORITIES OPERATIONS** 

3-163. DSCA is the process by which U.S. military assets and personnel can be used to assist in missions normally carried out by civil authorities. DSCA can be used for assistance for domestic emergencies, cyberspace incident response, law enforcement support, and other domestic activities or from qualifying entities for special events. DSCA includes support to prepare, prevent, protect, respond, and recover from domestic incidents. DSCA is provided in response to requests from civil authorities and upon approval from appropriate authorities. DSCA is conducted only in the U.S. homeland. In support of DSCA tasks. Army forces assist in responding to crises and relieving suffering by providing essential support services and specialized resources when the capabilities of civil authorities have been exceeded. For information on the DSCA, refer to DODD 3025.18, JP 3-28, and ADP 3-28. While DSCA tasks may require various types and levels of support, the primary purpose of such missions is to save lives, alleviate suffering, and protect property. The four key Army DSCA tasks are to provide— 

- Support for domestic disasters. 

- Support for domestic CBRN incidents. 

- Support for domestic civilian law enforcement agencies. 

- Other designated support. 

3-164. Other DSCA missions include wildland firefighting, oil and hazardous material response, nuclear and radiological incident support, animal and plant disease response, medical countermeasures distribution, pandemic influenza and infectious disease response, mass migration, and civil disturbance operations. 

3-165. While every domestic support mission is unique, four defining characteristics shape the actions of commanders and leaders in any mission. These characteristics are that— 

- State and federal laws define how military forces support civil authorities. 

- Civil authorities are in charge and military forces support them. 

- Military forces depart when civil authorities can continue without military support. 

- Military forces should document costs of all direct and indirect support provided. 

3-166. During DSCA, hospitalization support requirements will be tailored to meet the specific mission. Determining factors include the—type, severity, and geographic location of the incident; capabilities available within the local community; health threat; magnitude of the operation (local versus state or federal); and the anticipated patient workload. The support provided can range from routine hospitalization (essential care) support for military personnel, to hospitalization forces employed in support of law enforcement operations, and on-site triage, treatment, and hospitalization or evacuation of disaster victims. Specific guidance, authority, and legal advice should be obtained before beginning operations to ascertain eligibility or care criteria for medical treatment and hospitalization, what specific support can be provided, and where funding and/or reimbursement can be obtained. Within the context of the joint task force civil support commander’s intent and vision, hospitalization operational planning considerations should focus on applying the six AHS principles within the construct of providing hospitalization for DSCA operations. For more information on the medical aspects of DSCA, refer to ATP 4-02.42. Some planning factors for providing operational Role 3 hospitalization in support of DSCA include: 

- The hospital center's templated OPLANS/OPORDS for hospitalization in support of the four DSCA tasks, which can be quickly revised during the planning process to address various scenarios, such as CBRN events, natural disasters, law enforcement support, and pandemics. 

- The eligibility of care determination, which outlines the medical rules of eligibility for patients receiving care at the Role 3 hospital. 

- The priorities of triage, which dictate the order in which patients receive medical attention when hospital supplies and capabilities are overwhelmed. 

- The Role 3 hospital's position within the sustainment and transportation plans, including: 

 - Planning for food, water, and fuel to support hospital operations. 

 - Contracts for transportation and sustainment, such as food, water, and fuel, in case of limited deployable assets. 

 - Medical maintenance strategy to ensure equipment functionality. 

 - Class VIII resupply and delivery of blood products, including contracts for procurement and transportation. 

 - Mortuary affairs plan for the hospital. 

- The status and capabilities of civilian hospitals in the AO, including: 

 - Operational status. 

 - Communications systems compatibility with the Role 3 hospital. 

 - Staffing levels and types of medical providers. 

 - Hospitalization capabilities and functions, such as surgical, inpatient, and outpatient services. 

 - Medical device status and maintenance strategy. 

 - Class VIII inventory and management. 

- The feasibility of setting up the Role 3 hospital near an established civilian hospital or in another location, dependent on METT-TC (I) and PAR factors. 

- The deployment and utilization of Forward Resuscitative Surgical Teams (FRSTs), including the decision to keep them with the Role 3 hospital or move them to a civilian hospital with limited surgical capabilities. 

- The hospitalization consolidation of gains or transitions, which involves planning for the transition of patients and medical care from the Role 3 hospital to other medical facilities or civilian hospitals. 

- The Role 3 hospital's support for various DSCA tasks, including law enforcement, pandemic response, and CBRN event response, which requires tailored medical planning and execution. 

3-167. Hospitalization in the generating force consists of fixed military MTFs (Role 4) capable of providing definitive care to conclusively manage a patient’s condition, which normally leads to rehabilitation, RTD, or discharge from the Service. Within the NDMS, definitive medical care begins when disaster victims are admitted to a participating hospital. Once admitted, federal coordinating centers monitor the status of NDMS patients treated at associated MTFs. The federal coordinating centers also coordinate fiscal information to support the processing of financial claims for reimbursement. 

3-168. Definitive care within the DOD includes all the capabilities embedded in the Military Health System, plus extraordinary preventive, restorative, and rehabilitative capacity that does not exist in facilities with lesser capabilities of care. Hospitals within the continental U.S. support base represent the most definitive medical care available within the Military Health System and are expanded to include Department of Veterans Affairs and civilian hospitals to meet the requirements of the NDMS. During DSCA, Role 3 hospitals designated as federal coordinating centers may be activated/alerted to provide this support. For the additional information on the NDMS, refer to the Administration for Strategic Preparedness and Response website. 

#### **RECONSTITUTION OPERATIONS** 

3-169. _Reconstitution_ consists of those actions that commanders plan and implement to restore units to a desired level of combat effectiveness commensurate with mission requirements and available resources (ATP 3-21.20). Reconstitution may include removing a unit from combat, assessing it with external assets, reestablishing a chain of command, training a unit for future operations, and reestablishing unit cohesion. Reconstitution operations include reorganization and regeneration. For more information on reconstitution, refer to ATP 3-94.4 and ADP 3-90. 

3-170. The Army has not been prepared to conduct reconstitution as a part of large-scale combat operations in many years. Role 3 hospitals will play a key part in the theater reconstitution plan. The hospital should focus on returning Soldiers to duty to support the reconstitution plan. Prioritizing treatment should be centered on returning Soldiers to duty within the established theater evacuation policy. Every Soldier will be important to mission accomplishment and every one that can be kept in theater and returned to the unit is one less replacement that should be taken from elsewhere. 

3-171. Reorganization is the action to shift resources within a degraded unit to increase its combat effectiveness. Commanders use reorganization to restore capability and improve HSS effectiveness within a degraded unit. Reorganization is possible at tactical level. 

3-172. Regeneration is the rebuilding of a unit. It requires large-scale replacement of personnel, equipment, and supplies. Medical units also undergo regeneration and are rebuilt through large-scale replacement of personnel, equipment, and Class VIII resupply. Regeneration requires support from higher, is time sensitive, and more resource intensive. 

#### **CHEMICAL, BIOLOGICAL, RADIOLOGICAL, AND NUCLEAR OPERATIONS** 

3-173. The Role 3 hospital is equipped for and should be prepared to establish a PDS in proximity to the hospital. The hospital is not staffed to conduct patient decontamination operations alone and require nonmedical augmentation from supported units. For more information on HSS in a CBRN environment and how to establish and operate a PDS refer to ATP 4-02.7/MCRP 4-11.1F/NTTP 4-02.7/AFTTP 3-42.3. 

3-174. Leaving equipment covered until needed for operations will help protect materiel in a CBRN environment. Hospital leaders should establish a robust CBRN portion to their TACSOP. CBRN equipment should be inspected periodically in accordance with appropriate technical or owner’s manuals and the unit TACSOP. Chemical-biological protective shelters are essential to continue the support role in chemical and biological OEs. 

##### **CHEMICAL** 

3-175. Initial triage, EMT, and decontamination are accomplished on the dirty side of the PDS hot line. Lifesustaining care is rendered, as required, without regard to contamination. Secondary triage, treating trauma patients, and patient disposition are accomplished on the clean side of the hot line. When treatment is provided in a contaminated environment outside of a protective shelter, the level of care may be greatly reduced because medical personnel and patients are in MOPP gear. However, lifesaving procedures still must be performed. 

3-176. Providing emergency services will be complicated by several factors: 

- Varying levels of treatment received prior to arrival at the Role 3 hospital. 

- Combined conventional wounds and CBRN agent effects. 

- Heat-related complications associated with wearing MOPP. 

- Increased numbers of psychological casualties who should be triaged quickly to allow for treatment of those who need emergency management (this includes triaging the worried well). 

- The need to have EMT personnel at the patient drop-off point to conduct triage. 

- The potential of having to triage and provide patient care while in MOPP gear. 

- Reduced ability for EMT personnel to communicate between the various phases of the decontamination/treatment process. 

- The need to provide supervision/guidance to the nonmedical decontamination augmentation personnel from the supported units. 

3-177. Patient protection requires that patients have adequate individual protection. Each patient’s protective mask must be available and serviceable. Role 3 hospital personnel should check all patients’ masks as soon as they arrive. If a patient came from a contaminated area, the mask must be decontaminated, and the filter changed. 

3-178. Protection of medical materiel, especially expendable supplies, requires covers and barriers. All materiel not required for immediate use is kept in shipping containers, medical chests, or under cover (such as tentage, plastic sheeting, or tarpaulin) for protection against particulate or liquid hazard. Protection against vapor hazards may require multiple barriers through which the vapor must penetrate. For example, a situation where intravenous solutions are in their individual plastic bags, in the cardboard shipping box, on a covered pallet, or in a military van container represents four barriers against the vapor hazard. These principles should be used to the maximum extent practical. For more information on the treatment of chemical warfare agent casualties and conventional military chemical injuries, refer to ATP 4-02.85/MCRP 3-40A.1/NTRP 4- 02.22/AFTTP(I) 3-2.69. 

##### **BIOLOGICAL** 

3-179. A biological agent attack (such as the use of bomblets, rockets, spray or aerosol dispersal, release of arthropod vectors, and contamination of food and water) may be difficult to recognize. While such biological agents may eventually produce large numbers of casualties, initial casualties may be initially smaller at the Role 3 hospital. 

3-180. Medical surveillance is essential. Frequently, biological agent exposure does not have an immediate effect on exposed personnel. All hospital personnel must monitor for biological agent indicators such as increases in disease incidence or fatality rates, sudden presentation of an exotic disease, or other sequential epidemiological events. Most biological agent patients initially present with common nonspecific symptoms such as low-grade fever, chills, headache, malaise, and coughing. A higher rate of patients than normal based on the number and location of personnel, known disease vectors, and endemic diseases may be the first, best, or only indication of biological agent attack. 

3-181. Biological agent attack protective measures are the same as the measures for chemical agents when bombs, sprays, or aerosols are used. General protective measures are the same as for any infectious disease; specific protective measures are used once the method of transmission has been identified. If units cannot rapidly identify biological agents, this may require the use of higher levels of MOPP for longer periods of time. Passive defensive measures (such as immunizations, good personal hygiene, physical conditioning, using insect repellents, wearing the protective mask, and practicing good sanitation) will mitigate the effects of many biological agent hazards. 

3-182. Designating a single Role 3 hospital to care for these patients (from a patient care or disease transmission standpoint) may not be necessary. However, if there are a limited number of cases, consolidating them all at one facility maximizes the use of limited diagnostic laboratory and personnel assets. Quarantine of exposed personnel or isolation may be warranted in some cases, particularly with infectious biological agent exposure. For more information on the treatment of biological warfare agent casualties, refer to ATP 4-02.84/MCRP 3-40A.3/NTRP 4-02.33/AFMAN 44-156_IP. 

##### **RADIOLOGICAL AND NUCLEAR** 

3-183. The health care mission must continue in a nuclear environment. Well-constructed shelters with overhead cover and expedient shelters (reinforced concrete structures, basements, railroad tunnels, or trenches) provide good protection from radiological and nuclear attacks. 

3-184. Most protective measures against nuclear attack require engineer or intensive logistics support. This support includes placing sandbag walls around tents, digging trenches for patient occupation, or constructing earthen berms. Occupying existing structures, depending upon their strength and potential flammability, may be the best protection against the effects of a nuclear strike. 

3-185. Hospitals operating in a radiation fallout environment face several problems (which include operating with degraded capabilities) with — 

- The Role 3 hospital being immersed in fallout, requiring decontamination and relocation efforts. 

- Patients from continued radiation exposure. 

- The contaminated environment hindering medical treatment, hospitalization, medical evacuation, and medical logistics operations. 

3-186. Decontamination of most radiologically contaminated patients and equipment can be accomplished by removing outer layer of clothing or brush, wipe, or shake off. While contamination control measures should be practiced for radiologically contaminated casualties, lifesaving medical care should be administered as soon as possible with priority over thorough decontamination. For more information on the treatment of nuclear and radiological casualties, refer to ATP 4-02.83/MCRP 4-11.1B/NTRP 4- 02.21/AFMAN 44-161(I). 

##### **ROLE 3 CHEMICAL, BIOLOGICAL, RADIOLOGICAL, AND NUCLEAR IDENTIFICATION** 

3-187. The Role 3 hospital may assist in identification of CBRN hazards, depending on equipment and personnel assigned. Army field hospitals with polymerase chain reaction technology and appropriate film array may provide field confirmatory identification of certain biological hazards. 

3-188. The Role 3 hospital may assist in field confirmatory and theater validation of environmental samples of CBRN hazards with a CBRN laboratory, such as the Global Medical Field Laboratory or Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear, and Explosives Analytical Remediation Activity. The Global Medical Field Laboratory provides surveillance, analytical laboratory testing, and health hazard assessments of environmental, occupational, endemic and CBRN threats. The Chemical, Biological, Radiological, Nuclear, and Explosives Analytical and Remediation Activity has a mobile expeditionary laboratory that conducts theater validation identification of chemical, biological and explosive materials and other elements that conduct technical escort of these materials. Role 3 hospital centers should coordinate through their higher headquarters for planning concerning CBRN hazards with other laboratory assets available in the OE. For more information on CBRN planning and identification of CBRN hazards refer to ATP 3-11.36/MCRP 1010E.1/NTTP 3-11.34/AFTTP 3-2.70 and ATP 3-11.37/MCRP 10-10E.7/NTTP 3-11.29/AFTTP 3-22.44. 

#### **MASS CASUALTY OPERATIONS** 

3-189. A MASCAL event is one in which a significant disparity exists between the capacity and capability of the Role 3 hospital’s assets to deal with the number, complexity, and location of numerous patients. _Mass casualty_ is any large number of casualties produced in a relatively short period of time, usually as the result of a single incident such as a military aircraft accident, hurricane, flood, earthquake, or armed attack that exceeds local logistics support capabilities (FM 4-02). Formal declaration of a MASCAL situation, which is a top-down directed activity, will likely become the hospital commander’s main effort and will typically require assets from outside the hospital’s direct tasking authority. If MASCAL operations are viewed as part of the area damage control missions, then the medical requirements will be integrated into the plan. For information on the conduct of MASCAL operations at Role 1 and Role 2, refer to ATP 4-02.4 and ATP 4- 02.6.

3-190. To ensure effective management of MASCAL situations, hospital leaders should develop and rehearse their MASCAL plan. The MASCAL plan should include considerations for establishing the station in a noncontaminated environment and establishing the station when a PDS is required to be collocated with the MASCAL station. Planning considerations include— 

- Control and coordination: Establishing a central control element to oversee and direct the response effort, including the release of information updates. 

- Security and access control: Securing the area and limiting access to nonessential personnel to ensure a safe and efficient operation. 

- Communications: Establishing reliable communication links between different areas and with higher headquarters to facilitate information sharing and coordination. 

- Facility layout: Designating and setting up triage, treatment, and holding areas to manage patient flow and care. 

- Traffic management: Creating a traffic pattern that allows for the smooth flow of patients and vehicles and marking routes to different areas to prevent confusion. 

- Personnel orientation: Ensuring that all personnel, including medical and nonmedical staff, are familiar with the MASCAL station's layout, markings, and routes to follow during the operation. 

- Medical staffing: Organizing medical personnel to staff different areas, including triage, treatment, and holding areas. 

- Triage prioritization: Ensuring that triage officers understand the importance of prioritizing care based on the commander's assessment of resource capabilities. 

- Nonmedical support: Organizing nonmedical personnel to perform tasks such as litter bearer duties, messaging, and restocking supplies. 

- Medical logistics: Ensuring an adequate supply of blood and other critical Class VIII items and having a plan in place for resupply or procurement. 

- Evacuation planning: Providing for timely evacuation of patients to higher-level medical facilities, as needed. 

3-191. Hospital leaders should coordinate with the rear command post or units in the support area to receive nonmedical personnel to provide manpower for nonmedical functions. Furthermore, the hospital commander, with advice and guidance from the senior physician, should be the only person who determines the hospital is experiencing a MASCAL. A MASCAL battle drill should be included in the hospital’s TACSOP and rehearsed regularly. 

#### **PROLONGED CARE AND DELAYED EVACUATION OPERATIONS** 

3-192. _Prolonged care_ is patient care for extended periods of time when evacuation or mission requirements surpass available capabilities or capacity to evacuate the patient (JP 4-02). Specifically, a prolonged care scenario exists when a medical unit exhausts a specific capability that a patient requires. Prolonged care is a consideration that medical planners forecast in planning medical support based on the threat and operational requirements. Rehearsals of prolonged care under varying circumstances should be conducted prior to execution. 

3-193. Prolonged care may be a new term for the conventional army, but it is no stranger either. For decades, operational medicine formations have been tackling with what to do with patients when evacuation is delayed. In the past, units have addressed prolonged care in the TACSOP, and they should continue doing so. 

3-194. Prolonged care operations are a set of conditions pre-planned and executed on order at the tactical level that require a unit to hold patients for an extended amount of time. Prolonged care is normally conducted at Role 1 and Role 2. Delayed evacuation is conducted at Role 3 as they are used to medically regulating large numbers of patients. A Role 3 hospital still may be called upon to conduct prolonged care and the tasks become considerations that the leaders forecasts in planning hospitalization. 

3-195. The hospital TACSOP should articulate how prolonged care and delayed evacuation is rehearsed and executed. For additional information on the conduct of prolonged care operations, refer to ATP 4-02.4 and ATP 4-02.6. The hospital leaders should consider — 

- Providing prolonged care if isolated, MASCAL elsewhere prevents displacing patients, evacuation assets are unavailable, or METT-TC [I] dependent. 

- Managing casualty response events that include triage with limited resources and delayed evacuation procedures with an understanding of what principles will guide triage of patients and resources in these settings. 

- Coordinating and synchronizing additional evacuation platforms required to move large numbers of patients from the Role 3 hospital after prolonged care operations cease. 

- Recognizing the potential need for en route care and requesting appropriate assets to execute. 

- • Managing classes of supply consumption, especially Class I, III and Class VIII, will become critical. If patients can’t get out, supplies can’t get in. 

- Conducting walking blood banks to support increased blood and blood product requirements. 

- Developing and rehearsing tactics, techniques, and procedures to address prolonged care and delayed evacuation such as the use of "makeshift wards" for overflow. 

- Planning for the increase in mortality from wounds with the potential of prolonged care and delayed evacuation. 

- Additional training requirements for medical personnel to execute prolonged care and delayed evacuation at the Role 3 hospital. 

#### **PRISONER OF WAR AND UNPRIVILEGED ENEMY BELLIGERENT OPERATIONS** 

3-196. The Role 3 hospital may be required to provide medical care to prisoners of war or unprivileged enemy belligerents. Prisoners of war and unprivileged enemy belligerents shall in all circumstances be treated humanely and protected against any cruel, inhuman, or degrading treatment. This requirement has been reflected in international law, domestic law, national policy, and DoD policies. WII detainees shall be cared for. They should receive the medical care and attention required by their conditions. Health care personnel charged with the medical care of detainees have a duty to protect prisoners of war and unprivileged enemy belligerents physical and mental health and provide appropriate treatment for disease. If injured enemy combatants remain hospitalized, they should receive the same prioritization of care as friendly combatants, in accordance with the Geneva Conventions (see Appendix C). 

3-197. To the extent practicable, treatment of prisoners of war and unprivileged enemy belligerents should be guided by professional judgments and standards like those applied to personnel of the U.S. Armed Forces. For further guidance on medical care for detained personnel, refer to JP 4-02, AR 190-8, ATP 4-02.46, and TC 8-250. 

#### **DIED OF WOUNDS RECEIVED IN ACTION OPERATIONS** 

3-198. The Role 3 hospital is responsible for establishing a temporary morgue for patients who have died of wounds received in action at the MTF and are responsible for handling the remains until transported to the supporting mortuary affairs organization. Hospital staff coordinate with mortuary affairs for remains disposition. Supported units do not bring their dead to this area. This is only for the short-term storage of the remains of those who died of wounds received in action at the Role 3 hospital. For additional information on mortuary affairs, refer to JP 4-0 and ATP 4-46/MCRP 3-40G.3/NTTP 4—06/AFTTP 3-2.51. 

##### **PRINCIPLES GOVERNING MEDICAL DISPOSITION OF DECEASED PERSONNEL** 

3-199. All personnel assigned to medical units should be knowledgeable as to the proper procedure for handling deceased personnel. In the coordination process, handling and disposition of deceased personnel — 

- Human remains are— 

 - Segregated from other patients. 

 - Not moved on medical evacuation resources. 

 - Not evacuated with living patients. 

- A DD Form 1380 _(Tactical Combat Casualty Care [TCCC] Card)_ — - Should be initiated and attached to the remains if possible.

 - Is signed by a medical officer prior to their departure from the mortuary affairs collection point. 

- A certified medical officer will pronounce death and complete a DD Form 2064 _(Certificate of Death)_ or appropriate documents. The cause of death on the form will be listed as "pending Armed Forces Medical Examiner determination." 

3-200. The hospital patient administration section is responsible for reporting the death of a patient to the unit, casualty liaison team, and coordinating with mortuary affairs for the prompt disposition and removal of remains. 

**_Note_** . In the U.S. Army, U.S. Navy, and U.S. Marine Corps, transportation and handling of human remains is a logistics function and not a medical function. 

3-201. Careful contingency planning that provides workable guidelines for the disposition of patients and human remains should be conducted prior to the operation and prior to rotating personnel out of the AO. If biological warfare agents have been used in the theater of operation, redeployment planning should include the health screening of personnel before their movement out of the theater to prevent the spread of disease. Planning should also incorporate close coordination with multinational unit commanders, who have forces in the theater to ensure disease containment. 

##### **RESPONSIBILITIES** 

3-202. Combatant commanders are responsible for searching, recovering, tentatively identifying, and evacuating remains from their area of responsibility. Commanders are responsible for providing or arranging for mortuary affairs support for their personnel. Subordinate commanders at all levels are responsible for the initial search for recovery, tentative identification, and evacuation of all deceased unit personnel within their AO. 

3-203. Mortuary affairs personnel are responsible for coordinating the disposition of human remains. The joint mortuary affairs office acts as the theater central point of contact for coordination for the mortuary affairs. 

3-204. If a contaminated patient dies while being treated in a Role 3 hospital, medical personnel will ensure the contaminated remains are isolated and handed over to mortuary affairs personnel as soon as possible. The handling, transport, and disposition of CBRN contaminated human remains on the battlefield or after removal from the medical system are not a medical responsibility, but a logistics function; however, medical expertise should be sought. For more information on contaminated human remains, refer to JP 3-11, ATP 4-46, and ATP 4-02.83/MCRP 4-11.1B/NTRP 4-02.21/AFMAN 44-161(I). 

##### **<mark>SECTION VIII – HOSPITALIZATION EMPLOYMENT IN SPECIFIC ENVIRONMENTS</mark>** 

3-205. Specific regional OEs have an impact on hospitalization and how it is employed. During the planning process, medical and hospital commanders, staffs, and medical planners should take these factors into consideration. Each regional OE has its own set of challenges for hospitalization and applying the six AHS principles will be vastly different in each. This section details some of these impacts to hospitalization as they relate to maritime, arctic, mountainous, jungle, desert. urban, and subterranean operations. 

#### **MARITIME OPERATIONS** 

3-206. The maritime OE is vast and covers the arctic to Antarctica including littoral regions in between. However large the oceans are, land is what makes the maritime area important to the maneuver commander. The ultimate objective of conflict is typically not to control vast expanses of open water but rather the land within it. A maritime OE adds coordination and synchronization requirements for Army echelons that typically operate and train in land OEs. In a maritime OE, Army forces should be prepared to be the supporting Service. For more information on maritime operations, refer to FM 3-0. 

3-207. Operating in a maritime OE has unique characteristics and challenges for the Role 3 hospital. Planning hospitalization operations in maritime environments requires commanders and staffs carefully consider the OE. Commanders and staffs need to consider that applying an operational framework in a maritime environment is significantly different than applying an operational framework to a land operation. Some considerations to hospitalization operations include: 

- In a maritime OE, hospital leaders will need to be familiar with the joint planning process. 

- Units may need to rely on ship-based hospitalization during initial operations, which requires joint planning and coordination. 

- For site selection, terrain ranges from mostly flat in shore areas to mountainous further landward. This adds wind, tides, and monsoons as a factor in site selection. 

- Hardening of the Role 3 hospital in exposed locations, on small or remote islands, is crucial to maintaining survival and will require extensive engineer support. 

- The Role 3 hospital produces large noise, light, and heat signatures. Hospital leaders should consider locations for the Role 3 hospital that mitigate these factors. 

- In littoral regions, where the best location is for the Role 3 hospital to maintain proximity to the supported force. 

- The Role 3 hospital should be positioned in proximity to navigable bodies of water as the primary means of receiving patients may be over water. 

- Extreme weather plus the corrosive effects of salt water can rapidly degrade the maintenance readiness of medical devices already at the extent of long logistics lines of communications (this includes certain Class VIII and medicine). 

- Due to geographic constraints and enemy activity, communications with higher headquarters and supported forces may be limited, and hospital leaders should develop courses of action to mitigate this risk to maintain situational awareness and ensure minimal disruption of hospitalization operations. 

- The Role 3 hospital places an increased demand on certain classes of supply, which may be difficult to transport, have special storage requirements (for example, refrigeration), and be difficult to off-load in remote island locations. 

- How the Role 3 hospital expects to receive Class VIII and medical device repair parts. 

- Due to the geographical dispersion within the maritime OE, the Role 3 hospital may receive liaisons from supported joint or multination partners and should establish memorandums of agreement or understanding on how you utilize them. 

#### **EXTREME COLD WEATHER OPERATIONS** 

3-208. The Arctic region is significantly influenced by maritime and includes three combatant commands. Extreme cold weather presents a harsh and demanding maritime, land, and air environment for military operations. Extreme temperatures, long periods of darkness and extended daylight, high latitudes, seasonally changing terrain, and rapidly changing weather patterns define extreme cold weather conditions, and they all have impacts on the operational and mission variables. The variability of the physical terrain and weather exposes military forces and capabilities to increased levels of risk. For more information on Arctic operations, refer to ATP 3-90.97. 

3-209. Like all regional OEs, operating in extreme cold weather has challenges for the Role 3 hospital. Planning hospitalization operations in an extreme cold weather environment involves the hospital leaders studying the OE to apply the hospitalization operational framework in an extreme cold weather environment that best suits the supported force. Some considerations to hospitalization operations include: 

- Site selection for the Role 3 hospital in an extreme cold weather environment should include terrain that is relatively flat and, in a location, proximate to the supported force. Use of terrain for cover and concealment and protection from wind and blowing snow should also be considered. 

- Ice or snow covering gaps, holes, or crevasses can become a hazard when thawed as well as when a bog thaws if a Role 3 hospital is setup on top of it. 

- The Role 3 hospital produces large noise, light, and heat signatures. With long periods of darkness, light discipline becomes an important factor. Heat signatures in extreme cold weather are extremely easy to see with current technology. Hospital leaders should consider locations for the Role 3 hospital that mitigate these factors. 

- Ground mobility is typically most favorable during the winter months. Summer poses significant challenges for most vehicles moving off road, while the most challenging period is the spring thaw when ground movement becomes impossible across large swaths of territory. 

- Operations in extreme cold weather regions get more complicated when roadways, seaports, and airfields can become unusable depending on seasons. 

- In extreme cold weather, base infrastructure materials across the region need to have high thermal efficiency, long-term durability, tolerance to repeated freeze and thaw cycles, and resistance to permafrost degradation. 

- Solid medications and freeze-dried material instead of liquids can be used when building the list of authorized medical stocks to minimize freezing, storing, and handling problems. 

- Refrigerated and heated storage areas, such as warming tents, vehicles, and containers, are required for storing liquid medications, blood and blood products that has been collected and processed. 

- There will also be an increased requirement for lip balm, sunscreen, cough syrup, and decongestants. 

- Infrastructure in many austere locations has already deteriorated due to extreme environmental factors and snow accumulation can damage tentage and shelters. 

- Tentage and shelters may require liners, and additional heaters may have to be used when operating in an Arctic environment, to include laundry and bath, eating, and sleeping areas. 

- Class IV construction material resupply for extreme cold weather use may have long lead times. 

- How does the Role 3 hospital expect to receive Class VIII and medical device repair parts. 

- Cold weather injuries will be increased, and the Role 3 hospital should refer to TC 3-97.61 for more information on prevention and management of cold-weather injuries. 

- Medical leaders can prepare for extreme cold weather operations through acclimatization and training. 

#### **MOUNTAINOUS OPERATIONS** 

3-210. Mountainous regions are in all seven combatant commands. Different mountain chains have different types of climates. Some chains are in dry desert regions with temperatures ranging from extreme heat in the summer to extreme cold in the winter. In tropical regions, small to medium mountains are covered in lush jungles with deep ravines that flood during the rainy season. Temperatures in these areas typically remain warm and humid all year. Many of the mountains in Central America and many mountainous regions in Africa and South America located close to the equator have these characteristics. Some mountainous regions have a variety of environments where units have encountered several different mountainous environments within the same AO. In winter and at higher elevations throughout the year, snow covered slopes create an environment with its own distinct effects. Commanders operating in the arctic and subarctic mountain regions and the upper elevations of the world’s high mountains, are also confronted with vast glaciated areas. For more information on mountainous operations, refer to ATP 3-90.97. 

3-211. For the Role 3 hospital, operating in a mountainous OE can be difficult. The terrain, weather, and altitude all playing factor in providing hospitalization to the supported force. Hospital leaders need to understand how the mountains affect operational and mission variables. Limited lines of communication and restricted terrain may force evacuation of patients by air rather than ground. Many factors are related to the environment, hospital, and PAR. Some of these factors include: 

- It may be tough conducting site selection for the Role 3 hospital in a mountainous environment. Terrain needs to be relatively flat and, in a location, proximate to the supported force. Risk may be assumed in being forced to set up a smaller Role 3 hospital footprint due to terrain constraints. 

- Altitude will be an issue in mountainous operations. For more information on altitude acclimatization and illness management, refer to ATP 3-90.97, TB MED 505, and TC 3-97-61. 

- Landslides and avalanches should be considered when conducting site selection. 

- Mountainous regions form their own weather patterns that will impact the evacuation of patients to and from the Role 3 hospital. 

- These same weather patterns will impact sustainment and the delivery of Class VIII. The Role 3 hospital can mitigate this risk by stocking up Class VIII. 

- The mission, AO, terrain, and weather will dictate the radio nets, local area networks, and special purpose systems the Role 3 hospital may require with the communications priority being singlechannel radio and satellite communication whenever possible. 

- The Role 3 hospital may be reliant on the use of retransmission and radio relays stations to communication and maintain situation awareness. 

- In a cold, mountainous environment, personal hygiene is difficult to maintain due to limited water. 

- • The potential for the spread of infectious diseases increases by confined living spaces shared by multiple individuals. 

- Medical leaders can prepare for mountainous operations through acclimatization and training. 

#### **JUNGLE OPERATIONS** 

3-212. Jungles are masses of tropical vegetation that are often tangled, overgrown, and impenetrable climates that very by location. Successful jungle operations require modifications to equipment and tactics as the environment can profoundly affect military operations. Primary, secondary, and savanna are the three main classifications of jungles. Primary and secondary jungles can be further classified as tropical or deciduous based upon the predominate trees. Common land features in the jungle are swamps, savannas, and cultivated areas. The large amount of annual precipitation supports incredible plant and wildlife density and diversity as well as make jungles the most ecologically rich and vibrant environments on the planet. They occur most commonly in the tropical areas of the world, mainly Southwest Asia, Africa, and Latin America. For more information on jungle operations, refer to ATP 3-90.98/ATP 3-90.98/MCTP 12-10C. 

3-213. Role 3 hospital operations in the jungle require unique methods and means for providing hospitalization to the supported force. Hospital leaders need to understand how the jungle affects operational and mission variables. With limited lines of communication and restricted terrain, evacuation of patients by air may be the only method during mass casualty scenarios. Hospital leaders and planners should improvise and adapt modes of hospitalization to best suit the environment. For the Role 3 hospital, operating in a jungle OE can be difficult. Many factors are related to the environment, hospital, and PAR. Some of these factors include: 

- Jungle site selection for the Role 3 hospital has three major factors; is the terrain flat, with limited vegetation, is it high enough or away from streams and rivers due to the amount of rainfall and is it in a location proximate to the supported force. Risk may be assumed in being forced to set up a smaller footprint due to terrain constraints. 

- The hospital and supported force will encounter wildlife that can harm humans. Through the health hazard analysis, the hospital leaders should have country specific antivenoms to mitigate some of these threats. 

- Environmental effects on people are increased in jungle operations and include climatic stress on the human body such as sunlight, dehydration, heat injuries, and DNBI affected by hygiene, diet, latrines, and water exposure. 

- Environmental effects on equipment are high heat, humidity, and water which are key components of most jungles and will have an adverse effect on temperature regulated medicines, Class VIII, and medical devices. Hospital leaders should develop ways to mitigate these effects. 

- In the jungle, casualty rates are higher during offensive operations. 

- Due to the difficulties of ground evacuation in jungle operations, a greater emphasis on air evacuation is necessary so large helicopter landing zones are required that can accommodate a CH-47. 

- The effectiveness of radio communications is reduced in jungle operations. Very higher frequency and ultrahigh frequency radios are limited by dense foliage and terrain that obstructs transmissions. 

- Satellite communication is an effective means of communication when line of sight cannot be maintained but will need a clear view of the sky. 

- Jungle operations are often accompanied by a long, complicated, and potential tenuous line of communication that will impact sustainment and the distribution of Class VIII which can be mitigated by the hospital stocking up and safeguarding Class VIII. 

- Due to the environment and wildlife, the hospital should be in close contact with mortuary affairs to remove remains for those that have died of wounds received in action (known as DWRIA) at 

###### • 

the Role 3 hospital. _Died of Wounds Received in Action_ is a casualty category applicable to a hostile casualty, other than the victim of a terrorist activity, who dies of wounds or other injuries received in action after having reached damage control surgical capability (DODI 1300.18). Hospital leaders can prepare for jungle operations through acclimatization and training. 

#### **DESERT OPERATIONS** 

3-214. Deserts are arid, barren regions of the earth that feature the greatest extremes of temperatures and weather volatility. Successful desert operations require modifications to equipment and tactics as the environment can profoundly affect military operations. There are four main classifications of deserts: mountain deserts, rocky plateau deserts, polar deserts, and sandy or dune deserts. The common denominator of all four desert classifications is the lack of significant annual precipitation. Desert bedrock may have a layer of sand, ice, or gravel over it. Topsoil often has eroded or never formed due to any combination of a lack of water, heat, cold, or wind. Common land features in the desert consist of sand dunes, escarpments, dry riverbeds, and depressions. The lack of annual precipitation and topsoil leads to reduced wildlife and plant densities, often with unique and potentially hazardous adaptations, that give deserts their characteristic barren appearance. For additional information on desert locations, characteristics, and operations, refer to ATP 3-90.99/MCTP 12-10D and see Appendix A of this publication. 

3-215. The vast distances, increased lethality, and rapid pace of desert operations can overwhelm the Role 3 hospital and complicate patient treatment and surgical procedures. The potential for large numbers of patients increases as formations maneuver across wide areas and exchange indirect fire without natural cover and concealment to protect forces. These mass casualty situations can exceed the capabilities of all roles of care without careful planning and coordination. Effectively managing large numbers of patients requires clearly understood TACSOPs, rehearsals, adequate resources, rapid triage, surgical intervention, evacuation from the Role 3 hospital, and detailed patient tracking procedures. Many factors are related to the environment, hospital, and PAR. Some of these factors include: 

- Site selection for the Role 3 hospital should make good use of the terrain for cover and concealment but avoid wadis and dry riverbeds that can become a torrent of dangerously rushing water with little warning from relatively minimal rainfall. The site should be relatively flat and, in a location, proximate to the supported force. 

- Although casualties can occur during all operations and in any environment, the potential for mass casualty events increases during desert combat operations. The open terrain and lack of natural cover and concealment make it easier for enemy surveillance to detect and locate friendly forces. 

- The air stability in desert regions varies due to the extreme fluctuations between day and night temperatures that may impact medical devices, temperature sensitive Class VIII and medicine. 

- High winds occur in certain desert seasons. Blowing sand limit visibility and may impact medical devices, surgical procedures, and cause DNBI health concerns for the hospital and supported force. 

- Desert terrain has many variations ranging from nearly flat, with high trafficability, to impassable mountain ranges. Role 3 hospitals preparing to deploy to specific desert regions should seek detailed information on terrain prevalent in the expected AO. 

- Scorpions and centipedes are prevalent in many desert regions but are rarely fatal. Flies, fleas, mites, and lice pose a significant operational public health hazard to the hospital and supported forces. Hospital leaders should develop ways to mitigate these health threats. 

- Environmental effects on people in a desert environment can rapidly fatigue unprepared troops both physically and mentally and turn them into a patient. Climatic stress on the human body in hot deserts can be caused by any combination of air temperature, humidity, air movement, and radiant heat. This includes climatic stress on the human body such as sunlight, lack of water, dehydration, heat and cold injuries, wind that blows dust and sand. 

- Environmental effects on equipment are heat, sunlight, wind, humidity which can negatively affect temperature sensitive medicines, Class VIII, and medical devices. Dust and sand pose the greatest harm to vehicles and equipment in the desert and will have an adverse effect on certain Class VIII and may damage medical devices. Hospital leaders should develop ways to mitigate these adverse environmental effects. 

- Static electricity is common in desert environments due to the low atmospheric humidity and poor grounding potential of the dry ground surface. It may damage medical devices if not grounded to the desert floor. 

- Due to the environment and wildlife, the hospital should be in close contact with mortuary affairs to remove remains for those that have died of wounds received in action at the Role 3 hospital. 

- Hospital leaders can prepare for desert operations through acclimatization and training. 

#### **URBAN OPERATIONS** 

3-216. Urban terrain provides numerous advantages to the defender. Due to this advantage, casualties in urban terrain are more likely than in other types of operations due to the compressed proximity of forces. In urban offense and defense, friendly and threat forces engage at close range with little space to maneuver. Higher casualties occur among troops on the offensive where frontal assaults are often the only tactical option. Defenders with limited ability to withdraw suffer high casualties when isolated and attacked. The potential for high casualties and the subsequent need for casualty evacuation under difficult circumstances make the positioning and availability of adequate medical resources another important consideration. Additionally, high intensity urban combat and the potential for increased stress casualties require additional units to allow for adequate unit rotations so that Soldiers receive the rest they require. For more information on urban operations, refer to ATP 3-06/MCTP 12-10B. 

3-217. Wound types during urban operations tend to differ from those in other environments. Crush, thoracic, and injuries to limbs tend to be a greater percentage due to hard surfaces, structure collapse, and thermobaric weapons use. Some evidence indicates that head injuries and combat stress-related injuries may be higher as well. Firing large caliber weapons in enclosed spaces may result in internal organ damage. As these injuries demand skills that may not be readily available or addressed in medical training, for example detection of internal injury to organs. Therefore, considering the threat environment is imperative to the Role 3 hospital. 

3-218. A realistic understanding of the risk and the nature of casualties resulting from urban operations critically affect the military decision-making process or where to place Role 3 hospitals. If commanders assess the casualty risk as high, they should ensure proper placement of hospitalization assets within the AO. Some hospitalization considerations in urban terrain are: 

- Site selection for the Role 3 hospital in an urban environment should include terrain that is relatively flat and, in a location, proximate to the supported force. In an urban OE this can be buildings, warehouses, malls, factories, stadiums, parking lots, or parks. Factors for terrain should include cover and concealment when applicable. 

- In the offensive, the Role 3 hospital will need advanced stock of 120 percent modified table of organization and equipment for Class VIII in support areas. 

- Increased casualties will require additional forward-placed Role 3 hospitals and equipment to facilitate hospitalization of U.S. armed forces personnel and secondarily, care for civilian casualties as cessation of major urban combat and eligibility of care determination allows, and care for retained and detained personnel and EPWs, as required. 

- Class VIII will be consumed at a faster rate for military and civilian patients, especially if caused by U.S. forces and the eligibility of care determination allows treatment of civilians. 

- The Role 3 hospital may be asked to assist medical assessments of the impact of any of the humanitarian crisis, such as disease threats due to the lack of potable water, energy availability (electricity, oil, or natural gas) and the impact of the terrain. 

- To what extent civilian Class VIII be used? 

- What civilian medical services are available and operational and the feasibility of their use? 

- What civilian emergency services are still in existence and whether they can be used? 

- What civilian emergency services use radio communications and what frequencies they use? 

#### **SUBTERRANEAN OPERATIONS** 

3-219. Conducting operations in a subterranean environment provide unique challenges for Soldiers and the medical personnel supporting them. While disease, trauma, and behavioral health issues are common in all OEs, there is a greater risk of an increase in the number of patients, severity of wounds, and psychological stressors impacting Soldiers operating in confined spaces with little or no light. 

3-220. The types of traumatic injuries likely are like any environment. However, more so in subterranean operations, the effects of blast and blast overpressure is a unique challenge that Role 3 hospitals need to be prepared for. For detailed medical considerations for units supporting subterranean operations refer to ATP 3-21.51 and see Appendix A of this publication. 

This page intentionally left blank. 

##### **Chapter 4** 

### **Specialized Surgical and Medical Augmentation Organizations** 

Assigned to a TMC or MEDBDE and attached to the HHD, hospital center for C2, there are specialized augmentation detachments and teams that are available to provide additional surgical and medical capabilities to supplement the existing capabilities of the Role 3 hospital, attached augmentation detachments, or forward deployed with the division or brigade combat team (BCT). This chapter provides information on the mission, assignment and dependencies, capabilities and functions, and employment of these organizations. These units are— 

- The medical detachment (prolonged care augmentation). 

- The medical detachment (minimal care). 

- The medical detachment (forward resuscitative and surgical). 

- The hospital augmentation team (head and neck). 

##### **SECTION I – MEDICAL DETACHMENT (PROLONGED CARE AUGMENTATION)** 

4-1. This section provides an overall discussion of the medical detachment, PCAD allocation, assignments and capabilities, dependencies, employment, and organization and functions. 

4-2. The PCAD is a medical treatment capability that on order may be employed as far forward as Role 2. The PCAD is a 63-Soldier detachment that, when elements are attached to a Role 2 medical company, may augment the medical treatment platoon and evacuation platoon capabilities. This augmentation provides additional medical personnel to the Role 2 medical company to provide prolonged care and en route care, when necessary, within the maneuver brigade. The PCAD is not a self-sustaining unit and does not increase bed capacity and should be deployed with or attached to a Role 3 hospital or Role 2 medical company for support. Further, the PCAD is neither staffed nor equipped to provide routine primary care (sick call) functions. 

#### **MISSION** 

4-3. The PCAD provides augmented patient care for protracted periods for units conducting prolonged care and delayed evacuation. It further provides en route care during intratheater PM and provides critical en route care during intertheater PM to strategic support area medical treatment facilities. 

**_Note_** _._ Prolonged care may be a new term for the conventional army, but it is no stranger either. For decades operational medicine formations have been tackling with what to do with patients when evacuation is delayed. In the past, units have addressed prolonged care and delayed evacuation in their TACSOP, and they should continue doing so. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

4-4. The PCAD in coordination with MEDBDE, hospital center, or field hospital (32-bed) leaders should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I and in their unit TACSOP, add additional dependencies as required, and describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how these units will support the PCAD. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

4-5. The PCAD is assigned to a corps aligned HHC, MEDBDE. It may be attached to the HHD, hospital center when not operationally employed with elements further attached to a Role 2 medical company. 

4-6. There are many ways to describe the assignment or relationships the PCAD will have. Medical planners should be detailed toward the command and support relationships required of the PCAD through the orders process. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

4-7. The PCAD is dependent upon— 

- Appropriate elements within the AO for support in personnel services, financial management, administrative services, food service, communications, information management, logistical, mortuary affairs, religious, legal, power and generator, unit and field maintenance, security, and defense, CBRN, and AHS. 

- Medical company (brigade support) for the ground evacuation of patients and to provide medical maintenance and Class VIII resupply when attached. 

- Medical company, ground ambulance for the ground evacuation of patients and emergency movement of medical personnel and delivery of WB, biological, and medical supplies/equipment. 

- Medical company, air ambulance for the aeromedical evacuation of patients and emergency movement of medical personnel and delivery of WB, biological, and medical supplies/equipment. 

- Medical logistics company for Class VIII, and medical maintenance, as required. 

- Field hospital (32-bed), medical battalion (multifunctional) (MMB), medical company (area support), or medical logistics company for medical maintenance and Class VIIIA resupply. 

- Medical detachment, blood support for Class VIIIB resupply. 

- HHC and STB sustainment brigade, HHC and STB, division sustainment brigade, or supported unit for transportation support. 

#### **CAPABILITIES** 

4-8. The PCAD provides the following capabilities: 

- Prolonged care to patients in forward medical units, augmenting existing medical capabilities as needed. 

- En route critical care during patient evacuation, enhancing existing critical care capabilities. 

- Augmentation of critical care services on intertheater evacuation platforms. **_Note:_** The PCAD is not responsible for performing field maintenance on any organic equipment. 

- For security purposes, PCAD personnel are equipped with weapons for personal defense and protection of patients under their care. 

#### **FUNCTIONS** 

4-9. The PCAD is a modular organization that can be tailored to support various medical operations. Typically, the PCAD is divided into sections and attached to a Role 3 hospital or a Role 2 medical company to provide enhanced medical capabilities. The PCAD can be further subdivided into smaller teams, depending on the mission requirements. The detachment is composed of six sections: 

- A headquarters section that provides command and control. 

- Five PCAD sections that can be task-organized to support specific medical operations. 

##### **HEADQUARTERS SECTION** 

4-10. The Headquarters Section provides C2, administrative and logistical support. It also provides, direct supervision, operational planning and coordination for deployment, relocation, and redeployment, coordination for life support and security. All members of the headquarters section should be trained in managing prolonged care, en route care, and MEDEVAC procedures at the tactical level. 

4-11. If all PCAD sections are forward deployed or on order to deploy, the headquarters section should be prepared to assume a direct supporting role to the MEDBDE S-3 PM branch, MMB medical operations section, or corps and division surgeon section. The headquarters section consists of the commander, medical operations officer, and detachment sergeant. Duties and responsibilities aligned with these positions are described below. 

###### **Commander** 

4-12. The PCAD commander should have a thorough understanding of prolonged care, en route care, and intratheater and intertheater MEDEVAC operations to provide overarching C2 or consultation to corps or division level medical commanders, hospital commanders, or surgeon sections. The commander has the overall responsibility for employing and implementing prolonged care assets within the detachment’s AO. 

###### **Medical Operations Officer** 

4-13. The medical operations officer is responsible for communications, security, plans, operations, deployment, and relocation of the PCAD. This officer supervises all operations (including administrative, training, supply, and transportation requirements) conducted by elements of the detachment and is responsible for formulation of the PCAD TACSOP and prolonged care planning factors written in annex F (sustainment, appendix 3 [HSS]). 

###### **Detachment Sergeant** 

4-14. The detachment sergeant is responsible to the PCAD commander for all enlisted matters. This NCO also assists the medical operations officer in supervising the detachments operations (including administrative, training, supply, and transportation requirements). 

##### **PROLONGED CARE SECTION** 

4-15. The PCAD has five prolonged care sections assigned to it with each prolonged care section consisting of twelve personnel, organized as three squads of four personnel. The prolonged care augmentation squad operates alongside Role 2 medical companies to provide prolonged care and augment en route care. The senior officer and NCO within the three PCAD squads serves as the officer in charge and NCO in charge of the PCAD section. 

4-16. The PCAD squad can operate on MEDEVAC, and casualty evacuation platforms, or on intertheater evacuation aircraft. Squads can be further employed as two teams of two personnel, a team being a combination of one officer and one enlisted. 

4-17. Each PCAD squad consists of a physician assistant (65D), a critical care nurse (66S) or an emergency nurse (66T), a licensed practical nurse (68C), and a combat medic specialist (68W). All members of the PCAD squad should be trained in prolonged care, en route care, and MEDEVAC procedures at the tactical level. The senior officer serves as the squad leader and is responsible for the clinical operations of the PCAD squad. The duties and responsibilities of members of the PCAD squad are discussed in the following paragraphs. 

###### **Physician Assistant** 

4-18. The physician assistant (65D) assigned to a PCAD squad is advanced medical treatment qualified and provides Role 1- and Role 2-level medical treatment to all categories of patients in an OE under prolonged care conditions. This officer prescribes courses of treatment and medication when required consistent with their capabilities and credentials. The physician assistant in the PCAD squad will be the primary source of advice to determine the medical necessity, priority, and requirements for patient evacuation, initial emergency care, and stabilization. When forward deployed, the physician assistant may serve as the squad leader or team leader and be responsible for the clinical operations of the PCAD squad or team. 

###### **Critical Care Nurse or Emergency Nurse** 

4-19. When assigned a critical care nurse in the PCAD squad, the critical care nurse (66S) or emergency nurse (66T) provides intensive nursing care to all categories of patients in an OE under prolonged care conditions, and conducts triage, analysis, nursing diagnosis, planning, implementation of interventions (stabilization and resuscitation), and supervision and instruction for enlisted personnel. On order, this officer may provide en route care to patients moving within the continuum of care. When forward deployed, this officer may serve as the squad leader or team leader and be responsible for the clinical operations of the PCAD squad or team. 

###### **Practical Nurse** 

4-20. The practical nurse (68C) assigned to the PCAD squad provides emergency nursing care procedures under the supervision of a physician assistant or nurse to all categories of patients in an OE under prolonged care conditions. The practical nurse may accompany a nurse in support of en route care. 

###### **Combat Medic** 

4-21. The combat medic specialist (68W) assigned to the PCAD squad provides emergency medical treatment and preparation for evacuation of patients in an OE under prolonged care conditions under the supervision of a physician assistant, nurse, or practical nurse. The combat medic may accompany a nurse in support of en route care. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

4-22. This detachment is employed within a corps AO in support of a corps or divisions. The detachment headquarters provides C2 when it deploys as a complete detachment and is normally assigned to the corps aligned MEDBDE. 

4-23. As required, the five PCAD sections can be deployed autonomously. Each PCAD section is comprised of three squads each. Each prolonged care squad is capable of being forward deployed (with either medical or sustainment transportation support) to a Role 2 medical company. When deployed, the PCAD is most effective when its sections are deployed forward in the division AO under the C2 of the division aligned MEDBDE or HHD, hospital center. PCAD squads are further attached to a Role 2 medical company, as needed. The Role 2 medical company provides support functions that the PCAD is not designed nor equipped to provide for itself. See Appendix B of this publication for additional planning factors that may impact employment of the PCAD and prolonged care operations. Figure 4-1 depicts the task organization and composition of the PCAD. 

**Figure 4-1. Task organization of the prolonged care augmentation detachment** 

##### **EMPLOYING THE PROLONGED CARE DETACHMENT** 

4-24. The function of this detachment is to augment prolonged care for the Role 2 medical company with its mix of physician assistant and nursing personnel along with providing an en route care capability with its critical care nurse. The PCAD squad will normally be aligned with the patient holding squad but may assist with triage and treatment as needed. Organic personnel of the detachment, section, or squad prepare the medical equipment set, plan transportation requirements in preparation for operations or onward movement, and coordination with the supported unit to prepare for PCAD operations. The detachment is normally assigned to a corps-aligned MEDBDE with sections attached to division-aligned MEDBDE and attached to a Role 3 hospital and squads further attached to Role 2 medical companies. 

4-25. Mission dependent, in a corps with three divisions, the detachment normally retains two (2) PCAD sections to operate in the corps support area and provides each division with one (1) PCAD section each. The PCAD sections that remain in the corps support area may assist in augmenting intertheater evacuation or provide prolonged care or en route care within the corps rear area. 

4-26. The supporting MEDBDE operating in the division AO provides C2 for the forward deployed PCAD section, and it may be further attached to a Role 3 hospital in support of the division. When a PCAD squad is forward deployed to a maneuver brigade, the supported brigade will provide C2 all support requirements. This is normally the Role 2 BSMC. 

4-27. Once the sections are deployed, the headquarters section may be attached to the following medical C2 or medical control organizations: 

- To the MEDBDE, S-3 PM branch. 

- To the MMB, medical operations section. 

4-28. If no MEDBDE or MMB is aligned with the corps or division, then the headquarters section may be attached to the corps or division surgeon section to augment PM operations. The division surgeon section should outline the C2 and support relationships in the order or plan. 

##### **PLANNING** 

4-29. Tactical planning for employment of the prolonged care detachment is the responsibility of the corps or division surgeon section in coordination with the supporting MEDBDE. Operational and support estimates are developed by the surgeon section along with input from the medical C2 staff at echelon and are used in the planning process to develop prolonged care support estimates within corps and division AOs. All prolonged care operation planning factors should be considered during the initial development of the OPORD and plan. The plan is updated, as required, to meet METT-TC (I) and HSS operational requirements. Though not all inclusive, table 4-1 depicts several prolonged care planning considerations that may be used in determining the full potential and or limitations of prolonged care support. 

###### **Table 4-1. Prolonged care detachment planning considerations** 

- The medical detachment (prolonged augmentation care) (PCAD) has limited organic transportation assets, and plans to move a PCAD section or squad will require coordination with other medical or sustainment units for transportation support which may include: 

 - 12 personnel (per PCAD section) and four personnel (per PCAD squad). 

 - Three trailers (per PCAD section) and one trailer (per PCAD squad) with corresponding prolonged care medical equipment set. 

- The detachment has limited organic communication equipment and relies on supported unit. 

- Prolonged care assets are scarce. There is only one PCAD per corps, plan their use accordingly. 

- Use of en route care capabilities should be thoroughly planned. Medical commanders, hospital commanders, surgeon sections, and planners should take into consideration that when the critical care nurse is placed on an en route care mission that they may not know when that capability will return and <u>could incur additional transportation planning.</u> 

**Legend:** PCAD medical detachment (prolonged augmentation care) 

###### **Running Estimates** 

4-30. To ensure the success of the detachment, the PCAD leaders should maintain accurate running estimates, including those items of equipment, supply, and personnel which enable the PCAD sections and squads to successfully deploy forward with the division and maneuver brigade echelons. The PCAD leaders should identify minimum required stockage levels of each item and take steps to ensure timely resupply before low stockages negatively affect PCAD operations. PCAD leaders should also identify as critical information requirements those items which would require a decision if stockages become low. Examples of the items to be included in the PCAD running estimate may include, but are not limited to— 

- Blood (on-hand and required). 

- Medications. 

- Medical evacuation support (availability and times). 

- Medical device maintenance. 

- Rations. 

- Water. 

4-31. Requests for PCAD support is dependent on the PAR, casualty estimate, and the orders process and is inherent in the operations staff channel. It is difficult to forecast when prolonged care may be required. To address this, when analyzing the corps’ casualty estimate, the medical planner should also consider the enemy’s array of forces and capabilities and discuss with the S-2 (G-2) the feasibility of having delayed evacuation during the planned operation. After considering the planning factors through the military decisionmaking process to determine whether that prolonged care capabilities are required, the corps surgeon (through the corps G-3) requests prolonged care assets from its supporting MEDBDE. If the MEDBDE does not have prolonged care capabilities assigned it will request them from the TMC. The surgeon’s staff section should be simultaneously coordinating at echelon to synchronize the prolonged care within the corps AO. For detailed information on specialized augmentation training considerations, see Appendix F of this publication. 

###### **Basis of Allocation** 

4-32. The PCAD is allocated one per corps. 

###### **Mobility** 

4-33. This unit has very limited organic vehicles and must rely on external assets for mobility to move personnel and trailers.
