##### **SECTION II – MEDICAL DETACHMENT (MINIMAL CARE) (120-COT)** 

4-34. This section provides a discussion of the medical detachment (minimal care) (120-cot) mission, assignment and dependencies, BOA, capabilities and functions, mobility, and employment. 

#### **MISSION** 

4-35. This detachment provides minimal and convalescent hospital care, nursing, and rehabilitative services for up to 120 ambulatory patients. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

4-36. See the field hospital (32-bed) section for descriptions of what an assignment and dependency mean. The medical detachment (minimal care) has many dependencies due to its task organization and mission. 

4-37. The medical detachment (minimal care) in coordination with MEDBDE, hospital center, or field hospital (32-bed) leaders should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I and in include them in their unit TACSOP as required and describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how these units will support the detachment. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

4-38. This detachment will be assigned to a MEDBDE and normally attached to a Role 3 hospital. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

4-39. This unit is dependent upon— 

- The hospital or medical unit to which attached for food service, water distribution, religious, legal, force health protection, logistical services, finance, personnel and administrative services, unit health services, patient administration, medical maintenance, supply (all classes), and unit maintenance for the detachment’s communication equipment and additional power generation requirements. 

- The DCAS for treatment of dental patients held in a minimal care ward. 

- The medical detachment (combat and operational stress control) for treatment of behavioral health patients held in a minimal care ward. 

- HHC and STB, sustainment brigade or HHC and STB, division sustainment brigade for transportation services when single lift requirements exceed the HHD, hospital center or supported unit transportation capability, transportation and equipping for RTD personnel, to include individual clothing and equipment, seasonal outer garments, chemical protection garments, and shower and laundry services not related to patient care. 

#### **CAPABILITIES** 

4-40. This detachment provides— 

- Command and control of organic elements to include health support planning, policies, and support operations within the detachment’s AO. 

- Information to commanders and their staffs on the health and status of Soldiers in their commands. 

- Augmentation of the hospital to which attached to provide hospitalization and minimal nursing care, for up to 120 ambulatory patients and for reconditioning and rehabilitation for those patients who can RTD within the theater evacuation policy or who are awaiting further medical evacuation. 

- Provides 40-cot minimal care ward in support of holding/restoration of behavioral health patients for up to seven days (when augmented with combat and operational stress control personnel). 

- Physical therapy and occupational therapy services for patients. 

- Augmentation of the emergency nursing capabilities of the hospital to which attached during MASCAL situations. 

- Augmentation to the nutrition care capabilities of the Role 3 hospital to which attached to support patient feeding of this detachment. 

- Augmentation to the patient administration section capabilities of the hospital to which attached. 

- Three days of supply for all organic elements upon deployment and during routine operations. 

- Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

#### **FUNCTIONS** 

4-41. The medical detachment (minimal care) is normally attached to a Role 3 hospital and is task organized into three sections: a detachment headquarters, an occupational and physical therapy section, and three minimal care wards. Each section’s duties and responsibilities are discussed below. 

##### **DETACHMENT HEADQUARTERS** 

4-42. The detachment headquarters provides command and command, detachment level administration, supply support and plans and coordinates detachment operations. Personnel of the headquarters and support section provide supply and services to augment the respective sections of the hospital to which attached. 

4-43. The detachment headquarters performs unit plans, coordinates movement requirements, routine and specialized operations, mission-related task organization, and coordinates directly with the Role 3 hospital to which attached. The paragraphs below provide an overview of the primary functions of personnel assigned to the detachment headquarters. 

###### **Commander** 

4-44. The detachment headquarters include a commander (66H). The detachment commander should have a thorough understanding of minimal care operations to provide overarching C2 or minimal care consultation to corps or division level medical commanders, hospital commanders, or surgeon sections. The commander has the overall responsibility for employing and implementing minimal care ward assets within the detachment’s AO. 

###### **Executive Officer** 

4-45. The detachment headquarters include an executive officer (70B). The executive officer plans, coordinates, monitors, evaluates, and advises the commanders and staff in both medical and non-medical areas. This includes employing the detachment, PM, and treatment, organizational administration, supply, training, operations, intelligence, transportation, and maintenance operations within the detachment. 

###### **Detachment Sergeant** 

4-46. The detachment headquarters include a detachment sergeant (68W). Serves as the senior enlisted advisor to the detachment commander and medical training NCO. Responsible for all detachment enlisted personnel, medical equipment/supplies, coordination of key resources and mentorship of the junior medical officers. Primary focus is directing all resupply and maintenance activities. Specific duties and responsibilities of the detachment sergeant may include: 

- Planning and supervising the detachment’s administration, logistics, and maintenance operations. 

- Assists the commander and executive officer in supervising the detachment operations. 

- responsible for the training of the detachment’s Soldiers and provides training and supervision of operational security procedures. 

- Prepares reports of detachment activities and functions. 

###### **Supply Sergeant** 

4-47. The detachment headquarters includes a supply sergeant (92Y). The detachment supply sergeant supervises or performs duties involving request, receipt, storage, issue, accountability and the preservation of individual, organizational, installation, facilities and expendable/durable property and equipment. Operating and maintaining the Global Combat Support System-Army an automated supply system for accounting of organizational and installation property and equipment. Specific duties and responsibilities of the supply sergeant may include: 

- Receiving, inspecting, inventorying, loading, unloading, segregating, storing, issuing, delivering and turning-in organization and installation property and equipment. 

- Operating the Global Combat Support System-Army unit level computer. 

- Preparing all unit/organizational supply documentation and coordinating property and equipment activities. 

- Maintaining the automated supply system for accounting of organizational property and equipment. 

- Validating and posting transactions to organizational and installation property records and updates/maintains supporting document files. 

- Inspecting completed transactions/work for accuracy and compliance with established policy and procedures. 

- Determining required methods of relief from responsibility for lost, damaged, and destroyed property and equipment items. 

- Reviewing and annotating changes to the unit material condition status report. 

- Issuing and receiving small arms; secures and controls weapons, ammunition, and sensitive items in security areas; and scheduling and performing preventive and organizational maintenance on weapons and sensitive items. 

###### **Human Resources Specialist** 

4-48. The detachment headquarters include a human resources specialist (42A). The detachment human resources specialist performs personnel and administrative functions in support of the detachment and advises the detachment leaders on human resource matters. The human resources specialist operates and manages field personnel information systems and monitors system activities. The human resources specialist provides and manages the postal requirements for the detachment. Specific duties and responsibilities of the human resources specialist may include: 

- Preparing and reviewing personnel casualty documents. 

- Preparing and processing recommendations for awards and decorations and arranges for awards ceremony. 

- Preparing and monitoring requests for identification cards and tags and absences. 

- Typing military and nonmilitary correspondence in draft and final copy. 

- Preparing and maintaining functional files per Army Records Information Management System. 

##### **OCCUPATIONAL AND PHYSICAL THERAPY SECTION** 

4-49. The occupational and physical therapy section provides occupational therapy and physical therapy services to the detachment’s inpatients. Personnel in this section augment the respective sections of the Role 3 hospital to which attached. 

4-50. This section provides occupational therapy and physical therapy services to the detachment’s inpatients. The paragraphs below provide an overview of the primary functions of personnel assigned to the occupational and physical therapy section. 

###### **Occupational Therapist** 

4-51. The occupational and physical therapy section include an occupational therapist (65A). This officer plans and supervises occupational therapist and occupational therapist specialist programs within the detachment and in an OE. May serve as physician extenders-direct access providers. Ordinarily, provides services upon referral from physicians and other health care providers. The occupational therapist maximizes the functional abilities of service members. Specific duties and responsibilities of the occupational therapist include: 

- Educating and training to enhance habits, self-care, coping skills, and activities of daily living. 

- Identifying and evaluating mental and physical stressors, teaching prevention and coping skills. 

- Designing and developing splints, special equipment, and their application to disabilities. 

- Using physical reconditioning techniques to maintain fitness, hasten recovery, improve independence of self-care and activities of daily living. 

- Serving as consultant to commanders and the military community providing guidance within the areas of injury prevention and ergonomics for work-site evaluation, task analysis and modifications to the workplace. 

- Conducting functional evaluations of and provide individualized treatment to service members suffering the effects of traumatic brain injury and acute and chronic combat and operational stress. 

- Conducting battlefield unit needs assessments to determine unit mental health status. 

- Providing reconditioning and treatment to increase physical fitness, duty related skills and work performance to maximize RTD as soon as possible. 

###### **Physical Therapist** 

4-52. The occupational and physical therapy section include a physical therapist (65B). The physical therapist plans and supervises physical therapist programs through patient self-referral or referral from a medical or dental officer or other health professionals in an OE. Also serves as an independent practitioner and physician extender, evaluating, managing, and providing treatment to patients with neuromusculoskeletal disorders. Specific duties and responsibilities of the physical therapist include: 

- Primarily evaluating and treating disorders of human motion using physical/chemical therapeutic means, (such as skills, heat, cold, electricity, water, sound, and light, gravity as well as applied techniques of therapeutic exercise, joint mobilization biomechanics, pressure and traction). 

- Assisting the promotion of functional independence, healing and prevent or minimize the disability caused by disease, surgery, or trauma. 

- Serving as consultant to commanders and the military community providing guidance within the areas of physical fitness, physical training, and injury prevention. 

- Developing and conducting training programs on injury prevention and physical rehabilitation for medical and non-medical personnel. 

- The primary wartime role is providing burn and wound care and evaluation and treatment of patients with neuromusculoskeletal conditions to minimize RTD time. 

###### **Physical Therapist Sergeant** 

4-53. The occupational and physical therapy section includes a physical therapist specialist (68F). The physical therapy specialist, under the direction of a physical therapist and following medical referral, supervises or administers physical therapy to decrease physical disabilities and promotes the physical fitness of patients. Specific duties and responsibilities of physical therapist specialist may include: 

- Assisting with the management of the detachment’s physical therapy section. 

- Administering physical therapy treatment to battlefield patients. 

- Performing interviews, tests, and assists patients in activities with outpatient care and treatments or with inpatient care and treatment under the supervision of a physical therapist. 

###### **Occupational Therapist Sergeant** 

4-54. The occupational and physical therapy section include a occupational therapist specialist (68L). The occupational therapy specialist, under the direction of a registered military occupational therapist, supervises or interviews, tests, plans treatment programs, and teaches and assists patients to facilitate maximum recovery by decreasing physical and mental disabilities resulting from illness or trauma, prevent injury, and promote life-style modification to improve fitness. Specific duties and responsibilities of occupational therapist specialist may include: 

- Assisting with the management of the detachment’s occupational therapy section. 

- Administering emergency and routine combat stress or orthopedic treatment to battlefield patients. 

- Performing interviews, tests, and assists patients in activities with outpatient care and treatments or with inpatient care and treatment under the supervision of an occupational therapist. 

###### **Patient Administration Specialist** 

4-55. The occupational and physical therapy section include two patient administration specialists (68G). The patient administration specialists possess the working knowledge of patient medical eligibility programs such as Defense Enrollment Eligibility Reporting System and supervises or performs administrative duties in the detachment of supported unit’s patient administration section. Specific duties and responsibilities of patient administration specialist may include: 

- Augmenting the patient administration staff of the Role 3 hospital. 

- Compiling data and preparing required statistical report on inpatient visits, admissions, dispositions, and other selected workload area within the detachment or supported MTF. 

- Preparing and consolidating medical documents and files authorized documents ensuring proper sequencing of forms. 

- Appling knowledge of medical terminology as it applies to anatomy and physiology. 

- Maintaining a medical record tracking system within the detachment or supported MTF. 

- Processing patients for admission to the detachment or supported MTF. 

- Checking and verifying eligibility for care. 

- Prepare patient and associated documentation for medical evacuation. 

###### **Nutrition Care Specialist** 

4-56. The occupational and physical therapy section include three nutrition care specialists (68M). The nutrition care specialist serves as a nutrition extender to the 65C00 dietitian by providing medical nutrition care therapy to patients and staff within an OE. Specific duties and responsibilities of nutrition care specialists may include:

- Augmenting the nutrition care staff of the Role 3 hospital. 

- Performing basic clinical dietetics functions in the dietary management and treatment of patients. 

- Performing nutritional assessments and screening of individual patients for nutritional risk. 

- Preparing and serving modified and regular food items in the management of the normal nutrition needs, under the supervision of a dietitian or NCO. 

##### **MINIMAL CARE WARD (40-COT)** 

4-57. Three 40-cot minimal care nursing squads provide nursing supervision and management of medical or surgical patients who are ambulatory and partially self-sufficient and are in the final stages of recovery, awaiting RTD, or who are awaiting further MEDEVAC. The focus of nursing management is on an aggressive therapeutic environment which speeds recovery for RTD, or which ensures stabilization and preparation for medical evacuation. Nursing personnel administer medications and treatments which cannot be done by the patient and provide instruction in self-care and post-hospitalization health maintenance. Nursing personnel coordinate with the occupation/physical therapy personnel for rehabilitation and reconditioning of patients. Nursing personnel also coordinate with the Role 3 hospital to which attached for routine and emergency medical treatment needs of patients. Nursing and enlisted personnel monitor individuals who may have been exposed to an infectious agent and require isolation until disease manifests or the individual is determined not to be infected or infectious. When attached to the Role 3 hospital, it falls under the supervision of the chief nurse. The paragraphs below provide an overview of the primary functions of personnel assigned to the minimal care ward (40-cot). 

###### **Medical/Surgical Nurse** 

4-58. The ward includes a medical/surgical nurse (66H). Specific duties and responsibilities of the medical/surgical nurse include: 

- Serving as the head nurse and providing professional nursing care, services, and health promotion to patients admitted to the minimal care ward. 

- Responsibilities may span ambulatory or inpatient medical-surgical nursing and may include administrative or educational responsibilities. 

- Supervision and medical oversight of assigned enlisted personnel. 

###### **Wardmaster** 

4-59. The wardmaster supervises activities within the minimal care ward. Responsible for all assigned enlisted personnel, medical equipment/supplies, and coordination of key resources. Performs medical interventions with a higher technical ability. Principal trainer of squad/section personnel. Specific duties and responsibilities of the wardmaster include: 

- Examining and treating patients under the supervision of the medical/surgical nurse. 

- Optimizing use of Class VIII. 

###### **Health Care Sergeant** 

4-60. The ward includes 2 medical care sergeants (68W). Specific duties and responsibilities of health care sergeant may include: 

- Administering routine medical treatment under the supervision of the medical/surgical nurse or wardmaster. 

- Supervising assigned enlisted personnel medical readiness and requesting medical supplies. 

###### **Health Care Specialist** 

4-61. The ward includes health care specialists (68W). Specific duties and responsibilities of health care specialist may include: 

- Administering routine medical treatment under the supervision of the medical/surgical nurse, wardmaster, or health care sergeant. 

- Responsible for ward level personnel medical readiness, medical supplies, and equipment. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

4-62. The medical detachment (minimal care) is normally employed in support of a Role 3 hospital and attached to the HHD, hospital center for general support. This detachment can also provide ambulatory inpatient support to behavioral health and dental patients with provider augmentation from the medial detachment (combat and operational stress control) or DCAS. The minimal care detachment can also provide three separate minimal care wards (40-cot) for those ambulatory patients expected RTD within the theater evacuation policy or who are awaiting further medical evacuation. Per the orders process, each minimal care ward (40-cot) can deploy independent of the detachment, METT-TC (I) dependent. 

4-63. On arrival in the AO, the detachment establishes contact with the supporting unit to coordinate its support requirements. When attached to a Role 3 hospital the detachment also obtains situational updates and arranges for PM items and augmentation of patient administration, nutrition care, occupational therapy physical therapy, and any other operational requirements support. For additional planning factors that may impact minimal care operations, see Appendix B of this publication. 

4-64. In preparation for deployment, the medical detachment (minimal care) should take every opportunity to train. For detailed information on specialized augmentation training considerations, see Appendix F of this publication. Figure 4-2 depicts the task organization and composition of the medical detachment, minimal care (120-cot). 

**Figure 4-2. Task organization of the medical detachment minimal care (120-cot)** 

##### **EMPLOYING THE MINIMAL CARE DETACHMENT** 

4-65. The function of this detachment is to perform minimal care nursing, occupational therapy, and physical therapy for the ambulatory patients admitted to the Role 3 hospital to which attached and to other eligible personnel as determined by approved plans and orders. Organic personnel of the detachment set up and break down unit shelters and power-generating equipment in preparation for detachment operations or detachment movement, set up the nursing care and occupational therapy, physical therapy areas, and perform routine minimal care nursing and rehabilitation and reconditioning for ambulatory patients expected to RTD within the theater evacuation policy or who are awaiting MEDEVAC and require continued nursing supervision to include those individuals being monitored after suspected biological warfare agent and communicable disease contact. The detachment is normally attached to a Role 3 hospital and provides a detachment headquarters, an occupational and physical therapy section, and three minimal care wards. The DCAS and the medical detachment (combat and operational stress control) can provide appropriate support to augment the medical detachment (minimal care) treatment capabilities, if required. 

4-66. When deployed, the minimal care detachment is most effective when attached to a Role 3 hospital. The Role 3 hospital provide support functions that the minimal care detachment that it is not designed or equipped to provide for itself. As required, the minimal care detachment can be split into three minimal care squads comprised of 40-cots each. Each minimal care squad is capable of being forward deployed (with either medical or sustainment transportation support). The minimal care squad can forward deploy within a corps or division AO in support of a field hospital, medial detachment (combat and operational stress control), or DCAS. 

##### **PLANNING** 

4-67. Minimal care detachment operations involve all the factors that are considered in the initial developmental stages of the medical plan. The medical plan is updated to meet METT-TC (I), and operational requirements. Requests for minimal care detachment support is dependent on the PAR, BOA, and the orders process and resides in the operations staff channel. For example, if a corps’ casualty estimate coupled with the eligibility of care determination and theater evacuation plan determines that minimal care capabilities are required, the corps surgeon (through the corps G-3) requests that support from its supporting MEDBDE. If the MEDBDE does not have minimal care capabilities assigned it will request them from the TMC. The surgeon’s staff sections should be simultaneously coordinating at these echelons to synchronize the minimal care within the corps AO. 

4-68. Operational planning for employment of the minimal care detachment is the responsibility of the TMC or the MEDBDE with input from the minimal care detachment leaders. Operational and support estimates developed by the medical staff, along with input from the medical staff at echelon is used in the planning process to develop the AHS support estimates for medical support at EAB and elements forward deployed in the division AO. All factors should be considered during the initial development of the operational orders, and plans. The plan is updated, as required, to meet HSS operational requirements. Though not all inclusive, table 4-2 on page 106 depicts several minimal care planning considerations. 

**Table 4-2. Minimal care detachment planning considerations**

|---|---|
|•|Detachment has no organic transportation and plans to move a single minimal care ward (40-cot) will<br>require other medical units or sustainment transportation support to move:<br>‒<br>Eight personnel.|
||‒<br>One container (medical material sets: minimal care and physical-occupational therapy).<br>‒<br>Two to three 18-foot by 35-foot tents per 40-cot ward as a planning factor.|
|•|Mission dependent, should plan for behavioral health augmentation when receiving patient within this<br>medical function.|
|•|Mission dependent, should plan for dental augmentation when receiving patients within this medical<br>function.|

|•|Should plan for additional infection control and safety precautions and reporting requirements if<br>mission entails monitoringbiological agent and infectious diseasepatients.|

###### **Running Estimates** 

4-69. To ensure the success of the detachment, the commander should maintain accurate running estimates, including those items of equipment, supply, and personnel which enable the detachment to successfully support Soldiers in the field. The commander should identify minimum required stockage levels of each item and take steps to ensure timely resupply before low stockages negatively affect detachment operations. The commander should also identify as commander's critical information requirements those items which would require a decision if stockages become low. For example, if quantities of on-hand pharmaceuticals drop below minimum stockage levels, a decision to execute an emergency resupply mission or divert casualties to another treatment facility may become necessary. Examples of the items to be included in the detachment running estimate may include, but are not limited to the following: 

- Class VIII to include pharmaceuticals and medical maintenance. 

- Medical evacuation support (availability and times). 

- Fuel. 

- Rations. 

- Water. 

###### **Basis of Allocation** 

4-70. This detachment supports the requirement for all assigned AO minimal care ward cot requirements. For programming purposes, the BOA is 2.6 minimal care detachments per 1,000 Role 3 hospital patients. More specific BOA planning factors are: 

- 25 % of the total WIA/DNBI. 

- 21.5 % of blister; and 55% of nerve) and all communications zone minimal care ward cot requirements (75% of the total bed requirements with a 70% skip policy). 

- To get total bed requirements, minimal care detachment, cot requirements should be added to the ICU and ICW bed requirements generated by the corps and echelons above corps Role 3 hospital. 

###### **Mobility** 

4-71. This organization does not have organic lift capability and requires transportation support for movement. This unit has 181,305 pounds (16,600 cubic feet) of TOE assets requiring transportation. 

##### **ESTABLISHING THE MINIMAL CARE DETACHMENT** 

4-72. The minimal care detachment may deploy using several different types of tents or shelter systems. The type of tentage used is based on the detachment’s modified TOE or common table of allowances. The configuration or layout of the minimal care detachment is influenced by factors such as the type and location of the unit on which the detachment is in support of (Role 3 hospital, medial detachment [combat and operational stress control] or DCAS), terrain, and anticipated patient load.

4-73. The minimal care detachment requires around 4,500 square feet of space to operate while the minimal care squad (40-cot) requires around 1,500 square feet operate (when using 18 by 35-foot equivalent tents). For convenience and additional space, the detachment may choose to set up using multiples of a given shelter system or, if possible, occupy a building or hanger of opportunity. See figure 4-3 below and figure 4-4 on page 108 are examples of how a medical detachment (minimal care) and a minimal care squad (40-cot) may be configured. 

**Figure 4-3. Notional minimal care detachment (120-cot)Figure 4-4. Notional minimal care ward (40-cot)**

##### **<mark>SECTION III – MEDICAL DETACHMENT (FORWARD RESUSCITATIVE AND SURGICAL)</mark>** 

4-74. This section provides an overall discussion of the FRSD allocation, assignments and capabilities, dependencies, employment, and organization and functions. 

4-75. The FRSD is a Role 3 surgical capability that on order may be executed at Role 2. The FRSD is a 20Soldier detachment that when attached to a Role 3 hospital may augment the OR section. When further attached to a Role 2 medical company, the FRSD may provide forward resuscitative and surgical interventions to render nontransportable patients sufficiently stable enough to allow for MEDEVAC to a Role 3 hospital. A **_nontransportable patient_ is an individual whose medical condition is so severe that they require immediate stabilization or surgical intervention to prevent further deterioration and cannot be safely evacuated to a higher level of care without risking significant harm or compromise to their health.** The FRSD provides the capability to perform DCR and resuscitative surgery (often referred to as DCS) within the AO. Patients remain at the FRSD (for up to six hours) until they recover from anesthesia; once stabilized they are evacuated as soon as possible. The postoperative intensive care capacity of the FRSD is extremely limited and there is no organic holding capability. When collocated with a Role 2 medical company, the patient holding squad can provide a limited holding capability for the FRSD. The Role 2 medical company may be augmented with a PCAD squad which brings additional capabilities when paired with the patient hold squad that the FRSD may benefit from. The FRSD is not a self-sustaining unit and should be deployed with or attached to a Role 3 hospital or Role 2 medical company for support. Further, the FRSD is neither staffed nor equipped to provide routine primary care (sick call), x-ray, or clinical laboratory functions. 

#### **MISSION** 

4-76. The FRSD provides forward deployed DCR and DCS within an assigned AO to stabilize patients for further medical evacuation to the next higher role of medical care. 

**_Note 1_** . DCR for the FRSD is a medical process that prevents or mitigates hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy through combined treatment paradigms. It is comprised of early hemorrhage control, hypotensive resuscitation, (permissive hypotension), hemostatic resuscitation, prevention or alleviation of hypothermia and amelioration of acidosis through judicious use of blood products and hemodynamic resuscitation endpoints. 

**_Note 2_** . DCS for the FRSD is the rapid initial control of hemorrhage and contamination with surgical packing and temporary closure to save the life of a Soldier, followed by a short post operative phase and evacuation to a Role 3 hospital. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

4-77. The FRSD, in coordination with the MEDBDE, hospital center, or field hospital (32-bed) leaders should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I of the unit’s TOE. The FRSD TACSOP should include additional dependencies as required and further describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how those units support the detachment. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

4-78. The FRSD is assigned to the MEDBDE and attached to a Role 3 hospital center when not operationally employed and further attached forward to a Role 2 medical company. When attached to a Role 2 medical company, the FRSD has access to— 

- Additional imaging modalities (X-ray). 

- Additional laboratory testing capabilities. 

- Additional blood capacity. 

- Patient holding. 

- Medical evacuation support (via air or ground ambulance). 

**_Note 1_** . Though the TOE states that the FRSD may be assigned to the TMC, this is highly unlikely. 

**_Note 2_** . The FRSD is most effective and efficient when attached to a Role 2 medical company for support. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

4-79. This unit is dependent upon— 

- Appropriate elements within the AO for support in personnel services, financial management, administrative services, food service, communications, information management, logistical, mortuary affairs, religious, legal, power and generator, unit and field maintenance, security, and defense, CBRN defense, and AHS. 

- When operationally attached to a Role 2 BSMC, the brigade medical supply office to provide—  Medical maintenance. 

 - Resupply of Class VIIIA (medical materiel). 

 - Resupply of VIIIB (blood and blood products). 

- When operationally employed at EAB, the Role 3 hospital, MMB (through the medical logistics company), or the medical detachment, blood support provides—  Medical maintenance. 

 - Resupply of Class VIIIA (medical materiel). 

 - Resupply of VIIIB (blood and blood products). 

- The medical company (air ambulance), to provide — - Army aeromedical evacuation support to and between roles of care within the AO.

 - Emergency Class VIII resupply. 

 - Emergency movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

 - Air movement of the FRSD equipment and supplies, when required. 

- The medical company (ground ambulance) to provide— 

 - Ground ambulance evacuation of patients between roles of care within the AO. 

 - Emergency Class VIII resupply. 

 - Emergency movement of medical personnel to support the AHS mission. 

- Appropriate elements of the sustainment brigade, quartermaster company (aerial delivery support) or the brigade support battalion (airborne), infantry BCT (airborne) for — 

 - Rigging when airdrop operations are required (airborne only). 

 - Sling load operations when required. 

4-80. When deployed as part of a multinational joint task force, or supporting special operations forces, medical planners and FRSD staff should consider personnel and equipment augmentation for C2, communications, medical operations planning, additional power generation, vehicle maintenance, force protection, force health protection, primary care and patient administration, pharmacy, patient holding, Class VIII resupply, medical device maintenance, x-ray services, and medical laboratory services. 

4-81. The FRSD does not possess the personnel, equipment, medications, supplies, or administrative capabilities necessary to perform a primary care mission (sick call). However, if requested, FRSD personnel can augment the sick call capabilities of a supporting or supported MTF, at the discretion of the FRSD chief. 

4-82. The FRSD’s mission is to provide forward surgical capabilities. FRSD personnel are assigned weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care in accordance with the Geneva Convention. FRSD cannot assist in the coordinated defense of the unit’s area or installation and are dependent on the supported unit to provide security in accordance with the Geneva Conventions. 

**_Note_** . The FRSD staff members are not trained to perform maintenance that exceeds the operator level on assigned equipment. 

###### **Medical Device Maintenance** 

4-83. When forward deployed with a Role 2 medical company, a contact repair team from the medical logistics company should accompany the detachment. The contact repair team assists the biomedical equipment maintenance specialist from the brigade medical supply office in providing medical maintenance. The contact repair team should remain in place if the FRSD is engaged. 

###### **Blood and Blood Support** 

4-84. The FRSD is authorized four blood storage units which can be set for frozen or refrigerated storage. Each storage device can maintain 40 x 450 milliliter or 60 x 250 milliliter units regardless of setting. Frozen plasma or cryogenic units are stored using the freezer setting. RBC, liquid plasma, platelets, and low titer group O WB are stored using the refrigerator setting. Detachment personnel should be trained and equipped to collect fresh WB during emergency situations by conducting a walking blood bank when the need for blood and blood products exceeds the available supply. Additional blood storage capacity is provided by the supporting Role 2 medical company. 

4-85. Requests for resupply of blood and blood products are submitted by the administration/supply section to the supporting medical detachment, blood support, or other blood supply unit as designated by the combatant command joint blood program officer. 

4-86. The medical detachment (blood support) provides collection, manufacturing, storage, and distribution of blood and blood products to medical units operating at brigade, EAB, and to other services as required. The medical detachment (blood support) can deploy a collection storage and distribution team or a collection manufacturing and distribution team to collect WB from available donor pool or assist with blood distribution to MTFs. For additional information on blood support and medical maintenance, refer to ATP 4-02.1. 

###### **Disposition of Remains** 

4-87. If a patient dies in the care of the FRSD while forward deployed with a Role 2 MTF, a DD Form 1380 must be completed then signed by a physician. Coordination is then made with the Role 2 medical company and the deceased is immediately removed from the FRSD facility to the supported MTF’s temporary morgue for further processing. The supported MTF is responsible for notifying the deceased’s unit and the mortuary affairs coordinator for the coordination, movement, and transport of human remains to the designated mortuary affairs collection point. 

#### **CAPABILITIES** 

4-88. A standardized, rapidly deployable, networked, self-mobile, modular, and scalable resuscitative and surgical team capable of supporting short (less than 72 hours) and extended (greater than 72 hours) operations, including the ability to support split operations. The team is modular and scalable, with the ability to provide emergency treatment to receive, triage, and prepare incoming patients for surgery; and provide the required surgery and continued postoperative care. 

4-89. A complete FRSD consisting of the following components: administration, supply, two surgical and two resuscitative sections (20 personnel). In this configuration, the FRSD provides emergency treatment to receive, triage, and prepare 30 incoming patients for surgery over a 72-hour period. This provides the required surgery and continues postoperative care for critically wounded and injured patients with organic medical equipment sets (MES). Postoperative care can manage eight patients over six hours postsurgery. 

4-90. Two forward resuscitative and surgical squads (FRSS) capable of supporting split operations. Each FRSS consists of administration and supply, resuscitative, and surgical teams (10 personnel). In this FRSS configuration, the FRSD provides emergency treatment to receive, triage, and prepare 12 incoming patients for surgery over a 72-hour period; provides the required surgery and continued postoperative care for critically wounded or injured patients with organic MES. Postoperative care can manage four patients over six hours postsurgery. 

4-91. Two surgical teams, capable of supporting very short duration (24-hours) operations, consisting of only a surgical team (six personnel). In its smallest configuration, the single surgical team provides emergency treatment to receive, triage, prepare four incoming patients for surgery, and conduct the required surgery. The surgical team has a limited postoperative care capability for patients over a period of 24-hours. 

4-92. Urgent initial surgery for otherwise nontransportable patients, primarily when attached to a Role 2 MTF to maximize health system synergies (for example, access to X-ray; ancillary support; patient holding; and proximity to evacuation modalities), or when collocated with another organization capable of meeting its support requirements. 

4-93. Health service support operations for both short (less than 72 hours) and extended (greater than72 hours) duration missions dependent on METT-TC (I) if it achieves its personnel work rest cycles and gains associated dependency support. 

4-94. Postoperative acute nursing care for up to eight patients simultaneously for up to six hours prior to further patient evacuation. 

4-95. Technical advice and assistance to the supported unit surgeon and the surgeon section or medical operations center for the surgical services portion of the supported unit plans and policies. 

4-96. Surgical augmentation of the of Role 3 hospital’s surgical capability. 

4-97. Individuals of this organization are provided weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under their care. 

##### **DAMAGE CONTROL RESUSCITATION AND SURGERY** 

4-98. When forward deployed, the FRSD provides DCR and DCS capabilities to the Role 2 medical company. The following discussion describes some (but not all) of the most common resuscitative and surgical procedures performed to reduce morbidity and mortality of injured Soldiers and maximize patient outcomes. 

###### **Damage Control Resuscitation** 

4-99. DCR refers to those rapidly implemented medical interventions that preventor mitigate a patient’s irreversible physiologic deterioration. These interventions include but are not limited to: 

- Rapid hemorrhage control. 

- Hypotensive resuscitation (permissive hypotension). 

- Rapid replacement of circulating volume. 

- Prevention or treatment of hypothermia and correction of acidosis through use of blood products and medications. 

- Ongoing resuscitation in the post-op recovery area. 

###### **Damage Control Surgery** 

4-100. DCS refers to a series of rapidly implemented surgical interventions that include but are not limited to: 

- Laparotomy for control of trauma induced internal bleeding and enteric spillage. 

- Abbreviated thoracotomy for penetrating chest injury to control trauma-induced internal bleeding. 

- • Placement of external fixators. 

- Temporary restoration of blood flow to an injured limb using vascular shunts. 

- Rapid amputation of mangled limbs. 

- Fasciotomy. 

- Decompression of cardiac tamponade. 

- Emergency decompressive craniotomy. 

#### **FUNCTIONS** 

4-101. The FRSD’s modular design and organization enables its staff to provide a tailored forward resuscitative and surgical support package based on the type and number of operations being conducted. The detachment’s design provides a flexibility that allows it to be employed as a complete detachment, forward resuscitative and surgical sections, or as individual surgical teams. This modular design, however, limits what the detachment and its subsets can accomplish without being attached to a Role 2 medical when operationally employed. 

##### **DETACHMENT** 

4-102. The organizational construct of a complete FRSD includes an administration/supply section, a resuscitative section, and a surgical section. Figure 4-5 on page 113 depicts a complete FRSD using approved AHS military symbols. 

4-103. In this configuration, the detachment is designed and equipped to receive, triage, provide emergency treatment, and prepare up to 30 incoming patients for DCR and/or DCS over a 72-hour period using its organic medical equipment set. The detachment can provide post-operative care for no more than eight (8) patients at one time. The FRSD can monitor a post-operative patient for up to six hours after which the patient is generally determined to be sufficiently stable to survive evacuation to the next role of care. 

##### **SECTION AND SQUAD** 

4-104. There are situations when it may be necessary for the FRSD to provide forward resuscitative and surgical support for multiple units conducting concurrent operations. To meet this requirement, the detachment is staffed and equipped to divide the administration/supply section, the resuscitative section, and the surgical section evenly to form two (2) composite FRSS thus establishing a split FRSD. Figure 4-6 on page 114 depicts a split FRSD. 

4-105. In this FRSS configuration, the detachment is designed and equipped to receive, triage, provide emergency treatment, and prepare up to 12 incoming patients for DCR and/or DCS over a 72-hour period using its organic medical equipment set. The FRSS can monitor a post-operative patient for up to six hours after which the patient is generally determined to be sufficiently stable to survive evacuation to the next role of care. The FRSS can provide post-operative care for no more than four patients at one time. 

##### **TEAM** 

4-106. The team is the smallest element within the detachment and constitutes the fundamental building block on which the FRSD is built. Based on this design, the detachment chief can, under certain circumstances send a single resuscitative or surgical team forward to provide limited forward resuscitation and surgical support. 

4-107. In this configuration, the team is designed and equipped to receive, triage, provide emergency treatment, and prepare up to four incoming patients for DCR and/or DCS. The team can provide limited postoperative care no more than four patients for up to 24-hours using its organic medical equipment set. Figure 4-5 depicts a complete FRSD using approved AHS military symbols. The resuscitation team can assist with triage and resuscitation at the Role 2. Figure 4-6 on page 114 depicts a split FRSD using approved operational medicine military symbols. 

**_Note 1._** As written in Section One of the FRSD’s TOE, the terms, elements, teams, and sections are used interchangeably. To avoid confusion, this publication specifically points out the type of element discussed, for example, detachment, section, or team. 

**_Note 2._** The FRSD MES should be functional packed to support sending a single six-person surgical team.

##### **ADMINISTRATION/SUPPLY SECTION** 

4-108. The administration and supply section provides unit-level administration, supplies management, maintenance, and operational planning support. Specific duties and responsibilities of the field medical assistant and detachment sergeant include management of the routine operating issues of the detachment and keeping the FRSD chief apprised of— 

- Ongoing tactical operations. 

- Requirements to displace. 

- Movement considerations. 

- Reestablishment of the surgical facility. 

- Status of organization and medical supply or resupply. 

- Plan for FRSD current and future operations. 

- Status of individual and unit training. 

- Status of communications connectivity. 

4-109. As the detachment is dependent upon its supporting Role 2 MTF for a significant share of its administrative and logistical requirements, continuous coordination between the FRSD and the Role 2 medical company is required to ensure that shortfalls in support do not adversely impact patient care. 

4-110. When operationally employed, the administration/supply section enables the detachment to concentrate on its core mission of providing DCR and DCS. When the FRSD is required to split, the field medical assistant will fall in on one of the forward surgical sections and the detachment sergeant on the other, so that each section has an administration and supply point of contact. 

##### **RESUSCITATIVE SECTION** 

4-111. The resuscitative section manages trauma patients through trauma resuscitation (blood products, parenteral fluids, advanced airway management, intravenous, intraosseous infusion, and central line placement). The resuscitative section can also provide limited ancillary services with point of care laboratory assay measurement and imaging modalities to assist with initial assessment and ongoing patient management and treatment. Personnel assigned to the forward resuscitative section provide— 

- Trauma resuscitation (for example, blood and blood products, parenteral fluids). 

- Point of care laboratory assay measurements. 

- Imaging modalities (ultrasound). 

- Assistance with initial assessment and ongoing patient management and treatment. 

- • Infection control. 

- Pre-and postoperative care that includes: 

 - Blood and blood product management. 

 - Advanced airway management. 

 - Intravenous, intraosseous, and central line placement. 

- Continuation of trauma resuscitation using— 

 - Blood and blood products. 

 - Parenteral fluids and medications. 

- Critical care services that include: 

 - Mechanical ventilation. 

 - Advanced airway management. 

- Postoperative recovery care, which includes: 

 - Pain management. 

 - Pulmonary therapy. 

 - Fluid resuscitation. 

4-112. The following paragraphs provide an overview of the primary functions of personnel assigned to the resuscitative section. This includes both officers and enlisted personnel. 

###### **Emergency Physician** 

4-113. The resuscitative section includes two emergency physicians (62A). Their immediate decisions and actions are intended to stabilize the patient until surgical intervention is available. Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Providing— 

 - Initial examinations. 

 - Evaluations. 

 - Diagnostic measures. 

 - Treatment. 

 - Stabilization. 

 - Resuscitation. 

- Providing clinical management of— 

 - Trauma. 

 - Altered levels of consciousness. 

 - Acute sicknesses. 

 - Toxic exposures. 

- Obtaining intravascular access. 

- Draining blood or air from the chest cavity. 

- Reducing and splinting fractures. 

- Performing limited ultrasound examinations for diagnostic purposes. 

###### **Emergency Nurse** 

4-114. The resuscitation section includes two emergency nurses (66T). Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Caring for patients during the initial phase of hospitalization and treatment. 

- Providing trauma and emergency care to all categories of patients. 

- Practicing in settings in which patients require extremely complex and rapid assessment, highintensity therapies and interventions, and continuous nursing vigilance. 

- Conducting thorough assessments and performing life-saving interventions as required. 

- Performing point-of-care testing. 

- Operating life-support equipment 

- Providing ongoing sedation and analgesia of patients, as required. 

- Performing operator-level maintenance of medical equipment in accordance with appropriate technical manuals and manufacturer’s instructions. 

###### **Emergency Care Sergeant** 

4-115. The resuscitation section includes two emergency care sergeants (68W). The emergency care sergeant provides emergency medical treatment and assists with triage, preoperative, and postoperative care of FRSD patients. Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Administering emergency and routing medical treatment. 

- Assisting with outpatient care and treatment. 

- Performing point-of-care testing. 

- Performing operator-level maintenance on medical equipment in accordance with appropriate technical manuals and manufacturer’s instructions. 

##### **SURGICAL SECTION** 

4-116. The surgical section provides surgical services including anesthesia services, infection control, and DCS as well as postoperative care that includes continuing trauma resuscitation (blood products, parenteral fluids, advanced airway management, intravenous, intraosseous infusion, and central line placement), and critical care services management of mechanical ventilation, advanced wound management, and postoperative recovery care (pain management, pulmonary therapy, and fluid resuscitation). To enable the most efficient surgical throughput, each surgical section is designed to be supported by a resuscitation section. A surgical section can be employed separately from the FRSD for very short duration operation (24-hours) as a FRSS, normally conducted in support of special operations forces missions. The surgical section can provide limited ancillary services to include point of care laboratory assay measurement and imaging modalities to assist with ongoing assessment and to guide further treatments and interventions. 

4-117. Personnel assigned to the forward surgical section provide— 

- Anesthesia services. 

- Damage control surgery. 

- Infection control. 

- Postoperative care that includes: 

- Advanced airway management. 

- Intravenous and intraosseous infusions and central line placement. 

- Continuing trauma resuscitation using— 

 - Blood and blood product management. 

 - Parenteral fluids and medications. 

 - Advanced airway management. 

 - Intravenous, intraosseous, and central line placement. 

- Critical care services that include: 

 - Mechanical ventilation. 

 - Advanced airway management. 

 - Postoperative recovery care, which includes: 

 - Pain management. 

 - Pulmonary therapy. 

 - Fluid resuscitation. 

4-118. The following paragraphs provide an overview of the primary functions of personnel assigned to the surgical section. This includes both officers and enlisted personnel. 

###### **General Surgeon** 

4-119. The surgical section includes two general surgeons (61J), with the senior general surgeon normally assuming the FRSD chief responsibilities. Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Examining, diagnosing, treating, or prescribing courses of treatment. 

- Performing surgery for patients with injuries or disorders that require surgical intervention. 

###### **Orthopedic Surgeon** 

4-120. The surgical section includes two orthopedic surgeons (61M). Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Examining, diagnosing, treating, or prescribing courses of treatment. 

- Performing surgery for patients having disorders, malformations, diseases, and injuries of the musculoskeletal system. 

###### **Critical Care Nurse** 

4-121. The surgical section includes two critical care nurses (66S). Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Provide intensive nursing care to all categories of operational patients. 

- Conduct thorough assessments and taking immediate lifesaving action as required. 

- Performing— 

 - Point-of-care testing. 

 - Operator-level maintenance of medical equipment in accordance with appropriate technical manuals and manufacturer’s instructions. 

###### **Nurse Anesthetist** 

4-122. The surgical section includes two nurse anesthetists (66F). Specific duties and responsibilities include administering general or regional anesthesia for: 

- Surgical procedures. 

- Diagnostic procedures. 

- Therapeutic procedures. 

- Respiratory care. 

- Cardiopulmonary resuscitation. 

- Pain management. 

- Fluid therapy. 

###### **Operating Room Noncommissioned Officer** 

4-123. The surgical section includes one OR NCO and one OR specialist (68D). The operating room specialist assists the nursing staff in preparing the patient and the OR environment for surgery and for assisting the surgical staff during procedures. They also operate the CMS and are responsible for preparing and maintaining sterile medical supplies and special equipment for the FRSD. The operating room specialist also assists in the management of FRSD’s OR tables. Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Assisting the nursing staff in preparing patients and the ORs for surgery. 

- Supervising maintenance programs in the OR and the CMS. 

- Monitoring the quality of sterilization techniques to ensure adherence to established standards. 

- Establishing stock levels for requisitioning supplies and equipment and supervise their storage and issue. 

- Preparing and maintaining various reports and files. 

- Supervising and advising subordinate personnel. 

- Creating and maintaining sterile fields. 

- Draping patients. 

- Preparing, manipulating, and delivering surgical instruments and equipment. 

- Accounting for all instruments, needles, sponges, and medications placed within the sterile field. 

- Cleaning and sterilizing surgical instruments. 

###### **Practical Nurse Noncommissioned Officer and Practical Nursing Specialist** 

4-124. The surgical section includes one practical nurse NCO and one practical nursing specialist (68C). The practical nursing specialist supervises or performs preventive, therapeutic, and emergency nursing care procedures under supervision of a physician, nurse, or NCO. Specific duties and responsibilities may include: 

- Providing preventive, therapeutic, and emergency nursing care procedures under the supervision of a physician or nurse. 

- Administering point-of-care testing. 

- Performing operator-level maintenance on medical equipment in accordance with appropriate technical manuals and manufacturer’s instructions. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

4-125. Forward resuscitative and surgical detachments are normally employed in BCTs based on one per maneuver brigade. They are normally attached to an HHD, hospital center for general support. When operationally employed, FRSDs are attached to Role 2 medical companies. The FRSD may also be a part of a medical task force in support of special operation forces missions. An FRSD deployed forward from the Role 3 hospital is dependent on the Role 2 medical company they are attached to for X-ray, medical laboratory, patient hold, Class VIIIA resupply, Class VIIIB blood support (to include additional blood storage), and medical device maintenance. 

4-126. On arrival in the AO, the FRSD establishes contact with the supporting unit to coordinate its support requirements. They also obtain situational updates and arrange for x-ray, medical laboratory, medical records administration, PM items, and operational requirements support. See Appendix B of this publication for additional planning factors that may impact employment of the FRSD and forward surgical operations. 

4-127. In preparation for deployment, the FRSD should take every opportunity to train. For detailed information on specialized augmentation training considerations, see Appendix F of this publication. 

##### **EMPLOYING THE DETACHMENT** 

4-128. When deployed, the FRSD is generally attached to an HHD, hospital center with duty at the field hospital (32-bed) when not operationally employed at the Role 2 brigade support medical company. When supporting a maneuver brigade, the FRSD is most effective when attached its Role 2 medical company. The Role 2 medical company provides support and medical functions that the FRSD is not designed or equipped to provide for itself. 

4-129. If necessary, the detachment can be split into two FRSSs composed of 10 Soldiers each. Each FRSS can support operations in two geographically dispersed locations. This may be a single brigade operating in two different areas or two separate battalions in contact. Splitting the FRSD should be no more than 72 hours. 

4-130. Under certain circumstances, The FRSD can forward deploy a six Soldier surgical team for very short period (no more than 24-hours). An example would be to complete up to four urgent surgical cases in a 24hour period. The question should be, is this an appropriate use of this surgical capability. 

4-131. Modularly, and in line with the flexibility AHS principle, the FRSD may deploy a three-person resuscitative team (for example, to provide triage and resuscitation at the Role 2 or in support of a battalion as a main effort to provide additional triage and emergency care to a Role 1 battalion aid station). This tactic, technique, and procedure should be thoroughly planned as there is only one MES, FRSD and this team would require specific Class VIII to accomplish this. The FRSD provides the greatest capacity of forward resuscitation and surgical interventions when it is complete. Medical planners and FRSD leaders should note that splitting FRSD capabilities places an additional burden on sustainment and lowers resuscitative and surgical capacity. Consideration for the overall forward surgical mission should be considered as well if the removal of the resuscitative team would take away from the surgical capability and capacity based off the casualty estimate. If it is decided to forward deploy a resuscitative team, like forward deploying a surgical team, the team should only operate for no more than a 24-hour period before returning to the FRSD. 

4-132. By design, the FRSD should be forward deployed with a Role 2 medical company. When an FRSD is not attached and forward deployed to a Role 2 medical company may serve in two main support roles: 

- Stay with the Role 3 hospital with its personnel augmenting the surgical staff with its vehicles and equipment remaining packed and ready to forward deploy with a Role 2 medical company. 

- Stay with the Role 3 hospital and serve as the surgical capability of the Role 3 hospital as its moves forward to remain in proximity of the supported maneuver force. 

##### **PLANNING** 

4-133. Requests for FRSD support and the tasking authority that directs the support resides in the operations staff channel. For example, if a BCT surgeon determines surgical support is required, the BCTs S-3 requests that support from the division G-3, who may then request it from the corps G-3, who then tasks the MEDBDE for an FRSD to be temporarily attached to the BCT. The surgeon’s staff sections should be simultaneously coordinating at these echelons to synchronize the FRSD’s support to the BCT. 

4-134. Prior to (when possible) or upon arrival in the AO, the FRSD chief either directly or through the detachment’s administration/supply section contacts the supporting or supported unit to communicate the detachments support requirements. The detachment’s administration/supply section should also obtain situational updates and coordinate for X-ray, medical laboratory, medical records, patient administration support, and replacement of PM items as well as force protection requirements. 

4-135. Operational planning for employment of the FRSD is the responsibility of the MEDBDE with input from the FRSD’s chief, field medical assistant, and detachment sergeant. Operational and support estimates developed by the medical staff, along with input from the brigade medical staff is used in the planning process to develop the AHS support estimates for medical support at EAB and forward deployed forces at brigade and below. All factors should be considered during the initial development of the operations plan. The plan is updated, as required, to meet HSS operational requirements. The brigade surgeon’s cell is responsible for planning the employment of the airborne or air assault FRSD. 

4-136. Forward resuscitative and surgical detachment operations involve all the factors that are considered in the initial developmental stages of the medical plan. The medical plan is updated to meet METT-TC (I), and operational requirements. Though not all inclusive, table 4-3 depicts several FRSD planning considerations. 

###### **Table 4-3. Forward resuscitative and surgical planning considerations** 

- Detachment requires a minimum of two hours to set up and become functional. 

- Two operating tables per detachment. 

- Average surgical time per patient is 135 minutes (not including post-operative recovery). 

- Rest cycle planning consideration for each operating room table is six hours for every 24-hour cycle. 

- Maximum caseload per 24-hours equals 10 cases (medical equipment set can only support a combination of 30 resuscitation and surgical cases without resupply). 

- Should not begin surgery unless sufficient time to safely begin and complete necessary procedures and permit adequate post-operative recovery time for patient to survive evacuation (approximately six hours). 

- Post-operative care for up to six hours with eight patients. 

- The detachment should be relieved or rested and resupplied after 72 hours. 

###### **Running Estimates** 

4-137. To ensure the success of the detachment, the FRSD chief should maintain accurate running estimates, including those items of equipment, supply, and personnel which enable the detachment to successfully support Soldiers in the field. The FRSD chief should identify minimum required stockage levels of each item and take steps to ensure timely resupply before low stockages negatively affect FRSD operations. The FRSD chief should also identify as critical information requirements those items which would require a decision if stockages become low. For example, if quantities of on-hand blood units drop below minimum stockage levels, a decision to execute an emergency resupply mission or divert casualties to another treatment facility may become necessary. Examples of the items to be included in the FRSD running estimate may include, but are not limited to:

- Blood (on-hand and required). 

- Medications. 

- Surgical supplies. 

- Central materiel services support. 

- Medical evacuation support (availability and times). 

- Medical device maintenance. 

- Vehicle maintenance. 

- Fuel. 

- Rations. 

- Water. 

###### **Basis of Allocation** 

4-138. The BOA for the FRSD is one per committed BCT and one per BCT assigned to conducting stability and reconstruction operations. 

4-139. For the airborne FRSD the BOA is one per committed infantry BCT (airborne), one per special forces group (airborne), maximum of three, one per two committed airborne BCT in lesser contingency, and one per airborne BCT in joint forced entry package. 

###### **Mobility** 

4-140. Detachments with well-trained and rehearsed personnel can transport 100 percent of their personnel and equipment in a single lift using organic vehicle assets after the equipment has been consolidated, crossleveled, and loaded onto vehicles using proven load plans. 

##### **DISPLACEMENT AND REDEPLOYMENT** 

4-141. When deploying the detachment, the MEDBDE commander issues orders, either verbally or in writing, to the FRSD chief. When attached to a Role 2 BSMC movement orders are issued by the BCT commander. Frequently, the time to respond to orders is short; therefore, the FRSD should be constantly prepared to move. For this reason, a flexible entry and exit strategy during movement into and withdrawal from the AO is essential. After receiving the commander’s order, the FRSD chief, field medical assistant, and detachment sergeant, conduct a mission analysis, incorporating changes based on METT-TC (I) coupled with operational requirements. 

**_Note._** When a FRSD is attached to a Role 2 BSMC, it can be subjected to frequent movement based upon the BCT’s operations. 

4-142. The FRSD is generally attached for a period of up to 72 hours, after which it redeploys to its unit of assignment for rest and resupply. In situations where the detachment is required to remain on station for longer than 72 hours, the detachment should be resupplied through established support channels. In some cases, it may be augmented by another FRSD to manage patient loads during a given situation. 

##### **ESTABLISHING THE SURGICAL FACILITY** 

4-143. The FRSD may deploy using several different shelter systems (tentage). The type of tentage used is based on the detachment’s modified TOE or common table of allowance. The configuration or layout of the FRSD is influenced by factors such as the type and location of the unit on which the FRSD falls in (Role 3 hospital or Role 2 medical company), terrain, anticipated patient load, and the frequency with which they may be required to displace as designed and equipped; the complete FRSD requires approximately 1,500 square feet of space to operate (when using 18- by 25-foot equivalent tents). For convenience and additional space, the chief of the FRSD may choose to set up using multiples of a given shelter system or, if possible, occupy a building of opportunity. Figure 4-7 on page 121, figure 4-8 on page 122, figure 4-9 on page 123, figure 4-10 on page 124, and figure 4-11 on page 125 show examples of how an FRSD may be configured.
