##### **SECTION IV – HOSPITAL AUGMENTATION TEAM (HEAD AND NECK)** 

4-144. This section provides a discussion of the hospital augmentation team (head and neck) allocation, assignments and capabilities, dependencies, employment, and organization and functions. 

#### **MISSION** 

4-145. The mission of this team is to provide ear, nose, and throat surgery; neurosurgery; eye surgery, and maxillofacial surgical augmentation in support of the Role 3 hospitals and consultative services as required. 

#### **ASSIGNMENT AND DEPENDENCIES** 

4-146. See the field hospital (32-bed) section for descriptions of what an assignment and dependency mean. The head and neck team has many dependencies due to its task organization and mission. 

4-147. The head and neck team, in coordination with MEDBDE, hospital center or field hospital (32-bed) leaders, should build on the list of dependencies from the Section I and add additional dependencies in the unit TACSOP as required and describe in detail the nature of each dependency and how these units will support the team. 

##### **ASSIGNMENT** 

4-148. The team is assigned to a MEDBDE and normally will be attached to a hospital. 

##### **DEPENDENCIES** 

4-149. This detachment is dependent upon— 

- Appropriate elements for legal, religious, finance, human resources and administrative services, shower and laundry support, clothing exchange, patient decontamination, mortuary affairs, and EPW security during processing and evacuation. 

- The hospital it is attached to provide sheltered ORs; commonly used equipment; pre- and postoperative nursing care for all patients; field feeding (to include patient food service); AHS support; water distribution; security; human resources and administrative services; transportation, unit maintenance for generator; power support for all equipment; patient administration; coordination of MEDEVAC; and all classes of supply. 

#### **CAPABILITIES AND FUNCTIONS** 

4-150. The unit capabilities and function depict what the head and neck team is capable of accomplishing. Functions are descriptions of what each subcomponent of the head and neck team is responsible for in support of the overall mission. Due to assigned missions, if there are additional capabilities or functions then the head and neck team should add them to their TACSOP along with prescriptive verbiage for each capability and function. 

##### **CAPABILITIES** 

4-151. This detachment provides— 

- Initial and secondary ear, nose, and throat surgery and consultation services in support of Role 3 hospitals. 

- Initial and secondary neurosurgery and consultation services in support of Role 3 hospitals. 

- Initial and secondary eye surgery and consultation services in support of Role 3 hospitals. 

- Initial and secondary maxillofacial surgical augmentation in support of Role 3 hospitals. 

- Augmentation of the Role 3 hospital OR surgical and nursing services. 

- Three days of supply for all organic elements upon deployment and during routine operations. 

- Individual weapons for personal defense and protection of the patients under the detachment’s care. 

##### **FUNCTIONS** 

4-152. The function of the hospital augmentation team (head and neck) is to provide preoperative assessment and perform neurosurgery; ear, nose, and throat surgery; ophthalmic surgery; and maxillofacial surgery for patients admitted to the hospital to which the unit is attached. The team will also provide the hospital with neurosurgical, ophthalmic, and otolaryngological consultation services and postoperative follow-up. 

4-153. The equipment for the hospital augmentation team (head and neck) no longer includes the MMS (radiology, CT). This set is provided by the field hospital (32-bed) so the hospital augmentation team (head and neck) can perform CT scans prior to surgery and will decrease the requirement for exploratory surgery. The hospital augmentation team (head and neck) does not include an OR and work areas; it will perform surgery utilizing the OR and central materiel supply complex of the hospital to which it is attached. When attached to the hospital, it falls under the supervision of the chief, professional services. 

4-154. The following paragraphs provide an overview of the primary functions of personnel assigned to the hospital augmentation team (head and neck). This includes both officers and enlisted personnel. 

###### **Ophthalmologist** 

4-155. The head and neck team includes two ophthalmologists (60S). The ophthalmologist examines, diagnoses, and treats or prescribes course of treatment for personnel suffering from diseases, injuries, or disorders of eyes and performs surgery when required. Specific duties and responsibilities of the ophthalmologist may include: 

- Examining, diagnosing, and treating or prescribing courses of treatment. 

- Performing surgery for patients with injuries or disorders that require surgical intervention. 

###### **Otolaryngologist** 

4-156. The head and neck team includes one otolaryngologist (60T). The otolaryngologist is dual hatted as the team leader responsible for the health and welfare of assigned personnel and the planning of head and neck teams operations. The otolaryngologist examines, diagnoses, and treats or prescribes courses of treatment for personnel suffering from diseases, injuries, or disorders of ear, nose, and throat, and performs surgery when required. Specific duties and responsibilities of otolaryngologist may include: 

- Examining, diagnosing, and treating or prescribing courses of treatment. 

- Performing surgery for patients having disorders, malformations, diseases, and injuries of the ears, nose and throat. 

###### **Neurosurgeon** 

4-157. The head and neck team includes two neurosurgeons (61Z). The neurosurgeon examines, diagnoses, and performs surgery as required in cases of patients suffering from disease, injury, or disorder of nervous system. Specific duties and responsibilities of the neurosurgeon may include: 

- Perform surgical procedures. 

- Perform diagnostic procedures. 

###### **Clinical Nurse, Operating Room** 

4-158. The head and neck team includes two perioperative nurses (66E). The perioperative nurses perform specialized professional nursing duties in any phase of the operative process for patients undergoing all types of surgical procedures. They provide safe supplies and equipment for perioperative services. Specific duties and responsibilities of the perioperative nurse may include: 

- Prepare patient and OR for surgery. 

- Assist with surgical procedures. 

- Assist with therapeutic procedures. 

- Assist with respiratory care. 

- Assist with cardiopulmonary resuscitation. 

- Assist with fluid therapy. 

- Supervising enlisted operating room personnel. 

###### **Nurse Anesthetist** 

4-159. The head and neck team include two nurse anesthetists (66F). The nurse anesthetists perform nursing duties in the care of patients requiring general or regional anesthesia for: 

- Surgical procedures. 

- Diagnostic procedures. 

- Therapeutic procedures. 

- Respiratory care. 

- Cardiopulmonary resuscitation. 

- Pain management. 

- Fluid therapy. 

###### **Operating Room Non-Commissioned Officer, Sergeant, and Specialist** 

4-160. The head and neck team includes two 68D3Os, one 68D2O, and two 68D1O operating room specialists. The operating room specialists assist the team’s nursing staff in preparing the patient and the OR environment for surgery and for assisting the surgical staff during procedures. They also operate the CMS and are responsible for preparing and maintaining sterile medical supplies and special equipment for the head and neck team. The operating room specialists also assist in the management of team’s OR tables. Specific duties and responsibilities of enlisted OR personnel include: 

- Assisting the nursing staff in preparing patients and the ORs for surgery. 

- Supervising maintenance programs in the OR and the CMS. 

- Monitoring the quality of sterilization techniques to ensure adherence to established standards. 

- Establishing stock levels for requisitioning supplies and equipment and supervise their storage and issue. 

- Preparing and maintaining various reports and files. 

- Supervising and advising subordinate personnel. 

- Creating and maintaining sterile fields. 

- Draping patients. 

- Preparing, manipulating, and delivering surgical instruments and equipment. 

- Accounting for all instruments, needles, sponges, and medications placed within the sterile field. 

- Cleaning and sterilizing surgical instruments. 

###### **Biomedical Equipment Sergeant** 

4-161. The head and neck team includes one biomedical equipment specialist (68A). The biomedical equipment specialist performs maintenance services and repairs on all medical devices, medical systems, medically related devices and medically related systems Specific duties and responsibilities of biomedical equipment sergeant may include: 

- Performing preventive maintenance checks and services (for example, test, operates, lubricates, adjusts, tightens, and cleans), calibration, verification, certification, and electrical safety tests on medical and medically related devices. 

- Troubleshooting to isolate malfunctioning or defective parts and/or boards on medical and medically related devices. 

- Affecting repairs by replacing malfunctioning or defective parts and/or boards on medical and medically related devices. 

- Maintaining networked medical devices. 

- Performing quality assurance and quality control and acceptance inspections on medical and medically related devices. 

- Computing power requirements for medical devices. 

- Contributing to the development of the medical maintenance SOP and commanders’ maintenance directive. 

###### **Eye Sergeant** 

4-162. The head and neck team includes one eye specialist (68Y). The eye specialist conducts routine diagnostic tests and assists in the care and treatment of ophthalmology or optometry patients. Specific duties and responsibilities of eye sergeant may include: 

- Assisting the nursing staff in preparing patients and the ORs for surgery. 

- Conducting routine diagnostic tests and assists in the care and treatment of ophthalmology or optometry patients under the supervision of an ophthalmologist, otolaryngologist, or nurse. 

###### **Radiology Specialist** 

4-163. The head and neck team includes two radiology specialists (68P). The radiology specialists operate the team’s CT equipment and supervises CT activities. Specific duties and responsibilities of radiology specialist may include: 

- Assisting the nursing staff in preparing patients and the ORs for surgery. 

- Providing patient care within the radiology area. 

- Preparing, assembling, and adjusting instruments, materials, and equipment. 

- Inspecting and performing operator maintenance on CT equipment. 

- Applying radiation, electrical, and mechanical protective measures. 

- Performing CT examinations of the head and neck area. 

- Developing CT images using digital and manual processing. 

- Reading and interpreting CT requests and physician’s orders. 

- Utilizing hospital information systems and performing routine patient administration. 

- Maintaining patient locator file, CT files, and report files. 

- Packing, unpacking, loading, and unloading CT equipment, assembling, and dissembling CT equipment and shelters. 

#### **EMPLOYMENT** 

4-164. The head and neck team is not designed as a standalone unit and will be employed with, and further attached or deployed within, the field hospital (32-bed) assigned AO. See Appendix B of this publication for additional planning factors that may impact head and neck team operations. Figure 4-12 depicts the task organization and composition of the hospital augmentation team (head and neck). 

**Figure 4-12. Task organization of the hospital augmentation team (head and neck)** 

4-165. In preparation for deployment, the head and neck team should take every opportunity to train. For detailed information on specialized augmentation training considerations, See Appendix F of this publication. 

##### **EMPLOYING THE TEAM** 

4-166. When deployed, the head and neck team provides additional surgical capabilities to the hospital center in the form ear, nose, and throat surgery; neurosurgery; and eye surgery augmentation. The head and neck team does not have organic OR tables but relies on the Role 3 hospital to which attached to dedicate an OR table or provide them on a need’s basis. This should be outlined in both the head and neck team’s a Role 3 hospital’s TASCOPs. 

##### **PLANNING** 

4-167. Requests for head and neck team support and the tasking authority that directs the support resides in the operations staff channel. Though not all inclusive, table 4-4 on page 130 depicts several head and neck team planning considerations. 

###### **Table 4-4. Head and neck team planning considerations** 

- Prior to arrival of the head and neck team, the supported Role 3 hospital should have planned its configuration, staking, water, and power requirements to include the head and neck team and ensure it can be setup and begin head and neck surgical operations with minimal interruption of Role 3 capabilities. 

- Team requires a minimum of four (4) to six (6) hours to set up and become functional. 

- Detachment has no organic transportation, and plans to move the head and neck team will require other medical units or sustainment transportation support to move: 

 - 18 personnel. 

 - One container with the head and neck team equipment that includes the following medical materiel sets: 

 - Eye exam clinic. 

 - Maxillofacial head and neck surgical augmentation. 

 - Neurosurgery augmentation: deployable medical system. 

 - Radiology computed tomography augmentation. 

 - - 

 - <u>One shelter: tactical expandable one side with computed tomography device.</u> 

**_Note._** The head and neck team does not require a running estimate as it will always be a part of the Role 3 hospital OR/CSM Section. 

###### **Basis of Allocation** 

4-168. The BOA is one head and neck team per 650 conventional hospital patients in the theater. 

###### **Mobility** 

4-169. This unit has no organic mobility. This unit has 55,046 pounds (5,031 cubic feet) of TOE assets requiring transportation by external resources. 

##### **ESTABLISHING THE HEAD AND NECK TEAM** 

4-170. Establishing the head and neck team requires prior planning to ensure the team knows the Role 3 hospital configuration to ensure the team will be able to add its CT device to the Role 3 hospital layout and that there is a designated area to place its container. It is recommended that the power requirements of the head and neck team are provided to the Role 3 hospital in advance so that it may be added to the Role 3 hospital power plan. 

4-171. The head and neck team does not have organic OR tables. It should work with the OR staff to ensure the team is added into the daily OR schedule as needed to perform its mission. 

4-172. The head and neck team should provide the Role 3 hospital with personnel information so the Role 3 hospital can ensure sleeping areas are provided for the head and neck team and that the number of assigned personnel is added to the overall field feeding schedule for the staff. 

##### **Appendix A** 

### **Hospitalization Support Within the Army Strategic Contexts** 

Appendix A discusses potential hospitalization activities in support of the Army strategic contexts of competition below armed conflict, crisis, and armed conflict. While these activities may vary in different regions of the world, the figures provided, and the accompanying descriptions, are intended to be generic and not specific to a particular region or operating environment. Commanders and planners can use this information as a starting point in determining the activities for their specific requirements. Throughout Appendix A, see table A-1 for all acronyms used in figures A-1 through A-3.

##### **SECTION I – HOSPITALIZATION DURING COMPETITION** 

A-1. During competition, the Role 3 hospital has the difficult training tasks associated with remaining ready to deploy. Potentially, the two most difficult areas to remain proficient at are the clinical skills of the health care providers and maintaining the ability to provide medical C2, especially considering elements that may deploy as a part of a hospital are not organic to the senior mission commander. The hospitals conduct field training exercises, staff exercises, Role 3 support to combat training centers and require medical providers to attend these events. A large percentage of the medical providers work at defense health agency MTFs to remain proficiency in their individual skills. The challenge for the operational Role 3 hospital is to integrate the medical providers into hospital operations utilizing the assigned equipment, procedures, and clinical practice guidelines of the Role 3, which may be different than the installation MTF they are accustomed to working. 

A-2. Other operations include supporting Soldier readiness activities, assessing prepositioned stocks, medical device maintenance, and planning/rehearsing elements of the hospital to provide limited Role 3 early entry support. Additionally, Role 3 hospitals can support ongoing engagements with host and partner nations to increase those nations’ capacities and capabilities to provide medical care and participate in global health engagements. While conducting health engagements, the hospital staff is gaining a great deal of situational awareness of the medical capabilities and health threats within the operational area; both will be important if a large-scale deployment occurs to the area. 

A-3. During competition, the Role 3 hospital is more than likely still at home station. Through the orders process, elements of the Role 3 may deploy during any strategic context. When deployed, the Role 3 hospital is assigned to the MEDBDE. The hospital center has many augmentation pieces to it. Based on the orders process and PAR factors, the HHD hospital center may have combinations of field hospitals (32-bed), hospital augmentation detachments (surgical 24-bed), hospital augmentation detachments (medical 32-bed), hospital augmentation detachments (intermediate care wards (60-bed), hospital augmentation teams (head and neck), medical detachments (minimal care 120-cot), or medial detachments, forward resuscitative and surgical. The BOA for the HHD hospital center is one per two field hospitals (32-bed) with the HHD deploying with the first field hospital (32-bed). Elements of the Role 3 provide hospitalization within the theater, corps, and division echelons. During transition operations, Role 3 hospital planners should assess and modify Role 3 contingency or operation plans in support of crisis and armed conflict operations. Figure A-1 on page 133 depicts a notional hospitalization operational framework during competition below armed conflict. 

**Figure A-1. Hospitalization during competition below armed conflict operations** 

##### **SECTION II – HOSPITALIZATION DURING CRISIS** 

A-4. During crisis, Role 3 hospitals continue training all aspects of their required capabilities. Hospital staffs are engaged in mission analysis and coordination with the MEDBDE to ensure all aspects of deployment and employment are adequate. Especially key for the hospital is coordinating Army prepositioned stock draws, if applicable, support during reception, staging, onward movement, and integration activities (to include early entry Role 3), and ensuring the right capabilities are available if a transition to armed conflict occurs. As the OE grows and matures, the Role 3 will incrementally have a larger PAR. With more patients comes more medical waste; the Role 3 should establish waste management procedures with engineer or HN support to ensure the proper disposal of medical waste throughout the competition continuum. 

A-5. Role 3 hospitals may still be supporting ongoing engagements with host and partner nations to increase those nations’ capacities and capabilities to provide hospital care. While conducting health engagements, the hospital staff continues to gain a great deal of situational awareness of the medical capabilities and health threats within the operational area, both will be important if a large-scale deployment occurs in the area. Based on the OE, elements of the Role 3 deploy into the AO. 

A-6. During crisis, elements of the Role 3 hospital may still be at home station while others are forward deployed. Through the orders process, elements of the Role 3 may deploy during any strategic context. Deployment of elements of a Role 3 weigh heavily on the BOA (which is very detailed for hospitalization and requires medical planners to be well versed in the computations). They are also based on planning estimates and PAR factors. When deployed, the Role 3 hospital is assigned to the MEDBDE and per its various BOAs provides Role 3 hospitalization within the theater, corps, and division echelons. During transition operations, Role 3 planners should assess and modify Role 3 contingency or OPLANs for returning to competition and forward to armed conflict. Figure A-2 on page 135 depicts a notional hospitalization operational framework during crisis. 

**Figure A-2. Hospitalization during crisis operations** 

##### **SECTION III – HOSPITALIZATION DURING ARMED CONFLICT** 

A-7. The Role 3 hospital provides hospitalization to corps and divisions per their BOA. The Role 3 should remain as flexible as possible. While the hospital center was designed to be modular, it is still a significant effort to relocate any part or all the hospital. Commanders and staffs should plan for and maintain situational awareness of possible requirements to relocate Role 3 capabilities. This may include task organizing limited amounts of Role 3 capabilities (that is, DCR, DCS, and ICU) and employing it in direct support of a maneuver unit engaged in heavy combat operations. The Role 3 should be prepared to conduct walking blood banks to collect and utilize WB as the resuscitative fluid of choice. In addition, support may include providing hospitalization to detainees. This will require a significant amount of coordination with the MEDBDE and other enabling capabilities within the AO (such as military police and sustainment units). 

A-8. While conducting offensive operations, a Role 3 hospital should be prepared to move forward to stay in proximity of supported forces. Due to the time it takes to de-complex a Role 3 and METT-TC (I) dependent, the Role 3 may only setup 208 of its 240 beds. This leaves one 32-bed field hospital packed up and on order is ready to move to a new location. Once setup and fully operational the 208-bed hospital can de-complex, pack up, and move to the new location and re-complex. At the new location, the 32-bed hospital that just arrived will remain packed up and ready to jump to a new location when directed. Medical commanders do assume risk with not setting up the full 240-bed Role 3. Commander’s need to weigh their options when supporting the operational commander’s intent while conducting operations in armed conflict. 

A-9. The ability to evacuate patients during large-scale combat operations will likely be limited and may only occur during short periods when the OE is permissive enough Therefore, Role 3 hospitals need to clear beds regularly and be prepared to provide care for patients for prolonged periods of time, which might be longer than they are comfortable with or longer than the theater evacuation policy normally allows for. When conducting prolonged care operations at a Role 3, the hospital commander should be prepared to clear beds at a moment’s notice during windows of opportunity. Class VIII stocks, bed management, medical regulating, and maximizing RTDs are just a few key considerations in a prolonged care situation. The Role 3 supports reconstitution by maximizing the RTD rate within the limits of the theater evacuation policy and patient status, as close to the supported unit as possible. 

A-10. During armed conflict and METT-TC (I) dependent, Role 3 hospitals are deployed and located within the operational area based on the orders process, planning estimates, and PAR factors. When deployed the Role 3 hospital is assigned to the MEDBDE and per its various BOAs provides Role 3 hospitalization within the theater, corps, and division echelons. In addition to the various Role 3 augmentation detachments designed around the hospital center there are several other capabilities that can be employed at the Role 3. Through the orders process the Role 3 may have the following units attached: 

A-11. While conducting consolidation of gains, it may include many kinds of ongoing mission support requirements. While some maneuver units may still be engaged in combat operations, others may have transitioned to stability tasks. The Role 3 hospital should maintain situational awareness to remain flexible and conform to the operational commander’s requirements. This will require the hospital to continue to assess running estimates and be prepared to provide all aspects of Role 3 care while reducing capacities in support of forward movement in support of armed conflict operations, redeployment operations and downsizing the footprint in theater (such as reducing the number of ICU and intermediate care ward [ICW] beds). Additionally, many of the UAP engagement activities that occurred in competition and crisis will require support in consolidation of gains. Key to the successful consolidation of gains will be for the HN to restore essential services, which includes a hospital system that is self-sufficient. 

A-12. During transition operations, coordination between the outgoing and incoming commands is vital to ensure a smooth hand off and continuity of operations. Coordination should be made between the outgoing and incoming commands to determine if medical assets (personnel, equipment, and supplies) are required to be left behind, including planning for disposal of equipment and supplies that cannot be redeployed. Role 3 planners should assess and modify Role 3 contingency or OPLANs in support of operations for returning to state of crisis or competition. Figure A-3 on page 137 depicts hospitalization in armed conflict. 

**Figure A-3. Hospitalization during armed conflict operations** This page intentionally left blank.

##### **Appendix B** 

### **Hospitalization Planning Factors** 

Appendix B provides hospitalization planning factors and data to compute surgical hours, hospital water, fuel, sustainment, blood, oxygen consumption, patient and personnel feeding, and waste disposal. The data and planning factors should be reviewed and updated to reflect any special considerations based on the OE and mission assigned to the hospital. 

##### **SECTION I – GENERAL HOSPITALIZATION PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS** 

B-1. This section discusses health threat assessments and hospital planning. It also discusses hospitalization’s role in eligibility of care, hospitalization requirements, and timelines. 

#### **MEDICAL INTELLIGENCE** 

B-2. Hospitalization utilizes medical intelligence. Medical intelligence results from collection, evaluation, analysis, and interpretation of foreign medical, bioscientific, and environmental information that is of interest to strategic planning and to military medical planning. _Medical intelligence_ is the category of intelligence resulting from collection, evaluation, analysis, and interpretation of foreign medical, bioscientific, and environmental information that is of interest to strategic planning and to military medical planning and operations for the conservation of the fighting strength of friendly forces and the formation of assessments of foreign medical capabilities in both military and civilian sectors. See also intelligence. (JP 2-0). For more information on medical intelligence, refer to FM 4-02 and ATP 4-02.55. 

B-3. Medical intelligence is provided to the hospital leaders by intelligence organizations. Hospital leaders should contact their MEDBDE S2 for any medical intelligence information and review all medical intelligence analysis to better support the deployed force with hospitalization support. Up-to-date medical intelligence assessments can be obtained by contacting Director, Defense Intelligence Agency, Attention: Director, National Center for Medical Intelligence, Fort Detrick, Maryland 21702-5000 or via the contact information listed on their website or by commercial (301) 619-7574 or Defense Switched Network 3437574. Key medical intelligence factors that hospital leaders should consider are listed below: 

- Review the analysis of infectious disease/chemical/biological/radiological health threats in the OE that can degrade mission effectiveness of deployed forces. 

- Review analysis of endemic, emerging, epidemic and pandemic diseases, public health standards and capabilities, and the quality and availability of medical services. 

- Review the foreign medical infrastructure and MTF capabilities and services assessment. 

- Review the threat integration analysis and how it will affect the health of the command and the employment of hospitalization units. 

- Review all orders for medical intelligence critical information requirements. 

- Review the analysis of local medical supply and equipment sources that includes an analysis of local quantity, quality, and availability of medical supplies and equipment. 

#### **HEALTH THREAT ASSESSMENT** 

B-4. A critical element in medical planning is performing a thorough appraisal of the health threat. This assessment includes the health threat to Service members, Department of the Army (DA) Civilians, multinational partners, contractors, and local populace within the AO. The health threat is a composite of ongoing potential enemy actions; adverse environmental, occupational, geographic, and meteorological conditions; endemic diseases; and employment of weapons of mass destruction (to include CBRN weapons that have the potential to affect the short- or long-term health condition [including psychological impact] of personnel). Table B-1 on page 140 represents minimal subject areas considered in the health threat assessment. 

**Table B-1. Health threats** 

|**_Threats_**|**_Description of threats_**|
|---|---|
|Diseases|• Endemic and Epidemic.<br>• Foodborne.<br>• Waterborne.<br>• Arthropodborne.<br>• Zoonotic.<br>• Vectors.|
|Occupational and<br>Environmental Health<br>Hazards|• Climatic (heat, cold, humidity, and significant elevations above sea level).<br>• Toxic industrial materials.<br>• Accidental or deliberate dispersion of radiological and biological material.<br>• Disruption of sanitation services or facilities (such as sewage and waste<br>disposal).<br>• Disruption of industrial operations or industrial noise.<br>• Accidental injuries (falls, crush injuries, sports injuries, vehicle accidents).|
|Poisonous or Venomous<br>Animals, Flora, and<br>Fauna|• Poisonous plants and bacteria.<br>• Venomous reptiles, amphibians, arthropods, and animals.|
|Medical Effects of<br>Weapons|• Conventional (to include blast and mild traumatic brain injury).<br>• Improvised (to include improvised explosive devices).<br>• Chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear warfare agents.<br>• Directed energy.<br>• Weapons of mass destruction.|
|Physiological and<br>Psychological Stressors|• Continuous operations.<br>• Combat and operational stress reactions.<br>• Wear of mission-oriented protective posture ensemble.<br>• Stability tasks.|

#### **HOSPITAL PLANNING** 

B-5. AHS planning encompasses the integration of the operational management of the FHP and HSS missions for training, predeployment, deployment, and postdeployment operations. AHS includes all mission support services performed, provided, or arranged by the operational medicine formations to support FHP and HSS mission requirements for the Army and as directed, for joint operations, intergovernmental agencies, and multinational forces. For additional operational medicine planning guidance and terminology, refer to ATP 4-02.55. 

B-6. The planning factors in the remainder of this appendix complement the considerations regarding the employment of theater hospitalization discussed in previous chapters. When planning and executing theater hospitalization, it is important to consider and use the existing planning factors available for the various commodities within a hospital. This is where the art of planning (based on experience) combines with the science of data analysis to produce the best possible execution of hospitalization. 

B-7. Military operations may require AHS support for a wide category of potential patients. The diverse and varied potential AHS demands should be carefully considered in initial planning. Categories that require careful consideration include indigenous allies, friendly and unfriendly civilians, paramilitary organizations, representatives of various U.S. agencies, U.S. civilian contractor personnel, and other individuals that may be entitled to care based on agreements with multinational forces and the HN. There are fine lines of distinction that should be clarified by the command. For example, wounded unfriendly civilians may be detainees subject to restrictions and regulations which do not apply to EPWs for their treatment. 

##### **ELIGIBILITY OF CARE DETERMINATION** 

B-8. Through the orders process, hospital commanders should ensure that the eligibility for care matrix is widely disseminated throughout the command and that all medical personnel are aware of the process and how to obtain additional guidance, if required. Eligibility of care determination may impact the Role 3 hospital and effect several hospital planning considerations. Hospital leaders should look for the eligibility of care determination in Annex F (Sustainment), Appendix 3 (HSS) and plan accordingly. ##### **DETAINEE MEDICAL PLANNING** 

B-9. The law of armed conflict (refer to FM 6-27/MCTP 11-10C), states that detainees should be afforded the same medical care as patients of the detaining power. Seriously WII detainees will be evacuated through medical channels but will be segregated from United States and multinational patients. Enemy prisoners of war will be evacuated from the combat zone as soon as possible. They will not be hospitalized in hospital wards with United States military patients. Except in emergencies, detainees will be hospitalized in housing equal to that used for U.S. military personnel. Accountability and security of detainee patients will be processed through appropriate channels following treatment. Qualified medical retained personnel will be used as much as possible in medical and hygiene work needed for the well-being of detainees. For additional information on AHS support to detainee operations refer to ATP 4-02.46 and TC 8-250.

|DNBI<br>disease and non-battle injury<br>MCO<br>ICWAD<br>hospital augmentation detachment,<br>(intermediate care ward 60-bed)<br>SAD<br>MAD<br>hospital augmentation detachment,<br>(medical 32-bed)<br>WIA|major combat o<br>hospital augme<br>(surgical 24-be<br>wounded in act|perations<br>ntation detachment<br>d)<br>ion|,| B-11. When the tool is opened, enable editing, enable content, and select one of the populations at risk categories in the drop-down listings. The medical planner will enter the correct PAR and estimated beds occupied by four different categories of patients. The spreadsheet will then calculate the beds and units required to support the estimation of occupied beds. The medical planner can enter information in column J and review the results of beds required versus beds allocated by various hospital unit compositions or configurations.

B-12. Allocations based on single components (without considering other components) will not provide the full complement of beds or operating tables and surgical hours for the hospital. The exception would be employment of a single HHD, hospital center and one field hospital (32-bed) component that would provide all necessary services until all 32-beds are filled. At that point, augmentation component(s) to provide additional capacity for holding patients or evacuation would be needed to maintain the operational capability. 

B-13. If CBRN patients are not anticipated, the planner can build the unit based only on WIA and DNBI casualty estimates. The Joint Medical Planning Toolkit can be used to estimate a casualty stream based on the variables for the opposing forces, weather, and terrain and other factors. The number of WIA and DNBI casualties expected daily multiplied by the factors in the table below for each type of casualty will determine the number of each component that will be needed. The planner will have to round down or up for each unit to arrive at the integer number of components necessary to support the operation. For detailed information on this process, refer to ATP 4-02.55. The tables and instructions for calculating BOA below provide an example based on arbitrary expected bed occupancy numbers. 

B-14. The U.S. Army Futures Command, Analysis Branch, developed an Excel spreadsheet modeling tool to assist medical planners in determining the appropriate hospital elements to provide Role 3 hospitalization in an AO. The tools provide the resources to plan for hospitalization as a system of components that can be employed to build a hospital varying from 32-beds to 240 beds. The appropriate mix of components can be calculated from casualty estimates and application of the BOA for 1,000 beds occupied by WIA, DNBI, nerve, and blister patients. This tool is located MEDCoE Doctrine Microsoft team, under the MEDCoE Doctrine Channel - World Wide channel, 02 – Operational Medicine Planning tools. – World Wide. The tool is currently named ATP 4-02.10_FH_Solver_Production_with_NEO. 

B-15. A dispersion allowance of 20 percent is normally used for hospitals in the theater. This is to accommodate the occasional shut-down and movement of hospitals to follow the maneuver forces and other reasons why a totally full hospital system is not feasible. The dispersion factor for that 20-percent allowance is 1.25. The allocation rules in table B-4 incorporate dispersion factors. Refer to ATP 4-02.55 for more information on dispersion factors and allowance.

B-16. Instructions for computing the number of HHD, hospital centers, field hospitals (32-bed) and augmentation detachments required to provide Role 3 hospitalization with expected WIA and DNBI occupied beds— 

- Obtain the daily casualty counts by WIA and DNBI (this example will not address chemical casualties). Table B-5 shows the expected daily bed occupancy for this example. 

- At the end of 5 days, the anticipated bed occupancy will be 120 WIA and 95 DNBI patients (after removing any return-to-duty patients, died of wounds received in action-in-hospital patients, and evacuated patients from the occupied beds census). 

- The planner should consult the BOA tables above to find the WIA and DNBI factors for each component of the hospital system.

B-17. The BOA is defined as the placement of the unit and quantity based off the Training and Doctrine Command’s rules of allocation analysis process and final determination. It is the foundation or basis of where the unit needs to be and the quantity required. The BOA gives all unit’s equal amounts as an initial planning factor. During large-scale combat operations, leaders should have the operational freedom to reallocate medical forces where they are needed. Unit leaders should not solely base what medical support a unit is authorized on the BOA. They should use the casualty estimate, PAR, patient densities, mission variables, and orders process during the military decision-making process to ensure the six AHS principles are applied in the employment of scarce medical support to the deployed force. This application of medical C2 ensures units that are designated as the center of gravity or main effort in the orders process, that will be taking more casualties based off the mission variables, receive more medical support, while supporting efforts may receive less. This applies to all operational medicine units. 

B-18. For example, a main effort BCT’s casualty estimate is 800 for the next 48 hours. 20 percent of these 800 casualties may require surgical intervention. That is up to 160 surgeries required over 48 hours. The BCT has one attached FRSD that can perform 30-48 surgical interventions over 72 hours which equates to 20-32 for 48 hours. Even though the BOA states one FRSD per committed BCT, the main effort BCT would require additional FRSD augmentation to meet the forward surgical demand. The division could attach the FRSDs from the other BCTs within the division or request addition FRSD or medical company (area support) support from the corps. These decisions are dependent upon the mission variables, PAR, patient densities, availability of additional forward surgical capabilities, and orders process. 

B-19. Medical planners and hospital leaders should review the theater evacuation policy. This analysis should cover the entire theater or supported AO to ensure there is adequate Role 3 hospitalization at echelon to support the deployed force and conform to the OPLAN/OPORD. In accordance with the theater evacuation policy, consideration should be given to placing admission timeframe limitation planning factors at echelon. Operational medicine has a limited amount of hospital beds, these planning factors will aid medical planners and hospital leaders in ensuring the clearing of hospital beds is a theater priority. The Role 3 hospital in support of the division must maintain mobility to keep proximity to the supported maneuver element. As an example, some consideration should be given to limiting admissions to three to seven days in the division. In the corps, hospitalization may move less. Consideration of up to 14 days admission should be discussed as a planning factor. In the theater, medical planners can consider up to 30 days admission if service members can be returned to duty. All recommended admission time frame planning factors must be in consonance with the theater evacuation policy, mission variable, and orders process.

B-20. Army hospitalization modularity comes in a series of two OR table each. For planning factors, each table equals a 24-hour cycle. For 18 hours in a 24-hour cycle that OR table can perform surgery. Six hours of each 24-hour cycle is dedicated as a rest period for the surgical team aligned with that OR table. Hence the term OR hours. Rest periods should be staggered to maximize the amount of OR tables in use. OR table planning factors are depicted on table B-6. Surgical intervention planning factors are 135 minutes per procedure for the FRSD and 3 hours per procedure for the OR/CMS section of the field hospital (32-bed) and SAD (24-bed). This three hour includes the cleanup and turnover of the OR.

**_Note:_** a single operating room table can perform 18 hours of surgery with 6 hours of rest in a 24-hour cycle. Surgical units need to stagger their operating rooms to maximize the number of surgical interventions that occur within a 24hour cycle. 

B-21. To compute the components required to support this bed occupancy workload, multiply the factors in the WIA and DNBI columns and the beds occupied on day 5 (example from table B-5), and divide by 1,000. Thus— 

- Field hospital (32-bed) = WIA bed occupancy X WIA factor /1000 + DNBI bed occupancy X DNBI factor /1000. 

- Field hospital (32-bed) = 120 X 12.40 / 1000 + 95 X 5.56 / 1000. 

- Field hospital (32-bed) = 1.49 + 0.52 = 2.01 or 2 field hospitals (32-bed) components (plus one HHD, hospital center). 

- Hospital augmentation detachment surgical (24-bed) = 120 X 12.9 / 1000 + 95 X 0.00 / 1000. 

- Hospital augmentation detachment surgical (24-bed) = 1.55 + 0 = 1.55 or 2 hospital augmentation detachments, surgical (24-bed). 

- Hospital augmentation detachment (medical 32-bed) = 120 X 11.9 / 1000 + 95 X 7.21 / 1000. 

- Hospital augmentation detachment (medical 32-bed) = 1.43 + 0.68 = 2.11 or 2 hospital augmentation detachment (medical 32-bed). 

- Hospital augmentation detachment (ICW 60-bed) = 120 X .01 / 1000 + 95 X 9.45 / 1000. 

- Hospital augmentation detachment (ICW 60-bed) = 0.01 + .90 = .91 or 1 hospital augmentation detachment (ICW 60-bed). 

**_Note._** Minimal care cots are not calculated in the computation of the 240-maximum number of beds in the hospital center. The cots are considered as an augmentation of the hospital and established to provide care for ambulatory patients requiring minimal medical care or assistance and meet the criteria to be returned to duty within the prescribed theater evacuation policy. 

- Medical detachment (minimal care) = 120 X 1.35 / 1000 + 95 X 2.28 /1000. 

- Medical detachment (minimal care) = 0.16 + 0.27 = 0.43 or 1 medical detachment (minimal care). 

B-22. These calculations result in several fractions-2.01 field hospitals (32-bed) (and one HHD, hospital center); 1.55 hospital augmentation detachments (surgical 24-beds); 2.11 hospital augmentation detachments (medical 32-bed); 0.91 hospital augmentation detachment (ICW 60-bed); and 0.43 medical detachments, minimal care. The planner should then look at the capabilities of the components and round the fractions to estimate the correct number of beds, OR capacity, and type of hospital capabilities required. One possibility is-HHD, hospital center; two field hospitals (32-bed); two hospital augmentation detachments (surgical 24-beds); two hospital augmentation detachments (medical 32-bed); and one hospital augmentation detachment (ICW 60-bed). The medical detachment (minimal care) can be ignored because in this example there is enough available capacity for the expected minimal care patients in the ICW wards. The results of the computations are illustrated in table B-7.

B-23. There are enough beds to provide for the dispersion factor. However, the beds required appears to exceed what one HHD, hospital center, can support by 116 beds. Due to C2 limitations and span of control, the maximum bed configuration for a single HHD, hospital center, is restricted to 240 beds. However, the medical detachment, minimal care beds do not count against the maximum 240 bed limitation of the hospital center. It may, however, be considered a good option to deploy a second HHD, hospital center in this example. 

#### **HOSPITAL CENTER SUSTAINMENT AND SPACE REQUIREMENTS** 

B-24. The hospital center and subordinate detachments require a significant amount of logistical and transportation support getting to and once in theater. When planning for placement of Role 3 hospitals, medical planners and hospital leaders should consider fuel, power, water and space requirements. These requirements should include external support available and the amount of time it will take to decomplex, containerize, transport, and complex a Role 3 hospital. Medical planners and hospital leaders cannot expect a hospital to relocate from one location to another and be able to provide the full complement of Role 3 medical care at the new location in just one or two days. Executing the move of a hospital center may take a significant amount of time (depending upon the distance required to move and training level of the unit) considering the required planning, coordination for resources, evacuation of all patients, systematically discontinuation of services, decomplexing and packing the hospital, conducting the move, and then complexing the hospital is a significant undertaking.

B-25. Table B-8 depicted an overall estimate of fuel, power, water, and space requirements for the hospital center. This also includes the organic tents, shelter, containers, vehicles, life support and housing space, and the space required to properly disperse them during large-scale combat operations. Table B-8 also lists the transportation requirement planning factors for movement of the Role 3 hospital.

As stated earlier, the advantage with the hospital center is the modularity and flexibility these various hospitalization elements may be task organized and configured to support the range of military operations. 

**Figure B-1. Common hospital center task organizations**

The hospital center has the capability to perform split-based operations within an AO. ##### **HOSPITAL CENTER LAYOUT EXAMPLES** 

B-29. There are many ways to layout and establish the Role 3 hospital center, and it depends on factors such as the mission, task organization, single- or split-based operations, terrain, space available, personnel, and time. The layout should be established with effective and efficient movement of patients through the facility in mind and ensuring water and power is effectively established throughout the hospital. Additionally, functions of the hospital that support other capabilities should be as close as possible to those supported sections. For example, CMS should be near the OR. Figures B-2 through figure B-9 on page 153 through page 162 provide examples of how hospital centers can be set up depending on the task organization of the hospital. While it creates extra distance from the Triage/EMT to the operating room area, the four-tent baseline (triage/EMT/pre-operative tent 1, triage/EMT/pre-operative tent 2 [used as interchange], OR/CMS tent 1 [used as an interchange], and then OR/CMS tent 2 [operating room]) is the most effective way to ensure additional capabilities can be added to the field hospital (32-bed) as the theater matures without having to disestablish (de-complex) and setup (complex) any of the established Role 3 capabilities. Hospital leaders can use TC 8-13 to assist in developing tactics, techniques, and procedures for complexing and decomplexing a Role 3 hospital.

**_Note._** The configurations depicted in figures B-2 through B-9 are just the Role 3 hospital center footprint and do not take into consideration the space required for the containers, vehicles, and logistics support area (sleeping areas), which are required to be tactically dispersed. Proper dispersion of 50 meters between vehicles is recommended. These figures depict the tentage authorized on the unit TOE by section. Of note, the hospital center’s command, staff, and sleeping tentage are not TOE equipment and are considered common table of allowance items purchased with unit funds. Refer to table B-8 on page 148 of this publication for estimated hospital center operational space requirements. 

B-30. Figure B-2 on page 153 depicts a notional base Role 3 hospital center that consists of the HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed). Just the hospital center footprint is more than 240 feet wide by 200 feet in length and is more than an acre in size (or around .75 of a football field). When complexing a Role 3 hospital, planners tend to use the term interchange or plan for sections to have tentage not authorized on the TOE. Doctrinally there is no dedicated interchange tentage and if using authorized hospital tentage, planners need to understand that the tent is taken out of hide from a section within the Role 3. Some areas that indicate they have tents do not. Some examples include patient administration, nutrition care, and medical maintenance. This should be a topic discussed during the military decision-making process as commanders and their planners develop the capabilities required for layout and staking of the hospital; the above-mentioned sections have no tentage and, if provided, will be taken from a section that is authorized tentage. Ultimately, commanders have the final say on how their hospital is complexed and assume risk when needed. 

**Figure B-2. Notional headquarters and headquarters detachment, hospital center and fieldhospital (32-bed) configuration** B-31. Figure B-3 depicts a notional 56-bed configuration (HHD, hospital center, field hospital [32-bed] and a SAD [24-bed]). These configurations are like what might be in support of stability or DSCA operations METT-TC (I) and PAR dependent.

**Figure B-3. Notional 56-bed hospital center configuration** B-32. Just the hospital center footprint is over 200 feet wide by 320 feet in length and is over 2.5 acres in size (nearly 2 football fields). This is just the hospital infrastructure and does not consider the disbursement of containers, vehicles, or logistics support area space requirements. Figure B-4 depicts a 64-bed configuration (HHD, hospital center, field hospital [32-bed], and a MAD [32-bed]).

**Figure B-4. Notional 64-bed hospital center configuration** B-33. Figure B-5 on page 157 depicts a notional 88-bed hospital center configuration (HHD hospital center, one [1] field hospital [32-bed], a SAD [24-bed], and a MAD [32-bed]). These configurations are likely to be deployed in a division AO during large-scale combat operations. In the offense, it may be determined to assume risk and only complex an 88-bed Role 3 hospital center. This course of action keeps one field hospital packed and ready to move forward in support of the offense. Once the field hospital (32-bed) is complexed at the new location, the Role 3 hospital at the original location initiates de-complexing the hospital and begins movement to the new location. At the new location, the field hospital (32-bed) that just moved will remain packed up and ready to move forward to continue providing hospitalization during offensive operations for the division. This maximizes the use of organic transportation assets and uses minimal sustainment transportation assets.

**Figure B-5. Notional 88-bed hospital center configuration** 

B-34. Just the hospital center footprint is nearly 300 feet wide by 320 feet in length and is 96,000 square feet. This is more than two acres in size (slightly larger than two football fields). In support of defensive operations, it may be acceptable to complex the 120-bed Role 3 hospital center in two locations, METT-TC (I) dependent. Notice in figure B-6 on page 158 that the second field hospital (32-bed) is complexed in such a manner that if it should de-complexed to move forward it will not disrupt the rest of the Role 3 hospital center. This is key in planning and staking the hospital center for support to future operations. Figure B-6 depicts a notional 120-bed hospital center configuration (HHD hospital center, two [2] field hospital [32bed], a SAD [24-bed], and a MAD [32-bed]).

**Figure B-6. Notional 120-bed hospital center configuration** B-35. Figure B-7 on page 160 depicts a notional 148 bed hospital center configuration (HHD hospital center, a field hospital [32-bed], a SAD [24-bed], a MAD [32-bed], and an ICWAD [60-bed]). Figure B-8 on page 161 depicts a notional 208 bed hospital center configuration (HHD hospital center, a field hospital [32-bed], a SAD [24-bed], a MAD [32-bed], and two [2] ICWAD [60-bed]). Figure B-9 on page 162 depicts a notional 240-bed hospital center configuration (HHD hospital center, two [2] field hospital [32-bed], a SAD [24bed], a MAD [32-bed], and two [2] ICWAD [60-bed]). These configurations are likely to be in a corps or theater AO, METT-TC (I) and PAR dependent, and provide hospitalization support to the full range of military operations.

**Figure B-7. Notional 148-bed hospital center configuration_Note._** If a division is operating independently, METT-TC (I) and PAR dependent, it may receive hospitalization support like those depicted in figures B-7 through B-9. 

B-36. In the 208 and 240-bed configurations, the hospital center footprint is more than 420 feet wide by 320 feet in length and is nearly 135,000 square feet. This is approximately three acres in size (nearly 2.5 football fields). As previously stated, this is just the hospital infrastructure and does not consider the space required for proper dispersal of containers, vehicles, or logistics support area space requirements.

#### **TIMELINE TO ESTABLISH THE HOSPITAL** 

B-37. Since the hospital center is relatively new, there have not been many operational employments or training exercises conducted that would inform how long it will take to complex, erect, and become operational. From experience prior to 2001 with establishing Role 3 hospitalization in support of large-scale combat operations, an HHD, hospital center and field hospital (32-bed) should be able to provide limited C2, emergency medical services, surgical services, intensive care, and ancillary services within 24-36 hours of arrival to site. A second field hospital and augmentation detachments will take more time. Prioritizing how these services are established is up to commanders, but there should be an incremental increase in medical support capabilities throughout the period it takes to fully reach all services provided by the hospital. 

B-38. Part of the timeline to establish a Role 3 hospital includes what elements of the Role 3 are required. An HHD hospital with a field hospital (32-bed) requires less time to complex than a complete 240-bed hospital center. Medical planners and hospital leadership should practice complexing and decomplexing various Role 3 hospital configurations at home station. Complexing and decomplexing timelines should be noted in the Role 3 hospital center TACSOP. Adding these timelines to the unit TACSOP will allow medical planners and hospital leadership to better forecast how long it takes to complex and decomplex the various Role 3 configurations in an operational environment. These times can be used to forecast the optimal time to move a Role 3 hospital within an established OPLAN/OPORD timeline and when to request sustainment transportation support. 

##### **ROLE 3 HOSPITAL H-HOUR SEQUENCE** 

B-39. The Role 3 hospital should include a version of an h-hour sequence in the TACSOP that outlines the timeline that essential hospital services may be available to the supported force. This can be included in the orders process and the status reported up to the supporting MEDBDE or supported unit headquarters if no MEDBDE is deployed to provide C2. 

B-40. Table B-11 depicts a notional timeline for establishing a 148 bed Role 3 hospital. Time sequences should be adjusted to fit the Role 3 configuration required to support the PAR. The second field hospital and two ICW augmentation detachments are not annotated in the table and would have to be added in the unit TACSOP. Timeline rehearsals are crucial during home station training to ensure the Role 3 hospital can be staked and complexed in the shortest amount of time to provide hospitalization to the supported force in an OE. Role 3 hospitals should practice this in daylight, during limited visibility, and various climates.

#### **SHOWERS** 

B-44. The Office of The Surgeon General recommends, from a health maintenance perspective, a minimum of one shower and one change of uniform per Soldier per week. While this meets the minimum health standard requirements, from a morale standpoint, the Army goal is one standard shower and one expedient shower per week with two changes of uniform. The central hygiene and laundry planning factors are based on these two showers and 15 pounds of laundry per Soldier per week. 

B-45. Centralized hygiene, shower, and laundry water is required by theater quartermaster elements to provide individual Soldier laundry and shower field services. Water for centralized hygiene, such as field showers, can be disinfected nonpotable fresh water when approved by PVNTMED personnel. Water for laundry services can be nonpotable fresh water. Water used for personal hygiene will be potable water only. The health threat may impact the water quality standards and limit the use of nonpotable water for field services. 

#### **WASTEWATER AND SOLID WASTE PLANNING FACTORS** 

B-46. The hospital should plan for all patient and staff water and all laundry water requirements to become wastewater. Solid waste calculation (estimated): Total patients (beds) x 15 pounds = total patient solid waste per day; staff x 12.5 pounds = total staff solid waste per day. Hospital infectious waste planning factors (estimated): pounds (1 cubic foot) of infectious waste generated per bed per day. 

##### **SECTION III – MEDICAL MATERIAL CONSUMPTION AND REPLENISHMENT** 

B-47. This section provides planning guidance for the consumption and replenishment and reference publications to assist in planning operations. 

#### **CLASSES OF SUPPLY PLANNING FACTORS** 

B-48. Information on available operational rations and menu planning in an AO is available in ATP 4-41. Menu planning should be coordinated with the theater Class I (subsistence) manager and the designated theater dietitian to ensure the availability of the ration mix needed to support medical requirements. At a minimum, a 21-day basic load of medical nutritional supplements should be deployed until the logistical system is fully capable of Class I support. 

B-49. All Soldiers should deploy with at least a 30-day supply of personal-demand items. If exchange support is not readily available or cannot be established, health and comfort items are packaged and issued as a health and comfort pack. AR 710-2 provides guidance on planning and requisition of these items. Adjustments in quantity or selection of items in the health and comfort pack should be submitted to the theater Class I manager. The issue of health and comfort packs will cease when exchange facilities are available. Table B-14 on page 167 provides planning factors for several classes of supply (minus Class VIII) common in a hospital center. For additional information on sustainment, refer to ADP 4-0 and FM 4-0. 

**Table B-14. Classes of supply (Classes I through IV and VI) factor rates** 

|**_Class of supply_**|**_Planning factor_**|
|---|---|
|Class I—Subsistence|4.03poundsperpersonper day|
|Class II—General Support Items|3.67poundsperpersonper day|
|Class III—Petroleum, Oils, Lubricants|53.70 pounds per person per day (bulk)<br>0.59poundsperpersonper day (packaged)|
|Class IV—Construction Materials|8.500 pounds per person per day (includes 4.0<br>barrier materiel and 4.5 base construction)|
|Class VI—Personal Demand Items|3.20poundsperpersonper day|

B-51. Pounds per Soldier per day and pounds per wounded are planning factors used by medical logisticians when planning for Class VIII medical materiel support and transportation requirements. The patient estimate (derived from the casualty estimate) is the basis for applying these computations. The Class VIII medical materiel planning factors were developed using generic patient streams that are intended to include various types of patients. 

#### **CLASS VIIIB BLOOD PLANNING FACTORS** 

B-52. This section provides planning factors and references for planning blood requirements. Planners should update the planning factors for their AO. 

##### **MANAGEMENT AND PLANNING OF BLOOD REQUIREMENTS** 

B-53. The management and distribution of blood in the AO is a combined effort of Joint Blood Program Officer, medical detachment blood support, and medical providers. In the long-term and in a mature AO, blood management is based on resupply from the CONUS blood donor base, using a combination of liquid and frozen blood products. Each hospital stored a combination of liquid and frozen blood products of various groups and types. 

##### **LIQUID BLOOD PRODUCTS** 

B-54. Liquid blood products enter the AO through the U.S. Air Force Blood Transshipment Centers for further distribution to the Army medical detachment (blood support) located with the MMB. 

B-55. The medical detachment (blood support) provides collection, manufacturing, storage, and distribution of blood and blood products to AO MTFs. The detachment is resupplied from a supporting U.S. Air Force Blood Transshipment Center. The detachment commander may also serve as the area joint blood program officer if a DOD Area Joint Blood Program Officer is not available. 

B-56. Blood collection in the AO is governed by AO policy but normally is done to provide WB or apheresis platelets for emergency situations. Limited testing of blood drawn in the AO is done to minimize danger to recipients. 

B-57. Blood shipped into the AO will be liquid RBC and fresh frozen plasma and, possibly, frozen platelets. Subject to availability, RBC shipped from CONUS are packed with the unit group for type distribution. For additional information on blood planning and products refer to TM 8-227-12/NAVMED P-6530/AFH 44152_IP. Blood planning factors are depicted in table B-16. 

**Table B-16. Blood planning factors** 

|**_Blood Product_**<br>**_Therapy_**|**_Planning Factors_**|**_Role 3 Storage Capacities_**|
|---|---|---|
|Whole blood (WB) or<br>whole blood equivalent<br>(WBE)|8 units per wounded, ill, and<br>injured (WII) requiring blood<br>(see note 2)|420 units of whole blood of both Rh positive<br>and negative blood types.|
|Red Blood Cells|8 units per WII requiring blood|480 units of Groups A, B, and O liquid red<br>blood cells of both rhesus (Rh) positive and<br>negative blood types.|
|Fresh Frozen Plasma|8 units per WII requiring blood|50-100 units of Groups A, B, O, and AB fresh<br>frozen plasma depending on how individual<br>plasma units arepackaged.|
|Platelets|1.6 units per WII requiring<br>blood|Five units of Groups A, B, O, and AB platelets<br>of both Rhpositive and negative blood types.|
|Cryoprecipitate|1.6 units per WII requiring<br>blood|50-100 units of Groups A, B, O, and AB<br>cryoprecipitate depending on how individual<br>cryoprecipitate units arepackaged.|

###### **_Notes:_** 

1. The blood planning factors are for blood product utilization across all roles of care in a deployed environment. 

2. For planning purposes, each WIA requiring blood will require 8 units of WB or 8 WBE, not both. 

3. The use of low titer group O WB should be prioritized to the greatest extent possible. 

4. When WB supply is limited, prioritize WB distribution to roles of care closest to the point of injury and substitute WB with component therapy to approximate a WBE. 

One whole blood equivalent is composed of: 1 unit of Red Blood Cells, 1 unit of Fresh Frozen Plasma, 0.2 units of Platelets, and 0.2 units of Cryoprecipitate. 

|**Legend**||||
|---|---|---|---|
|<br>Rh|rhesus|WBE|whole blood equivalent|
|WB|whole blood|WII|<br>wounded,ill,and injured|

B-58. The expected admission rates per day are critical in computing initial blood requirements. These rates, along with the above blood planning factors, provide the AHS planner with an initial estimate of daily blood requirements. Table B-17 on page 169 provides a sample calculation for initial blood requirements. 

**Table B-17. Sample calculation for initial blood requirements** 

Wounded in action (WIA) = provided by G-1 (S-1) (personnel officer) casualty estimate **_Blood planning estimate = 20% of WIA will require blood transfusion_** 

<!-- Start of picture text -->
whole blood equivalent (WBE).<br>(WIA x blood planning estimate x blood planning factor) = WB or WBE required<br>(100 WIA)<br>or<br>(1,000 WIA)<br>or<br>(10,000 WIA)<br><!-- End of picture text -->

**_Medical planners Tool Kit may also be used to generate a blood estimate using planning factor of 8 whole blood (WB)/ whole blood equivalent (WBE).__Blood planning factor = 8 units of whole blood or whole blood equivalents per WIA requiring blood Formula_** 

(WIA x blood planning estimate x blood planning factor) = WB **or** WBE required 

**_Example 1_** (100 WIA) 100 x 20% x 8 = 160 units of WB 

100 x 20% x 8 = 160 units of Red Blood Cells, 160 units of Fresh Frozen Plasma, and 32 units of Platelets, and 32 units of Cryoprecipitate 

**_Example 2_** (1,000 WIA) 1,000 x 20% x 8 = 1,600 units of WB **or** 1,000 x 20% x 8 = 1,600 units of Red Blood Cells, 1,600 units of Fresh Frozen Plasma, and 320 units of Platelets, and 320 units of Cryoprecipitate 

**_Example 3_** (10,000 WIA) 10,000 x 20% x 8 = 16,000 units of WB 

###### **or** 

|10,000 x 20<br>of Platelets,|% x 8 = 16,000 units of Red Blood Cell<br>and 3,200 units of Cryoprecipitate|s, 16,000 units of Fresh Frozen Plasma, and 3,200 units|
|---|---|---|
|**Legend:**<br>|<br>||
|G-1 (S-1)|personnel officer<br>WBE|whole blood equivalent|
|WB|whole blood<br>WIA|wounded in action|

#### **OXYGEN PLANNING FACTORS** 

B-59. Table B-18 below and table B-19 on page 170 provide estimated planning factors and conversion factors for oxygen use in the facility. It is important to understand that the below oxygen factors were based on medical treatment provided in combat support hospitals and hospital centers over the last several years. However, there is research that suggests that providing severely WII Soldiers too much oxygen is not necessary and, in some cases, even harmful. Therefore, these planning factors should be used to determine how much oxygen to have on hand, a MEDLOG estimate, not a required or recommended treatment protocol for oxygen therapy. 

**Table B-18. Oxygen planning factors** 

|**_Location_**|**_Usage _**|
|---|---|
|Operatingroom table|2.8 litersper minute duringoperational time|
|Intensive care unit bed (ventilator)|4.5 liters per minute for 17 percent of the total<br>intensive care unit beds (patients on resuscitator or<br>ventilator)|
|Intensive care unit bed (nasal cannula)|3.1 liters per minute for 17 percent of the total<br>intensive care unit beds (patients on nasal cannula<br>or mask)|
|Miscellaneous requirements|An additional factor of 10 percent is applied to the<br>total of operating room and intensive care unit<br>requirements to account for oxygen requirements in<br>other areas of the hospital| **Table B-19. Oxygen conversion factors**

|**_Oxygenquantity_**|**_Conversion factor_**|
|---|---|
|1gallon(gaseous oxygen)|0.1333 cubic feet|
|95gallons “D” cylinder|12.7 cubic feet|
|1,650gallons “H” cylinder|220 cubic feet|
|1 cubic foot(gaseous oxygen)|28.317 liters|
|95gallons “D” cylinder|359.63 liters|
|1,650gallons “H” cylinder|6,229.74 liters|

B-60. The capability to fill medical oxygen tanks at or above the rate of consumption in a Role 3 hospital should be an important planning factor. Medical planners and hospital leaders should include large-scale combat operations centric courses of action that address the restriction of organic medical oxygen production due to maintenance of equipment, demand of medical oxygen exceeding the capabilities of organic medical oxygen producing equipment, or organic equipment has been destroyed. Medical planners and hospital leaders should ensure oxygen as a planning factor is included in their TACSOP. The TACSOP should include factors such as— 

- Being required, through the orders process, to provide medical oxygen to medical units on an area basis 

- Medical oxygen cylinder distribution and reconstitution. 

- Resupply of medical oxygen and cylinders through military sustainment, UAPs, or HN channels. 

- Are UAP and HN oxygen cylinders safe and compatible with organic medical oxygen producing equipment. 

##### **<mark>SECTION IV – HOSPITALIZATION STATISTICS, REPORTS, AND SITUATIONAL AWARENESS</mark>** 

B-61. Hospitalization statistics and reports are beneficial for future hospitalization course of action development. Role 3 hospitals should consistently request updates from their assigned/attached MEDBDE to ensure they are capturing the proper statistical data and reporting relevant and accurate information. Role 3 hospital leaders should establish robust statistical and reporting areas in their TACSOP. 

#### **HOSPITALIZATION STATISTICS** 

B-62. Army hospitalization statistical data complements and reinforces science and technology efforts conducted at home station that may result in improved clinical practice guidelines and improved Role 3 hospitalization. The list below is not all inclusive, but the Role 3 should consistently request updates from their assigned/attached MEDBDE to ensure they are capturing the proper statistical data. 

- Combat wounded, non-combat injuries, and DNBI admissions. 

- Length of stay. 

- Inpatient mortality rates. 

- Bed utilization rates (to include percentage of available beds and number of contaminated beds). 

- Incidents (surgical, medical, medication, and nonmedication related). 

- Patient administration (health record update status). 

- Number and type of ancillary services provided. 

- Class I (to include water), II, III, and V requirements and consumption rates. 

- Class IV requirements. 

- Class VIIIA and Class VIIIB requirements and consumption rates. 

- Medical regulating (patient evacuation rates). 

#### **HOSPITALIZATION REPORTS** 

B-63. Accurate hospitalization reports from each Role 3 hospital impact a balanced hospitalization bed status throughout the theater. They also provide situational awareness regarding classes of supply, to include Class VIIIA and Class VIIIB status and sustainment. Not all inclusive, listed below are several key reports every Role 3 hospital should use. Hospital leaders should request reporting requirements from their assigned/attached MEDBDE to ensure they are reporting accurately and consistently. For additional information on Army messages and reports, refer to FM 6-99.

B-73. Regardless of the format, the locally required format should be included in the tactical SOP. The information from this log is extracted, when required, and provided to the S-1 or supported unit requesting the information. The daily disposition log is also the primary source document for information needed in the preparation of DA Form 1156 ( _Casualty Feeder Card)_ , patient summary report, and the patient evacuation and mortality report. The daily disposition log can be modified to include other information if required by higher headquarters. 

B-74. The daily disposition log does not lend itself to transmission. However, the information may be extracted and provided via courier or electronic means to agencies responsible for preparing consolidated reports or casualty feeder reports. 

##### **PATIENT SUMMARY REPORT** 

B-75. The patient summary report is a non-standardized report that may be created in a locally required format. The patient summary report is maintained by all MTFs. Table B-29 on page 180 is provided as an example format, regardless of the format the locally required format should be included in the tactical SOP. The patient summary report is a weekly report compiled as of 0001 hours, Sunday. It is prepared by MTFs and is submitted to respective surgeons as required. The command surgeon can, however, dictate the frequency of submission to meet command requirements. Furthermore, the format can be adjusted if additional information is required by higher headquarters.

#### **ROLE 3 HOSPITALIZATION SITUATIONAL AWARENESS AND DECISIONMAKING** 

B-76. Time is crucial during large-scale combat operations. Army hospitalization is modular in nature and can be configured multiple ways to support the range of military operations. As a part of their unit TACSOPs, medical planners and hospital leaders should develop quick techniques and procedures to pass information and depict the current status of Role 3 hospitalization that are not part of full reports. These situational awareness and planning tools can be as simple as using numbers to depict the Role 3 configurations and OR/bed/cot status This quick reference would be annotated on a common operational picture or on a Role 3 military graphic as numbers. 

B-77. To depict the Role 3 configuration, The Role 3 hospital could use HHD/FH/SAD/MAD/ICWAD and in parentheses at the end, any further augmentation such as (head and neck teams, FRSDs, or minimal care wards (MCWs). For example, 1/2/1/1/2 (1/1/0) depicts a 240-bed hospital center composed of an HHD, two field hospitals, one SAD, one MAD, two ICWADs. It is further augmented by one head and neck team, one FRSD, and zero minimal care wards. 

B-78. We use a second set of numbers to depict OR/bed/cot status as EMT/OR/ICU/ICW (MCW) This second set of number 6/6/60/180 (0) depicts a Role 3 hospital with all six triage/EMT cots and OR tables occupied, all 60 ICU beds and 180 ICW beds occupied, and has zero minimal care cots. This hospital is at full capacity with all 240 beds in use. 

B-79. Another example of both numbers shows a hospital as 1/1/1/1/0 (2) (2/2/24/40 [60]). This depicts a hospital center with an HHD, one field hospital, one SAD, one MAD, zero ICWADs, and two minimal care wards. Its capacity depicts 2 available OR tables, 24 available ICU beds and 40 ICW beds, and 20 minimal care cots empty. This concise and detailed information can quickly assist medical planners and hospital leaders in making decisions on bed or cot availability for incoming patients and what hospitals need to clear beds and cots. See table B-30 for an example of a corps Role 3 situational awareness matrix.

**Table B-30.
