**214 ATP 4-02.10** 

#### **REHEARSALS** 

F-10. After completing training, Role 3 hospital and specialized surgical and medical augmentation unit personnel should refine their newly acquired skills using frequent and realistic rehearsals. Rehearsals reinforce newly acquired skills and help personnel become more confident and efficient in planning, establishing, disestablishing, and rapidly relocating the unit. This is especially helpful when frequent displacement, movement, and setup is likely. 

#### **TACTICAL STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES** 

F-11. The TACSOP is important in training and should be grounded in doctrine but detailed and prescriptive in nature. TACSOPs should be clear and concise and reflect procedural guidance that supports the mission and doctrinal requirements. A well-written TACSOP outlines how battle rhythm events, daily operations, and the duties and responsibilities of individual positions within an organization are executed. The TACSOP provides step-by-step instructions compiled by a unit to help personnel carry out complex routine operations. The aim of the TACSOP is to achieve efficiency, quality output, and uniform performance while reducing miscommunication and failure to comply with regulations and standards. Each TACSOP should clearly articulate those critical tasks that personnel should accomplish for each collective task and by position for the organization to be successful. This is especially important if a primary team member is absent, and another member or members of the unit should fill the position. For the Role 3 hospital, development and maintenance of clinical practice guidelines is the responsibility of the deputy commander of clinical services, chief of surgical services, and the deputy commander of nursing. For the FRSD, development and maintenance of clinical practice guidelines is the responsibility of the general surgeon appointed as chief. The Role 3 hospital and specialized surgical and medical augmentation units should base their TACSOPs on the TMC and MEDBDE TACSOPs and refine them based on the OE.

##### **Appendix G** 

### **Hospitalization Information Systems** 

The purpose of this appendix is to provide an overview of medical information and communication systems (and personnel responsible for maintaining these systems) and how they are employed to optimize care and enable C2 for the Role 3 hospital center (with subordinate field hospital and medical augmentation detachments). The hospital center is modular in design. This design allows a bed capacity range from a 32 (field hospital [32-bed]) to full bed capacity designated in the orders process (complete hospital center), which will require information and communication platforms that are employed in dynamic contiguous and noncontiguous AOs. Specifically, battle command and health information systems enable effective planning, preparing, decision-making, and execution of mission objectives. 

##### **SECTION I – OVERVIEW** 

G-1. The HHD hospital center S-6 has a signal officer (25A00) in the grade of O-3 serves as the officer in charge and a signal support specialist (military occupational specialties 25U3O) who serves as the NCO in charge for the S-6 section. The S-6 is responsible for all aspects of automation and communications electronics for the Role 3 hospital. These personnel determine signal requirements, capabilities, and operations to meet the hospital commander’s intent. 

G-2. The field hospital (32-bed) has a health services system management officer (70D67) in the grade of O-3, an information technician team chief (25B3O), a section chief (25U3O), four addition information technician specialists (25B1O) and a signal maintenance specialist and signal tactical network specialist (both 25U1O). The health information systems officer and information services technicians are employed within the operations section. 

G-3. The hospital center S-6 and field hospital (32-bed) signal and health information system officer work closely together to provide signal support and health information system capabilities to the entire Role 3 hospital center. These capabilities include advice and consultation on signal and health information systems used throughout the hospital and subordinate elements. These sections ensure internal and external communication connectivity within the hospital and subordinate elements. 

G-4. When the hospital center is task organized with two field hospitals (32-bed), the signal and health information system personnel will combine to form a more robust signal and health information system section to support the expanded hospital center. 

**_Note._** For planning considerations, signal support requirements are communicated via Annex H of an operations order (see FM 6-0 for template). It is imperative that the Annex H is completed correctly to ensure that the supporting signal unit can provide adequate signal support. Failure to do this could result in degraded communications and have severe impact on information technology systems. 

G-5. Given a dynamic theater environment and vast array of battle command and health information systems, it is key for all information management and information technology personnel be fully trained and engaged in their prescribed duties to ensure mission success. Below are some of the assigned communication and information systems the S-6, health information systems officer and S-3 should be familiar with. This list is not all-encompassing. As virtual health and other new technologies and advanced medical devices are developed and mature, this list will grow. 

##### **SECTION II – TACTICAL COMMUNICATIONS SYSTEMS** 

G-6. Joint Battle Command Platform, commonly referred to as Blue Force Tracker, uses L-Band satellite link for situational understanding in near real time, preformatted message templates for 9-line MEDEVAC requests, medical resupply, and logistics support. This is a common platform at brigade and below. 

G-7. Single Channel Ground Airborne Radio System is a frequency modulated tactical, encrypted voice, vehicle-mounted or stand-alone radio system. It is used for C2, administration, logistics, intelligence, operations, and other networks. 

G-8. The tactical satellite single channel radio provides beyond line-of-sight encrypted voice and limited data transmission to assigned users on a global scale. 

G-9. The 150-watt high-frequency radio system is also a beyond line-of-sight encrypted voice and available email application for assigned users on a global scale. A time-tested system dating back to WWII, high frequency utilizes the earth’s atmosphere to radiate its transmission signal anywhere in the world. High frequency does require a trained operator familiar with its unique capabilities. 

##### **SECTION III – INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY SYSTEMS** 

G-10. The OMIS-A system is designed to digitally capture patient encounter treatment data using the Armed Forces Health Longitudinal Technology Application-Theater and Theater Composite Healthcare System Cache in deployed environments, enhance continuity of care, and enable each Soldier to have a comprehensive electronic health record (the Role 3 hospital should use AR 40-66 and AR 40-400 as applicable). It also enables the management of Class VIII using Defense Medical Logistics Standard Support and Defense Customer Assistance Module. For the full listing of the TMIP-J suite of applications, see table G-1 on page 219. 

G-11. Command Post of the Future enables commanders to visualize the battlefield and plan the mission through a dynamic view of critical resources and events. 

G-12. Transportation Coordinators’ Automated Information for Movement Systems II is designed to enable users to manage all aspects of transportation operations. It provides automated support to functions performed by a wide range of users from unit movement officers to installation transportation officers, to mode managers responsible for transportation and distribution in support of the full continuum of operations. 

G-13. The Combat Service Support Automated Information Systems Interface allows current and emerging battlefield combat service support automation devices to electronically exchange information via tactical networks. This system also interfaces with other operational and institutional force automated systems and provides unit commanders and logistics managers with an interface device to support combat service support doctrine across the range of military operations. This capability supports noncontiguous concentrations of users and the transfer of real-time information in both fixed and mobile operating environments. 

G-14. Very Small Aperture Terminal provides a worldwide satellite communications network, engineering services, integrated logistics support, infrastructure, and portable remote terminal units in support of logistics information systems operating from garrison or deployed within the OE. 

##### **SECTION IV – JOINT OPERATIONAL MEDICINE INFORAMTION SYSTEMS** 

G-15. The Joint Operational Medical Information Systems (JOMIS) provides interoperable medical information technology capabilities to the warfighter across the range of military operations. This system allows decision-makers, medical logisticians, planners, and healthcare providers with joint operational medicine health information software solutions. The JOMIS connects data across the continuum of care. The JOMIS spans domestic and international conditions and helps caregivers provide the best care possible wherever and whenever it is needed JOMIS provides senior leaders with the information necessary to make tactical decisions based on available medical information. 

G-16. All software on JOMIS was developed according to the joint technical architecture and is common operating environment compliant as mandated by the Joint Requirements Oversight Council. JOMIS is comprised of TMIP-J software and Army-specific applications interfacing with the Army infrastructure (hardware and communications). The JOMIS Program Management Office is responsible for integrating the joint medical software applications on Army baseline systems, new equipment fielding, new equipment training, pre- and during deployment support, and technical and maintenance of the JOMIS hardware and systems.

#### **JOINT TRAUMA SYSTEM** 

G-19. The Joint Trauma System is an organized approach to providing the improved trauma care across the continuum of care to trauma patients, battle and nonbattle injuries. The Joint Trauma System is dedicated to the reduction of morbidity and mortality of combat patients and is engaged in a systematic fashion to determine the acute and long-term outcomes of patients, the quality of their care, improvements in prevention and treatment, and logistical considerations. The Joint Trauma System addresses all components identified with optimal care of a patient from prevention through acute care, rehabilitation, and RTD. For additional information, refer to the United States Army Institute of Surgical Research, Joint Trauma System website. 

G-20. The Joint Theater Trauma Registry is part of the TMIP-J and supports the capture of theater trauma care information across the continuum of trauma care, from the deployed AO to garrison-based Role 4 hospitals. Trauma information collected and reported by the Joint Trauma Registry includes trauma care and outcomes for military and civilian patients. This information combined with Armed Forces Medical Examiner data in a central repository is provided to the DOD for use in the requirements development process to enhance medical care in future operations.

Log into the Army Training Network (known as ATN) to download collective and individual tasks. These tasks have been developed and approved by the Army Training Development Capability (known as TDC) and hosted on the Central Army Registry (known as CAR) to develop unit training plans.

##### **Appendix H** 

### **Hospitalization Role in Telemedicine** 

Appendix H provides a brief description of telemedicine in the Role 3 hospital and those techniques that may increase the survivability of Soldiers. It covers the aspects of telemedicine (virtual health) best practices, planning, security, and documentation. The Role 3 hospital should establish detailed telemedicine procedures in its unit TACSOP. 

#### **TELEMEDICINE** 

H-1. Telemedicine, also referred to as virtual health, encompasses a set of tools that leverages information and communication technologies to extend medical care across geographic distances and boundaries. Telemedicine can be useful in stability and DSCA OEs. During large-scale combat operations, our peer threats equate an electromagnetic signature larger than a platoon a lucrative target. Telemedicine may be used but extreme caution should be exercised as use of telemedicine results in electromagnetic signatures that may provide a signal that can be triangulated by the enemy, allowing the exact location of the signal to be pinpointed. 

H-2. Operational virtual health provides the resource of clinical expertise to the point of need. When clinicians managing patients reach the limits of their knowledge, skills, or abilities, two options exist for ensuring a patient receives optimal care: evacuate or call for help. Increasingly, calling for help has been identified as best practice because calling ensures the resources match the requirement without placing additional resources at risk. More importantly, telemedicine has improved the care provided to the patient. Telemedicine provided during high-fidelity medical simulation of prolonged care has been demonstrated to reduce mortality and improve quality of care provided by less-experienced medical providers. 

H-3. Telemedicine-conversations between two caregivers can be accomplished through telementoring, teleconsultation, or a combination of the two. The conversations simultaneously enhance the knowledge, skills, and abilities of the local caregiver through education during the mentoring or consultation as well as that of the remote expert (advisor) through increased understanding of austere and operational contexts. 

##### **TELEMENTORING** 

H-4. Telementoring is a process by which a medical professional with greater knowledge and experience guides another medical professional with less knowledge who is treating a patient at a remote location. Telementoring assists the combat medic in providing lifesaving measures. The primary situation is a mentor (medical provider) at a Role 3 hospital guiding another medical provider through a procedure or treatment of a wounded Soldier. This guidance enables the other medical provider to make an accurate assessment of the patient’s condition and treat the patient according to the guidance provided by the mentor. Telementoring offers a viable adjunct to health care to increase the capability of a caregiver and thus increase capacity to manage patients in a deployed setting. Telementoring is not intended for use on every patient; it is used on an "as needed" basis and is METT-TC (I) dependent. 

H-5. Every deployed health care professional making use of telementoring is responsible for evaluating the appropriateness of applying it in the settings of any clinical situation. When telementoring modalities are used appropriately, telementoring can increase the operational reach of the deployed healthcare professionals to support the warfighter. Telementoring may be accomplished by— 

- Minimum: Voice telephone call with next higher role of healthcare provider reading from DD Form 1380 and a locally produced teleconsultation script. 

- Better: Voice connection with concurrent email or photo exchange (for example, send a photo of the call script, vital signs flowchart, and a locally produced teleconsultation report, such as a prolonged care flowsheet, locally produced teleconsultation report, such as a prolonged care flowsheet the patient care environment, and available kit), closely followed by a phone call. 

- Best: Real-time audio or video feeds, with or without remote diagnostic equipment, using the teleconsultation script as a guide. And for emergent, use the joint trauma system recommend virtual critical care consultation guide and send a picture or patient capabilities as well as vital sign trends to the consultation via email or message using appropriate operational security and protections of patient privacy. 

##### **TELECONSULTATION** 

H-6. The types of teleconsultations consist of emergent, urgent, and routine request. There is a spectrum of technology (phone, email, video teleconference, or remote health care monitoring) available for health care professionals to deliver telemedicine capabilities for the warfighter and provide emergent, urgent, and routine consultations. For most routine cases, asynchronous consultation is often sufficient. For urgent and emergent cases, voice communications, plus or minus images sent via electronic means, is recommended. Healthcare providers should review clinical practice guidelines for more detailed information on emergent, urgent, and routine teleconsultation indicators. 

###### **Emergent** 

H-7. Healthcare providers should recognize when to use teleconsultation. If available, continuous monitoring for an extended consultation (for example, tele-critical care) should be employed, METT-TC (I) dependent. Emergent consults are for patients with critical wounds, illness, or injury. Indications to consider critical care consultation may include— 

- Vital signs are very abnormal for more than 30 minutes. 

- Unfamiliarity with the medical issues. 

- Respiratory failure requiring advanced support (for example, intubation or mechanical ventilation). 

- Hemodynamic instability, evidence of shock, or shock. 

- Complex wounds, severe infection, sepsis, or septic shock. 

- Severe electrolyte abnormalities. 

- Encephalopathy or severe traumatic brain injury with concern for elevated intracranial pressure. 

- Poisonings or toxicology emergencies. 

- Any questions regarding a patient with a life-threatening or potentially life-threatening condition. 

- Unique patient populations (for example, obstetrics or pediatrics). 

- Chemical patient. 

###### **Urgent** 

H-8. Urgent consults are all other cases that do not fall under the routine or emergent categories. Urgent consults usually require specialty medical advice and would benefit from communication between the healthcare provider and remote consultant. Specialties may include— 

- General and orthopedic surgery. 

- Emergency medicine. 

- Burns. 

- Pediatrics. 

- Infectious disease. 

- Obstetrics or gynecology. 

- Dental. 

###### **Routine** 

H-9. Routine consults require a consultant response within 24-hours. Routine patients have normal vital signs and are not at risk for deterioration within the next 24-hours. The healthcare provider and patient have time to wait for a response. Some mission requirements demand more immediate medical decisions due to shorter time allotments for communications or operational context. Limitations due to time should encourage local caregivers to use urgent consultation systems. 

**Hospitalization Role in Telemedicine** 

H-10. To conduct routine consults, use the Global Teleconsultation Platform. The platform requires a username and password, which you must request through its website. By taking this step, you will establish the necessary credentials to access the platform and complete your routine consults efficiently. 

##### **SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR BEHAVIORAL HEALTH PATIENTS DURING TELECONSULTATION** 

H-11. Role 3 behavioral health personnel are responsible for triaging and screening patients to ensure they are appropriate for telehealth. Exclusionary criteria include, but are not limited to— 

- Refusal to participate in virtual behavioral health. 

- Acutely violent, unstable, impulsive, or agitated behavior. 

- Need for special monitoring not available at the site. 

- Symptoms that could be exacerbated by video teleconference (for example, ideas of reference, psychosis, or thought insertion). 

- Information that should be shared in person. 

- Hearing, visual, or cognitive deficits that limit the patient’s ability to fully participate. 

- Appropriate uses of virtual behavioral health appointment in the deployed, rotational, or remote setting include, but are not limited to— 

 - Coordination of care for the stable patient. 

 - Continuity of care with rear-detachment or home station behavioral health provider. 

 - Second opinion evaluations from a consultant. 

H-12. The Role 3 may have to contact the combat and operational stress control detachment operating in the AO for teleconsultation support. Role 3 behavioral health personnel should use the minimum, better, best method to ensure a good teleconsultation session. 

#### **OPERATIONAL TELEMEDICINE BEST PRACTICES** 

H-13. Telemedicine, unfortunately, has one major limitation-it requires network resources. If the network is not available, it is not possible to conduct telemedicine. This may occur asynchronously using text, email, or web-portal. All of these require minimal network resources. Synchronous voice using radio or telephone call is sufficient for most consultations. Voice calls plus asynchronous file transfer (for example, images) is an adaptable solution for using telemedicine in very austere locations. Table H-1 provides operational telemedicine best practices. 

**Table H-1. Operational telemedicine best practices** 

|**_Setting capability_**|**_Asynch_**<br>|**_ronous_**<br>|**_Synchronous_**<br>|**_Continuous_**<br>**_with no_**<br>**_inde_**<br>|**_(synchronous_**<br>**_signal loss_**<br>**_finitely)_**<br>|
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
||**_Email, text,_**<br>**_and images_**|**_Voice_**|**_Video_**<br>**_teleconference,_**<br>**_low data_**<br>**_Interruptible_**|**_Remote_**<br>**_monitoring_**<br>**_(vital signs)_**|**_Video_**<br>**_teleconference,_**<br>**_high data_**<br>**_Uninterruptible_**|
|Non-Urgent<br>Provider Consult|X|||||
|Urgent Provider<br>Consult|X|X||||
|Non-Urgent Patient<br>Encounter|X|X|X|||
|Optimal Critical<br>Care Consultation|X|X|X|||
|Remote Monitoring|X|X|X|X||
|Procedural<br>(surgical)<br>Support/Mentoring||||X|X| H-14. One of the greatest drawbacks to using teleconsultation during care in the AO is that it takes time. Telemedicine is inherently inefficient as a care medium because the remote consultant should use words, and sometimes images, to understand what is happening locally and then should effectively communicate the recommendations. The following tips may help improve this inefficiency:

- To improved telemedicine efficiency, a healthcare professional should use a script when presenting a patient to the remote consultant. Ideally, this script is the same one used during simulation-based training and the remote expert has a copy of it. 

- Whenever possible, the local caregiver should send a copy of the script, documentation flow sheets of patient vital signs and treatments, and other background information to the remote consultant before engaging in the telemedicine encounter. Valuable background information includes— 

 - A list or picture of the local medical supplies and equipment, including monitoring capabilities. 

 - A list of available medications. 

 - A list of fluids and blood products. 

 - Diagnostic tests available (for example, labs or imaging). 

 - Pictures of the patient and the care environment. 

H-15. These items help provide context and prevent the consultant from giving recommendations that are not possible for the local caregiver to carry out. The best practices for telemedicine use in the AO are explained in table H-2. 

###### **Table H-2. Best practices for telemedicine support in an area of operations** 

First, the unit needs to ensure that the operational environment allows the use of telemedicine due to mission, enemy, terrain and weather, troops and support available, time available, civil considerations, and <u>(informational considerations) implications.</u> 

Telemedicine is a skill that should be practiced by both local caregivers and remote experts for interactions to be efficient and effective. Ideally, the care team should train together using simulated patient care to optimize communication and ensure the technology choices match the clinical needs. 

Prior to travelling to the area of operation, care teams should develop a communication plan for telemedical support (Primary, Alternate, Contingency, and Emergency). This includes when and how (technology) to use telemedicine. Local caregivers should recognize when to “make the call.” Conceptually, the “sicker” a patient is, the earlier and more frequently the local caregiver should engage with the remote expert. If available, continuous monitoring for an extended consultation should be employed. Effective and efficient teleconsultation takes practice but can be improved by— 

- Using a script familiar to the both the local caregiver and remote consultant. 

- • Sending background information using asynchronous technology ahead of synchronous telemedicine encounters. 

- Using closed loop communication that includes intentional pauses in communication for read-back and clarification. 

- Having the remote expert document, the telemedicine encounter and send that documentation with <u>a summary of recommendations to the local caregiver.</u> 

- Solve technical challenges using a well-developed and practiced communication plan. 

H-16. Health care professionals should send images of patients and care context to remote experts before starting a telemedicine encounter. This helps the remote expert provide "consultation in context" and avoids recommendations that are discordant with local capabilities. Photographs that are less than 500MB are often of insufficient quality to convey information to a remote consultant. This makes use of telemedicine during large-scale combat operations is difficult as more time is needed to transfer large amounts of data meaning you remain a target the entire time data is being transferred which impacts survivability. Per DODI 6025.18 _,_ images should be compliant with the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act whenever possible. Personally identifiable information, for example, like full-face images and unique tattoos, unless they are essential for communicating injury or disease state, should not be included in the photo to the remote expert. Images of the environment that could cause a safety issue **Hospitalization Role in Telemedicine**

for the local care team by exposing its location, affiliation, or other information that could be used against it (for example, media implications of the image) should not be included. 

**_Note._** Digital photos may also contain "geotags" that can provide the exact location where it was taken. This feature needs to be disabled on the electronic device taking the photo. 

#### **COMMUNICATION PLANNING FOR TELEMEDICINE** 

H-17. The safety and quality of care is the responsibility of the local caregiver and, therefore, telemedical support in the AO requires preparation and a communication plan to be successful. If a local caregiver intends to have consultation available but is unable to reach a remote consultant through one communication method, alternate methods should already have been established and tested. Thus, PACE planning takes two forms, one about when to utilize telemedicine and one for how to use telemedicine. An example of a PACE communication plan is shown in table H-3. 

**Table H-3. Notional communication plan for telemedicine** 

||**_Patient careplan_**|**_Telemedicine technology_**|
|---|---|---|
|**_Primary_**|Use local assets; evacuate the patient;<br>pre-position resources (for example,<br>expect to need a surgeon, have an<br>operating room and a surgeon locally<br>available).|**Power:**Electrical grid<br>**Network:**Fiber or cable internet<br>**Equipment:**Dedicated telemedicine<br>equipment (for example, video<br>teleconference, exam station, room<br>camera, augmented reality device, and<br>peripheral devices)|
|**_Alternate_**|Use established telemedicine relationship<br>to support care-in-place or during<br>evacuation.|**Power:**Solar cell battery<br>**Network:**4G or long term evolution (LTE)<br>cellular network<br>**Equipment**: Mobile phone or tablet with<br>or withoutperipheral exam equipment|
|**_Contingency_**|Use ad hoc telemedicine relationships (for<br>example, “phone-a-friend”) to support<br>care-in-place or during evacuation.|**Power:**Gasoline generator<br>**Network:**Satellite<br>**Equipment:**Laptop or desktop computer,<br>digital camera,and email|
|**_Emergency_**|Use un-approved, non-standard local<br>solutions (for example, local economy,<br>high-risk care by untrained individual) to<br>achieve “best-possible” outcome.|**Power:**Batteries<br>**Network:**Telephone, radio<br>**Equipment:**Phone and radio|

##### **DANGERExtreme caution must be exercised when using the electronic<mark>devices described in Table H-3 as they all produce an electromagnetic signature that may impact survivability on the battlefield of the individuals using the devices and those around</mark> them.** 

H-18. Like the need for planning, optimal teleconsultation requires practice for its efficient and effective execution. Telemedicine is a skill set for both local caregivers and remote experts. Before traveling to the AO, caregivers should engage their telemedicine team using the technologies in their PACE plan. Practicing caring for patients with the remote expert team during high-quality training, ideally using highfidelity manikin, simulation-based training, builds trust between the caregiver and the remote expert and builds strong habits, such as using a script to convey information. 

H-19. Telemedicine is a skill set and a tool that requires training, practice, and a communication plan. A team that has used telemedicine in training is able to adapt to conditions on the ground and optimize care for the patient. 

H-20. The telemedicine technology PACE plan supports the alternate and contingency patient care plans. The power, network, and equipment items should be considered independently of plans and mixed-andmatched according to need and functioning. Thus, the primary power plan could support the alternate network and contingency equipment. The technology plan should build from most reliable to least reliable. 

#### **SECURITY DURING TELECONSULTATION** 

H-21. Traditional teleconsultation is unclassified, and the local caregiver should maintain normal rules of operational security when utilizing unclassified networks. Consultants in standard medical systems typically do not have means of secure communications. Teleconsultation should not be delayed due to an unsecure connection unless operational situations dictate otherwise. Encryption may enhance security or protect patient information but is not a requirement at this time. 

H-22. Maintaining patient privacy should be a priority, and many simple or available tools for communication meet patient privacy requirements. When sending patient information or images by open communication methods, patient identification should be limited to sex and age. Location can be generic addressing temperature (hot, warm, or cold), surroundings (urban or rural), or environments (desert or tropical). Location to the level of continent or region can be useful for the consultant or expert to better identify diseases specific to certain areas (hemorrhagic fevers or malaria). Photographs should not include the face or identifiable scars or tattoos unless unavoidable due to location of injury. 

#### **DOCUMENTATION** 

H-23. Documentation of care in an OE is ultimately the responsibility of local caregivers. All theater and local command documentation requirements should be completed locally. It is useful, however, for the remote expert to document thoughts and consultation when possible. All efforts should be made by the remote expert to send recommendations to the local caregiver. These recommendations should be copied into or from the operational virtual health report. If the patient is a DOD beneficiary and the local caregiver can give at least two of the patient identifiers, the consultant should document the encounter in the electronic medical record by copying the information from the operational virtual health report to the medical record note. At least two of the following patient identifiers are needed to document care in the electronic medical record: 

- Patient name. 

- DOD ID number and battle roster number. 

- Department of Defense identification number. 

- Date of birth. 

- Pseudo identification number. 

**_Note:_** The social security number is no longer used for identification purposes within DOD. Role 3 hospitals will use the Soldiers DOD identification number or Battle Roster number from the casualty feeder card which contains the last four of the soldier's social security number. For additional information refer to DODI 1000.30 _._ 

H-24. Many teleconsultation encounters for patients will likely be utilizing the remote expert platform especially for those- 

- Whom are not DOD beneficiaries (for example, local nationals or other government agencies) but for whom U.S. military caregivers are responsible. 

- Whom the local or austere caregiver cannot give the patient’s name or identifying information.

##### **SECTION II – TERMS** 

###### **agility** 

The ability to move forces and adjust their dispositions and activities more rapidly than the enemy. (FM 3-0) 

###### **area support** 

A task assigned to a sustainment unit directing it to support units in or passing through a specified location. (ATP 4-90) 

###### **Army Health System** 

The Army Health System is a component of the Military Health System that is responsible for operational management of the health service support and force health protection missions for training, predeployment, deployment, and postdeployment operations. The Army Health System (AHS) includes all mission support services performed, provided, or arranged by the Army Medicine to support health service support and force health protection mission requirements for the Army and as directed, for joint, intergovernmental agencies, Army Forces, and multinational forces (FM 4-02) 

###### **casualty** 

Any person who is lost to the organization by having been declared dead, duty status – whereabouts unknown, excused absence-whereabouts unknown, missing, ill, or injured. (JP 4-02) 

###### **continuity of care** 

Attempt to maintain the role of care during movement at least equal to the care provided at the preceding facility. (FM 4-02) 

###### **convergence** 

The outcome created by the concerted employment of capabilities from multiple domains and echelons against combinations of decisive points in any domain to create effects against a system, formation, decision maker, or in a specific geographic area. (FM 3-0) 

###### **crisis** 

An emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve national and/or strategic objectives. (JP 3-0) 

###### ***damage control resuscitation** 

The strategy to limit blood loss and prevent coagulopathy by combining hypotensive resuscitation, early airway control, and early and balanced use of blood products and other hemostatic agents. Also called DCR. 

###### ***damage control surgery** 

The surgical intervention (rapid initial control of bleeding and contamination with packing and temporary closure) to keep the patient alive, targeted at prevention of the triad of death (hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy), rather than correction of anatomy. Also called DCS. 

###### **depth** 

The extension of operations in time, space, or purpose to achieve definitive results. (ADP 3-0) 

###### **died of wounds received in action** 

A casualty category applicable to a hostile casualty, other than the victim of a terrorist activity, who dies of wounds or other injuries received in action after having reached damage control surgical capability. (DODI 1300.18) 

###### **endurance** 

The ability to persevere over time throughout the depth of an operational environment. (FM 3-0) 

###### **essential care** 

(Joint) Medical treatment provided to manage the casualty throughout the roles of care. (JP 4-02) 

(Army) The absolutely necessary initial, en route, resuscitative, and surgical care provided to save, stabilize, and return as many Soldiers to duty as quickly as possible. (FM 4-02) 

###### ***forward resuscitative surgery** 

Urgent initial surgery required to render a patient transportable for further evacuation to an inpatient medical treatment facility staffed and equipped to provide for the patient’s care. 

###### **fragmentary order** 

A fragmentary order is an abbreviated operation order issued as needed to change or modify an order or to execute a branch or sequel. Also called FRAGORD (JP 5-0) 

###### ***hospital** 

A medical treatment facility capable of providing inpatient care. 

###### ***inpatient** 

A person admitted to and treated within a Role 3 hospital and who cannot be returned to duty within the same calendar day. 

###### **mass casualty** 

(Joint) Any number of human casualties produced across a period of time that exceeds available medical support capabilities. (JP 4-02) 

(Army) Is any large number of casualties produced in a relatively short period of time, usually as the result of a single incident such as a military aircraft accident, hurricane, flood, earthquake, or armed attack that exceeds local logistics support capabilities. (FM 4-02) 

###### **medical intelligence** 

That category of intelligence resulting from collection, evaluation, analysis, and interpretation of foreign medical, bio-scientific, and environmental information that is of interest to strategic planning and to military medical planning and operations for the conservation of the fighting strength of friendly forces and the formation of assessments of foreign medical capabilities in both military and civilian sectors. See also intelligence. (JP 2-0) 

###### **medical regulating** 

Medical regulating is the coordination for the movement of patients to match patients with an appropriate medical treatment facility. See also health service support; medical treatment facility. (JP 4-02) 

###### **medical treatment facility** 

(Joint) A facility established for the purpose of furnishing medical and/or dental care to eligible individuals. (JP 4-02) 

(Army) Any facility established for the purpose of providing medical treatment. This includes battalion aid stations, Role 2 facilities, dispensaries, clinics, and hospitals. (FM 4-02) 

###### **multidomain operations** 

The combined arms employment of joint and Army capabilities to create and exploit relative advantages to achieve objectives, defeat enemy forces, and consolidate gains on behalf of joint force commanders. (FM 3-0) 

###### ***nontransportable patient** 

An individual whose medical condition is so severe that they require immediate stabilization or surgical intervention to prevent further deterioration, and cannot be safely evacuated to a higher level of care without risking significant harm or compromise to their health. 

###### **operation order** 

An operation order is a directive issued by a commander to subordinate commanders for the purpose of effecting the coordinated execution of an operation. Also called OPORD. (JP 5-0) 

###### **operational reach** 

The distance and duration across which a force can successfully employ military capabilities. (JP 3-0) 

###### ***outpatient** 

A person receiving medical/dental examination and/or treatment from medical personnel and in a status other than being admitted to a hospital. 

###### ***patient** 

(Joint) A sick, injured, wounded, or other person receiving medical care, dental care, or other medical treatment. (JP 4-02) 

(Army) A wounded, ill, and injured Soldier who receives medical care or treatment from medically trained personnel. 

###### **patient movement** 

The act or process of moving a sick, injured, wounded, or other person to obtain medical and/or dental care or treatment, which includes medical regulating, patient evacuation, and en route medical care. (JP 4-02) 

###### **point of injury** 

The location a wounding, illness, or injury was sustained. (FM 4-02) 

###### **preventive medicine** 

(Joint) The anticipation, communication, prediction, identification, prevention, education, risk assessment, and control of communicable diseases; illnesses; and exposure to endemic, occupational, and environmental threats. Also called PVNTMED. (JP 4-02) 

###### **prolonged care** 

Patient care for extended periods of time when evacuation or mission requirements surpass available capabilities or capacity to evacuate the patient. (JP 4-02) 

###### **reconstitution** 

Reconstitution consists of those actions that commanders plan and implement to restore units to a desired level of combat effectiveness commensurate with mission requirements and available resources. (ATP 3-21.20) 

###### **return to duty** 

A patient disposition which, after medical evaluation and treatment when necessary, returns a Soldier for duty in their unit. (FM 4-02) 

###### **roles of medical care** 

The characterization of health support for the distribution of medical resources and capabilities. (JP 4- 02) 

###### **theater evacuation policy** 

A command decision indicating the length in days of the maximum period of noneffectiveness that patients may be held within the command for treatment, and the medical determination of patients that cannot return to duty status within the period prescribed requiring evacuation by the first available means, provided the travel involved will not aggravate their disabilities or medical condition. (ATP 4-02.2)
